16 
II. 
Purpose of Testimony 
17 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the Panel’s testimony? 
18 
A. 
Niagara Mohawk remains committed to being at the heart of a clean, fair, 
19 
and affordable energy future by reducing emissions, empowering 
20 
customers to take control of their energy use, and accelerating the 
21 


--- Page 6 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 4 of 31 
 
transition to a modern decarbonized electric grid.  New York State 
1 
likewise reaffirmed its commitment to advancing the transition to a clean-
2 
energy economy with passage of the Climate Leadership and Community 
3 
Protection Act of 2019 (“CLCPA”), codifying the most ambitious 
4 
greenhouse gas emission and renewable energy targets in the nation.  The 
5 
purpose of this testimony is to explain why the Company believes AMI is 
6 
a foundational investment required to deliver this shared vision of the 
7 
clean-energy future.     
8 
 
9 
Specifically, the Panel discusses: (i) the refinement of its AMI proposal 
10 
through the collaborative process established as part of the Joint Proposal 
11 
in Cases 17-E-0238 and 17-G-0239 (the “2017 Niagara Mohawk Rate 
12 
Cases”); (ii) the revenue requirement needed to deliver the AMI project; 
13 
(iii) proposed testing protocols to support full-scale meter and gas module 
14 
deployment; (iv) recovery of undepreciated automated meter reading 
15 
(“AMR”) assets and gas encoder receiver transmitters (“ERTs”); (v) 
16 
potential opportunities for multi-jurisdictional cost synergies; and (vi) the 
17 
status of the Company’s Clifton Park REV Demonstration Project.  The 
18 
Panel provided information concerning the projected AMI costs to the 
19 
Revenue Requirements and Information Technology Panels for use in 
20 
developing Niagara Mohawk’s proposed revenue requirements. 
21

7 
III. 
Background 
8 
Q.  
Please describe the Company’s AMI proposal. 
9 
A. 
Pursuant to the Joint Proposal in the 2017 Niagara Mohawk Rate Cases, 
10 
the Company convened a collaborative with Department of Public 
11 
Service Staff (“Staff”) and 16 interested stakeholders to refine and update 
12 
the AMI business case (the “AMI Collaborative”).  The collaborative 
13 
process, which consisted of eight large and small group meetings, as well 
14 
as separate deep-dive discussions on areas of interest with stakeholders, 
15 
culminated in filing of the AMI Report and the Supplemental AMI 
16 
Report.  The Company proposed a phased six-year full-scale AMI 
17 
deployment (two years of back-office systems and four years of 
18 
meter/gas module deployment).  Altogether, the Company will replace 
19 
approximately 1.7 million AMR electric meters and 640,000 gas modules 
20 
across its service territory.   
21 


--- Page 9 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 7 of 31 
 
Q. 
Is the Company’s proposal consistent with peer utilities? 
1 
A. 
Yes.  From a national perspective, the Federal Energy Commission’s 
2 
(“FERC”) most recent “Assessment of Demand Response and Advanced 
3 
Metering” (December 2019) found that advanced meters are now the 
4 
most prevalent type of meter deployed in the country, accounting for 
5 
more than half of all installed and operational meters.  Likewise, the 
6 
Edison Foundation’s Institute for Electric Innovation reported that the 
7 
number of installed AMI meters in the United States exceeded 88 million 
8 
in 2018, which accounts for nearly 70 percent of households.  The Edison 
9 
Foundation further projects that AMI meter installations approached 98 
10 
million in 2019 and will reach 107 million by the end of calendar year 
11 
2020.   
12 
 
13 
Q.  
Does this hold true relative to peer utilities in New York? 
14 
A. 
Yes.  Specific to New York, Consolidated Edison Company of New 
15 
York, Inc. (“ConEd”) received approval in March 2016 to deploy AMI 
16 
throughout its service territory.  According to its most recent AMI 
17 
Metrics Report, as of April 30, 2020, ConEd installed nearly 2.7 million 
18 
AMI meters enabling functionalities related to billing, near real-time data 
19 
delivered to customers on ConEd’s customer portal, and remote 
20 
connection and disconnect capability for residential customers, among 
21 


--- Page 10 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 8 of 31 
 
other benefits.  Similarly, Orange and Rockland Utilities, Inc. (“O&R”) 
1 
began deploying AMI electric meters and gas modules in July 2017.  As 
2 
of its March 31, 2020 AMI Metrics Report, O&R has deployed nearly 
3 
332,000 meters.  On June 21, 2020, New York State Electric and Gas 
4 
Corporation (“NYSEG”) and Rochester Gas and Electric (“RG&E”) filed 
5 
a joint proposal, which would enable AMI deployment to approximately 
6 
1.3 million electric meters and 600,000 gas modules.  Under the NYSEG 
7 
and RG&E proposal, back-office work would begin in the third quarter 
8 
of 2020, with three years of meter/module installation beginning in the 
9 
second quarter of 2022.  If the Commission approves the NYSEG and 
10 
RG&E joint proposal, four out of the six major investor-owned utilities 
11 
in New York State will have approval to install AMI throughout their 
12 
service territories. 
13 
 
14 
Q. 
What is the status of the Company’s AMI proposal? 
15 
A. 
The Company’s AMI proposal remains pending with the Commission.      
16 
 
17 
IV. 
AMI Capital and Operations and Maintenance Expense 
18 
 
A. 
Updated Assumptions 
19 
Q. 
Is the Company proposing any changes to the AMI proposal? 
20 


--- Page 11 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 9 of 31 
 
A. 
The Company is not proposing changes to the underlying proposal 
1 
reflected in the AMI Report and the Supplemental AMI Report.  
2 
However, for purposes of developing the AMI portion of the revenue 
3 
requirements, the Company adjusted two BCA assumptions.  First, the 
4 
Company incorporated a six-month managed ramp-up period following 
5 
Commission approval to allow for resource onboarding, contract 
6 
execution, and project sanctioning.  The revenue requirement 
7 
calculations assume the managed ramp up begins with Commission 
8 
approval in October 2020, leading to a project start date in April 2021 
9 
(FY 2022).  Second, because the initial AMI filing assumed an April 
10 
2019 start date, the Company adjusted the BCA model to reflect a 2.00 
11 
percent inflation adjustment for labor and a 2.10 percent inflation 
12 
adjustment for non-labor.      
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Is the Company proposing any additional modifications to the AMI 
15 
proposal? 
16 
A. 
Yes.  The Company designed the proposed deployment of AMI and 
17 
AMI-compatible gas modules to closely align with the planned 
18 
replacement cycle for the current fleet of AMR meters and gas modules, 
19 
which are nearing the end of the manufacturer’s projected 20-year useful 
20 
life.  As described in the business case accompanying the AMI Report 
21 


--- Page 12 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 10 of 31 
 
(Exhibit A, page 7), the Company estimates that by FY 2025, 
1 
approximately 96 percent of electric meters and 80 percent of gas 
2 
modules will have reached the 20-year period.  However, given the 
3 
current projected start date and the proposed deployment schedule (see 
4 
AMI Report, page 15), only half of the meters will be replaced over this 
5 
same period.  To mitigate the risk of large-scale meter / module failures 
6 
and to deliver benefits more quickly to customers, the Company proposes 
7 
to accelerate the AMI deployment schedule by shifting 10 percent of the 
8 
deployment forward from the final year.  With the adjustment, the new 
9 
deployment schedule would be as follows: 
10 
 Accelerated AMI Meter / Module Deployment Schedule 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Do the proposed adjustments affect the BCA ratios included in the 
13 
Supplemental AMI Report? 
14 
A. 
The impact to the BCA ratio is minimal.  With the adjustments, the BCA 
15 
dropped from 1.36 to 1.35 for the opt-out time-varying pricing (“TVP”) 
16 
rate scenario and remained the same at 1.23 for the opt-in TVP rate 
17 
scenario. 
18 
 
19 
Q. 
Did the Company consider other adjustments to the proposal? 
20 
FY24 
FY25 
FY26 
FY27 
20% of customers 35% of customers 35% of customers 10% of customers 


--- Page 13 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 11 of 31 
 
A. 
Yes, as mentioned, the existing fleet of metering assets are approaching 
1 
the end of their manufacturer’s estimated useful life and, for the gas 
2 
ERTs, anticipated battery failures.  The continued operation of these 
3 
assets is critical to the Company’s ability to provide safe and reliable 
4 
service to customers.   
5 
 
6 
To further mitigate the risk of large-scale AMR meter and ERT failures, 
7 
the Company considered including additional replacement costs in the 
8 
rate case for FY 2024 and FY 2025.  Although the Company is not 
9 
proposing to include the additional costs at this time, the Company 
10 
expects that any delays beyond a FY 2022 project start date will present 
11 
sufficient risk of meter/ERT failure that proactive measures will be 
12 
required to ensure the Company can satisfy its service obligations to 
13 
customers.  Therefore, if the AMR meters and ERTs begin to fail at 
14 
significant rates in advance of AMI deployment or the project start date 
15 
slips past April 2021, the Company will require additional funds to 
16 
address the meter/ERT failure risks.  Such an approach, if required, will 
17 
also affect the Company’s unrecovered AMR meter and gas ERT 
18 
forecasts, as set forth in Exhibit ___ (AMIP-6).   
19 
 
20 


--- Page 14 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 12 of 31 
 
Q. 
In addition to the meter and ERT replacement costs, did the 
1 
Company consider any other adjustments? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  The Company has continued to evaluate options for reducing the 
3 
time it takes to deliver raw energy usage data to customers, often referred 
4 
to as data latency.  In the AMI Report, the Company proposed to read 
5 
meters every 15-minutes and present the data to customers every four 
6 
hours on the web-based Customer Energy Management Platform 
7 
(“CEMP”).  Based on feedback from the AMI Collaborative, the 
8 
Company further committed to evaluating the benefits and costs of 
9 
reducing data latency, presenting raw energy usage data every 30 
10 
minutes instead of every four hours.  Through the request for proposal 
11 
(“RFP”) process, the Company identified a preferred metering solution 
12 
that will enable customers to access real-time raw energy usage data 
13 
using a Wi-Fi enabled home-area network (“HAN”).  In addition, the 
14 
Company identified approximately $9.07 million in incremental costs to 
15 
reduce the latency of when raw energy usage data will be presented to 
16 
customers on the CEMP from four hours to every 30 to 45 minutes.  The 
17 
incremental costs are not included in the revenue requirement, but could 
18 
be added as part of the Corrections and Updates filing if the Commission 
19 
requires reduced AMI data latency.   
20 
 
21 


--- Page 15 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 13 of 31 
 
Q. 
Has the Company identified impacts on the AMI program from the 
1 
COVID-19 pandemic? 
2 
A. 
Yes, although the actual long-term impact of the pandemic remains 
3 
uncertain, the Company has a few general observations relative to AMI.  
4 
First, the proliferation of work-from-home and homeschooling efforts 
5 
requiring changes in energy usage patterns and using internet-connected 
6 
devices suggests customers would likely realize additional benefits from 
7 
real-time energy usage data delivered over a HAN, as well as the 
8 
personalized energy insights available from the Company and third-
9 
parties.  Second, the remote reconnect and outage management 
10 
capabilities offered by AMI would safeguard customers and Company 
11 
employees from situations where adequate physical distancing may not 
12 
be possible, if such measures are required in the future. 
13 
 
B. 
Updated Project Costs 
14 
Q. 
After incorporating the managed ramp-up, inflation adjustment, and 
15 
accelerated deployment schedule what are the estimated project 
16 
costs? 
17 
A.  
The Company’s proposal, as adjusted for a six-month managed ramp up, 
18 
inflation, and the accelerated deployment includes approximately 
19 
$654.13 million in anticipated costs (20-year net present value (“NPV”)), 
20 
assuming an opt-out TVP rate structure.  Likewise, assuming an opt-in 
21 


--- Page 16 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 14 of 31 
 
TVP rate structure would yield approximately $634.88 million in costs 
1 
(20-year NPV).   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
What are the total expenditures? 
4 
A. 
The expenditures in the Rate Year (the 12 months ending June 30, 2022) 
5 
and Data Years (Data Year 1 is the 12 months ending June 30, 2023, 
6 
Data Year 2 is the 12 months ending June 30, 2024) are based on the 
7 
Company’s proposed opt-out scenario costs of $654.13 million (20-year 
8 
NPV).  Exhibit ___ (AMIP-4) includes a summary of the expenditures 
9 
for the first five years of the project, assuming an April 2021 (FY 2022) 
10 
project start. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Does the Company anticipate incurring any of the project costs prior 
13 
to the Rate Year? 
14 
A. 
Yes.  The Company assumes Commission approval of the AMI project in 
15 
October 2020 with operations and maintenance (“O&M”) expense in the 
16 
months following the anticipated project start date in April 2021 and the 
17 
beginning of the Rate Year.  Consistent with the AMI Report (page 17), 
18 
the Company proposes to defer the associated revenue requirement of the 
19 
AMI-related expenditures incurred prior to the start of the Rate Year and 
20 
allocate such costs as set forth in Exhibit ___ (AMIP-5). 
21

Q. 
Please summarize the cost components. 
1 
A. 
The AMI project consists of four major cost categories: (1) AMI meters, 
2 
gas modules, and installation; (2) communications network equipment 
3 
and installation; (3) platform and ongoing IT operations costs; and (4) 
4 
project management and ongoing business operations costs.   
5 
 
6

7

8

9 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-4), the projected electric capital expenditures, 
10 
including cost of removal, for the AMI project are: $1.353 million in FY 
11 
2022, $7.424 million in FY 2023, $63.775 million in FY 2024, and 
12 
$109.590 million in FY 2025.     
13 
 
14

15

16

18

19

20

C. 
Opportunity for Multi-Jurisdictional Synergies. 
5 
Q. 
What is the status of AMI in National Grid’s Rhode Island and 
6 
Massachusetts jurisdictions? 
7 
A. 
National Grid’s Rhode Island and Massachusetts affiliates continue to 
8 
evaluate advanced metering functionality (“AMF”) solutions,1 with an 
9 
AMF filing anticipated in Rhode Island this year.   
10 
 
11 
Q. 
If another jurisdiction adopts AMI/AMF, are there opportunities to 
12 
share costs? 
13 
A. 
Yes.  As set forth in the AMI Report (page 27), to the extent other 
14 
jurisdictions adopt AMI, there would be opportunities to obtain cost 
15 
synergies.  For example, certain back-office IT systems owned by the 
16 
Service Company will be used to manage AMI, store customer data, and 
17 
support billing and other customer service activities.  At this time, the 
18 
AMI costs are allocated entirely to Niagara Mohawk’s gas and electric 
19 
                                                 
1 In the Rhode Island and Massachusetts jurisdictions, AMI is often referred to as 
advanced metering functionality, or AMF. 


--- Page 21 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 19 of 31 
 
customers.  If, during the term of the rate plan, other jurisdictions 
1 
implement AMI using the same back-office systems, National Grid 
2 
proposes to re-allocate the costs and establish a deferral to pass back any 
3 
savings to Niagara Mohawk customers.  The magnitude of the synergies 
4 
created by AMI approval in multiple jurisdictions will depend, in part, on 
5 
the timing of the approval, as well as the specific deployment scenarios.   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
How would the Company re-allocate the shared costs? 
8 
A. 
As proposed in the AMI Report (page 27), the Company would share 
9 
such common costs consistent with the approved cost allocation 
10 
methodologies provided for in the Company’s Service Agreement with 
11 
the Service Company and the Service Company’s Cost Allocation 
12 
Manual, as may be amended or modified from time to time. 
13 
 
14 
V. 
Meter and Module Testing 
15 
Q. 
What does the Company propose regarding petitioning for approval 
16 
of the electric AMI meters?  
17 
A. 
The Company will support the meter manufacturer’s petition to the 
18 
Commission for approval of the electric AMI meters consistent with the 
19 
requirements of Public Service Law §67, 16 N.Y.C.R.R. Parts 92 and 93, 
20 


--- Page 22 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 20 of 31 
 
the 16 N.Y.C.R.R. Part 92 Operating Manual, and applicable Federal 
1 
Communications Commission (‘FCC”) regulations.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Is the Company seeking to clarify any of the electric meter testing 
4 
procedures in a full-scale AMI deployment scenario? 
5 
A. 
Yes.  Specifically, the Company seeks three clarifications.  First, the 
6 
Company proposes to establish a quality assurance testing program, 
7 
where it defines the quality assurance requirements for the manufacturer, 
8 
who will then pull the metering samples in accordance with the 
9 
Company’s procedures and send them to the Company for testing at the 
10 
Company’s New York-based meter lab.  Second, if the deployment 
11 
timeframe varies for each of the regions throughout the Company’s 
12 
service territory, the Company proposes testing a random sample of 200 
13 
meters in each AMI deployment region the year after deployment begins 
14 
in that region as part of the selective testing requirements set forth in the 
15 
Part 92 Operating Manual.  Third, the Company proposes defining the 
16 
AMR meters as retirement program meters once AMI meter deployment 
17 
begins, removing the AMR assets from the final accuracy and pick-for-
18 
test test requirements.  Once AMI deployment is complete in the region, 
19 
the Company will resume normal statistical and variable pick-for-test 
20 
requirements on the new AMI meter population.  
21 


--- Page 23 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 21 of 31 
 
Q. 
Why does the Company believe the proposed meter testing 
1 
clarifications are appropriate? 
2 
A. 
Given the historical accuracy of the current fleet of AMR meters, as 
3 
evidenced by the pick-for-test program, the Company believes it can 
4 
reduce costs for customers while still ensuring the accuracy of its meter 
5 
reads.  To that end, the Company’s proposal allows for a centralized 
6 
repository to efficiently receive and evaluate the meter population at its 
7 
New York-based meter testing facility.  Likewise, the Company believes 
8 
waiver or reductions in the pick-for-test and final accuracy testing 
9 
requirements will obviate the need for significant additional resources to 
10 
test, transport, and store approximately 1.7 million AMR metering assets 
11 
that are being permanently removed from the field.  This practice is 
12 
consistent with the approach taken during the Company’s prior large-
13 
scale meter deployment effort. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
What does the Company propose for approval and testing of the gas 
16 
modules? 
17 
A. 
The Company will petition the Commission for approval of the AMI-
18 
compatible gas modules consistent with the requirements of Public 
19 
Service Law §67, 16 N.Y.C.R.R. Parts 226 and 227, and applicable 
20 
Federal Communications Commission (“FCC”) regulations. 
21 


--- Page 24 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 22 of 31 
 
Q. 
Is the Company proposing any modifications to the gas-module 
1 
testing procedures? 
2 
A. 
No.  The Company’s proposal is focused on replacing AMR gas 
3 
modules, also referred to as ERTs, with AMI-compatible gas modules.  
4 
As such, the Company intends to use the EPS system to test each AMI-
5 
compatible gas module in its New York-based testing facility prior to 
6 
field installation.   
7 
 
8 
VI. 
AMI Fees 
 
9 
Q. 
Is the Company proposing any fees for customers who opt-out of 
10 
receiving an AMI meter? 
11 
A 
Yes.  As set forth in the AMI Report (page 31), customers who opt-out 
12 
prior to AMI installation will be assessed a monthly meter reading fee.  
13 
In addition, customers who receive an AMI meter and later choose to 
14 
opt-out of the AMI program will be assessed an initial fee to replace the 
15 
AMI meter, as well as the monthly meter reading fee.  The fees are set 
16 
forth in Exhibit ___ (ERDP-6), Schedule 5, and are based on those 
17 
proposed for customers who opted out of the AMR program.  See P.S.C. 
18 
220 – Electric Tariff (Rule 25.6) and P.S.C. 219 – Gas Tariff (Rule 13.6). 
19 
 
20 
Q. 
What is the monthly meter reading fee? 
21 


--- Page 25 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 23 of 31 
 
A. 
For customers who opt-out of an AMI meter, the monthly meter reading 
1 
fee is $33.65 for electric-only service, $30.04 for gas-only service, or 
2 
$46.87 for combined electric and gas service.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
What is the meter replacement fee for customers who opt-out after 
5 
receiving an AMI meter? 
6 
A. 
Customers who opt-out of the AMI meter after meter installation will 
7 
also be assessed an initial fee of $92.45 for an electric-only meter 
8 
replacement, $84.14 for gas-only meter replacement, or $154.62 for both 
9 
an electric and gas meter replacement.   
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Is the Company proposing a meter installation fee for customers who 
12 
initially opt-out of the AMI program and later ask for an AMI 
13 
meter? 
14 
A. 
No.  As set forth in the AMI Report (page 31), the Company seeks to 
15 
foster adoption of this technology, because it can deliver benefits to 
16 
customers and the overall electric and gas systems.  Therefore, the 
17 
Company is not proposing a fee or penalty for customers who adopt the 
18 
technology later than their peers.  
19 
 
20 
VII. 
Time-Varying Pricing  
21 


--- Page 26 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 24 of 31 
 
Q. 
Is the meter installation opt-out described above, the same opt-out 
1 
used when referring to the BCA scenarios? 
2 
A. 
No.  The Company proposed a two opt-out approach for AMI.  The first 
3 
pertains to the customers’ option whether to receive an AMI meter and 
4 
gas module.  The second refers to the customers’ option whether to 
5 
participate in the TVP rate structure proposed in the AMI Report.  The 
6 
Company calculated the BCA using both an opt-out TVP rate scenario, 
7 
where 85 percent of the customers are assumed to participate in the rate 
8 
structure, and an opt-in scenario, which assumed 20 percent of customers 
9 
participate in the TVP rates.   
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Please summarize the TVP rate structure included in the AMI 
12 
Report. 
13 
A. 
As set forth in the AMI Report (pages 22-23), based on feedback from 
14 
the AMI Collaborative, the Company proposed a TVP structure that 
15 
includes two components.  First, a time-of-use (“TOU”) supply rate with 
16 
on-peak periods from 10 a.m. to 9 p.m. excluding weekends and 
17 
holidays.  Second, a critical peak pricing (“CPP”) component designed to 
18 
recover capacity costs within 70 critical peak hours shown by New York 
19 
Independent System Operator (“NYISO”) conditions during the summer 
20 
period (June through September).   
21 


--- Page 27 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 25 of 31 
 
Q. 
Is the Company including mass market TVP rate leaves with this 
1 
filing? 
2 
A. 
No.  There are numerous TVP rate proposals and initiatives ongoing in 
3 
the State (see e.g., the AMI Report, AMI-related rate pilots, and the 
4 
White Paper on Rate Design for Mass Market Net Metering Successor 
5 
Tariff (December 2019) in Case 15-E-0751).  The Company anticipates 
6 
the Commission will consider a mass market TVP rate structure as part 
7 
of those separate proceedings.    
8

IX. 
Clifton Park Demonstration Project 
 
4 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s Clifton Park Demand Reduction 
5 
REV Demonstration Project. 
6 
A. 
The Company initiated the Clifton Park Demand Reduction REV 
7 
Demonstration Project (the “Demand Reduction Demonstration”) in 
8 
January 2017.  The project included installing approximately 13,300 
9 
electric AMI meters and 11,500 gas ERTs in Clifton Park, as well as 
10 
testing customer education strategies, high-bill alerts, and a peak-time 
11 
rewards (“PTR”) approach to reducing peak load.  The Company 
12 
finished installing AMI meters in July 2017, and it commenced its fourth 
13 
season of PTR beginning in June 2020.  The project, in its current form, 
14 
is expected to end March 31, 2021.   
15 
 
16 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s plan for Clifton Park following 
17 
expiration of the Demand Reduction Demonstration? 
18 
A. 
Based on the feedback received from the AMI Collaborative (AMI 
19 
Report, page 25), the Company proposed to modify and extend the 
20 
project to test innovative pricing rate structures.  On February 14, 2019, 
21 


--- Page 31 ---
Testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel 
Page 29 of 31 
 
the Company filed its initial Innovative Pricing REV Demonstration 
1 
Project (“Innovative Pricing Demonstration”) proposal seeking to 
2 
leverage the AMI capabilities in Clifton Park to test the TVP rate 
3 
structures proposed in the AMI Report.  See Case 19-E-0111.  After 
4 
further discussions with Staff, the Company filed an Innovative Pricing 
5 
Demonstration petition on October 22, 2019, modifying the rate 
6 
structures it proposed to test.  On January 24, 2020, the Company filed a 
7 
supplemental Innovative Pricing Demonstration petition.  The 
8 
Company’s Innovative Pricing Demonstration proposal remains pending 
9 
with the Commission.

Report of
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid
on the Proposed Implementation of Advanced Metering Infrastructure
I.
Preliminary Statement.
Pursuant to Section 15.4(f) of the Joint Proposal1 approved by Order of the Commission
on March 15, 2018 in this proceeding,2 Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid
(“National Grid” or the “Company”) hereby requests the New York State Public Service
Commission (the “Commission”) approve the Company’s proposed implementation of advanced
metering infrastructure (“AMI”) as set forth in this “Report”3 and as supported by the Electric
and Gas Advanced Metering Infrastructure Business Case (“AMI Business Case”), “Customer
Engagement Plan,” and Benefit-Cost Analysis (“BCA”) Model attached hereto as Exhibits A, B,
and C respectively.4
1 Cases 17-E-0238 and 17-G-0239, Proceedings on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges,
Rules and Regulations of Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation for Electric and Gas Service (“Rate Case
Proceeding”), Joint Proposal (filed January 19, 2018) (the “Joint Proposal”) at page 114.
2 Rate Case Proceeding, Order Adopting Terms of Joint Proposal and Establishing Electric and Gas Rate
Plans (issued March 15, 2018) (“Rate Case Order”).
3 By letters dated September 5, 2018 and October 19, 2018, the Commission’s Secretary, Kathleen H.
Burgess, extended the filing deadline for this Report until November 15, 2018.
4 The BCA Model contains confidential proprietary information. As such, the Company filed Exhibit C
with the Commission’s Records Access Officer in accordance with the provisions of Public Officers Law
§§ 87(2)(d) and 89(5)(a)(1), as well as the Commission’s implementing regulations 16 NYCRR §6-1.3.
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 2 of 313


--- Page 38 ---
3
The Company fully supports the Commission’s Reforming the Energy Vision (“REV”)
objectives, as well as the State’s clean energy goals. The smart meter technology contemplated
by the Company’s proposed investment in AMI is foundational to National Grid’s ability to
deliver on the new energy future embodied therein.5 The Company’s refined AMI proposal also
represents precisely the type of REV investment the Commission envisioned “to lower the costs
of and speed the achievement of the State’s policy goals through accelerating the deployment at
scale of solutions that create the most economic value for both consumers and for the State’s
energy system….”6
With AMI, the Company will be better positioned to develop and deploy solutions aimed
at achieving the State’s clean energy policy goals such as reducing greenhouse gas emissions 80
percent from 1990 levels by the year 2050 (the “80x50 Target”).7 Among other benefits, AMI
will support grid modernization and distributed system platform (“DSP”) planning functions,
5 See Case 14-M-0101, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission in Regard to Reforming the Energy
Vision (the “REV Proceeding”), Order Adopting Distributed System Implementation Plan Guidance
(issued April 20, 2016) at page 58 (“The deployment of AMI … will be an important contribution to
enabling utilities to assume the role of the DSP. AMI will provide information that affords customers the
opportunity to participate in demand response and energy efficiency programs, as well as innovative rate
structures, allowing them to better manage electricity consumption and bills and drive overall system
efficiencies.”); see also REV Proceeding, Department of Public Service Staff Whitepaper: Guidance for
2018 DSIP Updates (April 26, 2018) at page 26 (“Advanced Metering Infrastructure (AMI) provides
grid-edge measurement, data acquisition, and control capabilities which are either essential or beneficial
to a number or important functions in modern distribution system.” (Emphasis added.)); see also REV
Proceeding, Order Adopting Regulatory Policy Framework and Implementation Plan (issued February
26, 2015) (“Track One Order”) at page 98 (“It is clear that some form of advanced metering will be
needed to implement REV.”).
6 See Rate Case Order at page 85.
7 New York Executive Order No. 24, Establishing a Goal to Reduce Greenhouse Gas Emissions Eighty
Percent by the Year 2050 and Preparing a Climate Action Plan (2009); cf. Case 15-E-0302, Proceeding
on Motion of the Commission to Implement a Large-Scale Renewable Program and a Clean Energy
Standard, Order Adopting a Clean Energy Standard (Issued August 1, 2016) at page 78 (“[T]he
Commission finds and determines that the goal of the [State Energy Plan] that 50% of New York’s
electricity is to be generated by renewable sources by 2030, as part of a strategy to reduce statewide
greenhouse gas emissions 40% by 2030, is reasonable and necessary to provide for the safe and adequate
service of retail electric consumers in New York State and in a manner that promotes economy,
efficiency, and care for the public safety, the preservation of environmental values and he [sic]
conservation of natural resources.”).
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 3 of 313


--- Page 39 ---
4
such as demand modeling, load forecasting, hosting capacity analysis, and capital investment
planning, that will promote the development of a more dynamic, efficient, sustainable, and
reliable energy network capable of serving future generations of utility customers. AMI will also
serve as a foundation for innovative rate design opportunities. Without it, the Company will be
challenged to implement strategies that positively affect the State’s clean energy trajectory,
which at the current pace will be hard pressed to hit the 80x50 Target.8 Moreover, AMI will
provide customers with more information and greater control over their energy usage and
associated costs, the opportunity to participate in future innovative pricing plans, and the ability
to choose new solutions from third-party vendors who are responding to an animated energy
market.
Finally, without AMI, the Company will lag behind peer utilities throughout the
country, who have collectively installed approximately 78.9 million AMI meters as of 2017,9
with 90 million smart meter installations projected by 2020.10 The Company’s customers expect,
and the Company is committed to delivering, the same level of expanded service and benefits
that AMI is capable of providing.
The Company’s AMI proposal is driven by a commitment to advancing a clean energy
future. It is a once-in-a-generation opportunity to align the State’s critical policy objectives with
operational
requirements
and
customer
benefits,
as
the
Company’s
existing
metering
infrastructure is approaching life-cycle replacement. In furtherance of achieving these shared

clean energy goals, the Company respectfully requests the Commission approve this proposal to

II.
Background.
a. Current Metering Capabilities.
National Grid’s electric transmission and distribution business serves approximately 1.6
million customers across 25,000 square miles in Upstate New York.
The gas distribution
business serves more than 600,000 customers in the areas of central, northern, and eastern New
York. The Company currently uses automated meter reading (“AMR”) technology throughout
its service territory. The AMR system is read on a monthly basis by a fleet of Company vehicles
that retrieve consumption data from the AMR meter using radio frequencies.
Similarly, the
Company’s gas meters are equipped with an external module that records and transmits gas
consumption data measured by the gas meter.
Both the AMR meters and the gas modules (also
referred to as encoder receiver transmitters or ERTs) are nearing end-of-life conditions, and the
Company designed its proposed deployment of AMI and AMI-compatible gas modules11 to
closely align with the planned replacement cycle for those devices.
b. What is AMI?
AMI refers to four key advanced metering elements: 1) an integrated system of smart
electric meters and gas modules capable of capturing customer energy usage data at defined
intervals and supporting grid-edge applications; 2) a two-way communications network and
related information technology (“IT”) infrastructure for transmitting the data and control signals;
3) a meter data management system (“MDMS”), IT platform, and cybersecurity protections to
convert the data into actionable information (e.g., billing purposes); and 4) project management
11 The Company’s AMI proposal includes the installation of AMI-compatible gas modules.
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 5 of 313


--- Page 41 ---
6
and ongoing meter operations.12 In the field, AMI technology will capture and transmit energy
usage data (15-minute intervals every four hours for electric and one-hour intervals every eight
hours for gas) over a mesh13 communications network to the Company’s backhaul.
The
backhaul transmits the data to the Company’s head-end system, which collects the data before it
is transmitted to the MDMS, where it is then converted into a form suitable for billing or
advanced analytics.
Finally, the data is transmitted to customer systems (e.g., the Customer
Service System for billing or Green Button Connect functionality for authorized third-party
customer data sharing).
AMI is foundational to supporting the development of distribution markets in furtherance
of the Company’s role as the DSP provider.14
With AMI, the Company will be capable of
providing customers with the knowledge and tools needed to better inform their energy decisions
and manage energy costs.
AMI will also enable the Company to enhance planning and
operations, as well as integrate more distributed energy resources (“DER”)15 to the system. In
short, AMI increases access to detailed energy usage data; enhances energy management
opportunities and access to third-party services; improves outage management; offers more
detailed system information (e.g., voltage status); and enables the Company to remotely connect
or disconnect service.16
Taken together, the AMI capabilities will make it possible to drive
energy usage changes and increase DER integration in furtherance of the REV objectives and the
State’s clean energy goals.
12 See AMI Business Case at page 28 for a graphic depiction of AMI components.
13 The Company will utilize a cellular solution in areas (e.g., rural) where customer density is insufficient
to support a mesh network design.
14 See supra note 4.
15 REV Proceeding, Track One Order at page 3, footnote 3 (The Commission stated, “‘DER’ is used to
describe a wide variety of distribute energy resources, including end-use energy efficiency, demand
response, distributed storage, and distributed generation.”).
16 Any remote connections or disconnections would be done in accordance with applicable laws and
regulations, including the Home Energy Fair Practices Act (“HEFPA”).

c.
Prior AMI Proposals.
The Company first filed an AMI business case as part of it initial Distributed System
Implementation Plan (“DSIP”) on June 30, 2016.17
There, National Grid identified AMI as
foundational to its grid modernization effort, analyzed three alternative deployment options, and
outlined a proposed investment plan. The three scenarios analyzed were: 1) full deployment; 2)
deployment in high-density population areas; and 3) opt-in only deployment. For each scenario,
the Company also considered a number of sensitivity assumptions regarding cross-jurisdictional
synergies with Company affiliates (i.e., sharing common back-office system costs with a
simultaneous Massachusetts deployment) and the levels of participation in a time-varying pricing
(“TVP”) rate program.
Of these scenarios, the BCA models showed positive ratios supporting the full-
deployment scenario.18
Only full deployment would provide the customer, operational, and
societal benefits of AMI in a cost-effective manner. Indeed, anything less than full deployment
would require a more costly non-mesh AMI solution that offers less functionality.
Full
deployment also aligned with the broader planning, operational, and market capabilities
necessary to put National Grid on a path toward achieving the REV objectives and moving the
State closer to its clean energy goals. Based on the foregoing, the Company proposed a six-year
implementation timeline, including a five-year meter installation program, beginning in fiscal
year 2019.19

Distributed System Implementation Plans (“DSIP Proceeding”).
18 See Initial DSIP at pages 85 to 86.

On April 28, 2017, National Grid filed an updated AMI business case when it initiated

two years later (beginning in fiscal year 2021 instead of fiscal year 2019). The Company further
outlined its proposal for the initial phase of the project, including design, procurement, and back-

track the success of the AMI program.23

formal AMI Collaborative large and small group meetings.41

separately met with stakeholders who sought deep-dive discussions on areas of interest, such as

Implementation Plan Report.
a. Refined and Updated AMI Business Case.
National Grid proposes a phased deployment, on an opt-out basis, of electric AMI meters
and AMI-compatible gas modules for the Company’s gas meters over a six-year period
beginning in fiscal year 2020.50
The AMI Business Case includes a number of notable
refinements and updates as compared to the AMI plan set forth in the Company’s rebuttal

ii. AMI Costs
AMI deployment costs are generally comprised of four categories: 1) AMI meters, gas
modules, and installation; 2) two-way communications network and installation; 3) IT platform
and ongoing IT operations; and 4) project management and ongoing business operations. The
Company anticipates these components will collectively cost $657.4 million (20-year NPV) to
procure, install, and operate. With the support of Accenture Consulting, the Company utilized
the aforementioned RFS with AMI vendors, industry benchmarks, and its own experience
implementing AMI as part of the Clifton Park REV Demonstration Project (“Clifton Park
Demonstration”) to help refine many of the costs.
The industry benchmarks, in particular,
suggest that the Company’s estimated per meter cost of $241 is well within the range of industry
standards.57 A complete breakdown of the costs is contained in the AMI Business Case.58
1. AMI Cost Tracking and Reporting.
For purposes of tracking the costs, the Company will generally follow the approach
agreed to in the Joint Proposal for certain large projects (e.g., Information Services (“IS”) and
Gas Business Enablement, Section 7.8.4).59 Biannually, the Company will file an AMI Program

Company’s deployment schedule, because no plant closings are forecasted prior to fiscal year
2022.
3. Capital Expenditure Cost Cap.
The Company proposes to cap total capital costs, comprised of capital expenditures for
both the Company and the Service Company, related to AMI deployment at $446.35 million
through fiscal year 2025. In future rate proceedings, the Company may seek recovery of costs in
excess of this cap that are associated with incremental investments in AMI beyond those
described in this Report, as long as the incremental costs are prudently incurred to address
61 When the Service Company owns a shared asset, it charges the Company and its affiliates a

unforeseen circumstances or conditions associated with the AMI deployment or the incremental
costs are justified by measureable benefits. The Company believes the structure of this cap will
afford it the flexibility to substitute, change, or modify the timing of its AMI investments as
necessary to deliver the scope of the program without such rigidity as to unnecessarily require
Commission approval of all implementation adjustments.
4. Commitment to Performance-Based Ratemaking Principles.
At its core, this AMI proposal reflects a commitment to advancing the Commission’s
careful and deliberate shift “towards aligning financial incentives with REV objectives by
combining discrete reforms to conventional ratemaking with new earning opportunities that
better align the utility and consumer economic welfare interests.”62
The Company’s
commitment to performance-based ratemaking principles can be seen in three aspects of this
proposal.
First, deploying AMI will drive a step change in utility planning capabilities, customer
energy behaviors, and levels of third-party market participation, as measured by the Company’s
suite of Earnings Adjustment Mechanisms (“EAMs”). Indeed, AMI technology directly supports
the energy policy goals and outcomes underlying the Company’s EAMs by enabling more
granular energy usage data and system information that will animate the market for new energy
management opportunities and third-party services.
For example, the DER Utilization EAM
metric incentivizes the Company to make productive use of more granular data to increase DER
interconnections; likewise, the Peak Reduction EAM metric incentivizes the Company to work
with customers through increased education and innovative rate designs to shift or reduce energy
use during peak periods. In future rate proceedings, the Company will consider the impacts of
62 REV Proceeding, Order Adopting a Ratemaking and Utility Revenue Model Policy Framework (issued
May 19, 2016) (“Track Two Order”) at page 39.
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 18 of 313


--- Page 54 ---
19
AMI, including the customer response to TVP, in developing proposals for the next evolution of
EAMs.
Second, to address the perceived capital bias reflected in traditional ratemaking, the
Company proposes to share with customers any efficiency savings associated with its AMI-
related capital costs.
To the extent the Company’s actual capital costs at the time of project
closure63 are less than the full sanction estimate64 by $10 million or more, the Company will
share the savings with customers (80 percent to customers / 20 percent to the Company). This
approach is consistent with similar efficiency mechanisms in the Joint Proposal, which includes a
capital incentive for certain projects (e.g., Green Button Connect), giving the Company an
opportunity to earn an incentive for managing the level of capital investment.65 The efficiency
sharing mechanism contemplated here is based on the Company’s forecast capital costs (as
reflected in the revenue requirement) of $408 million during the first six years of deployment.66
An example calculation of the AMI capital expenditure efficiency savings proposal, including
the $10 million trigger, is attached hereto as Exhibit F.
Finally, the Company remains committed to identifying opportunities where it can further
align the financial interests of customers, the Company, and third parties with the REV
objectives.
To that end, the Company proposes to convene a collaborative with Staff and
interested stakeholders during the pre-deployment phase for purposes of developing: 1) a process

5. Cost Allocation.
The Company proposes to assign AMI costs that are discretely electric or gas costs, such
as electric meters and gas modules, to the respective electric and gas businesses. Likewise, for
common costs that support both businesses, the Company proposes to primarily allocate such
costs to the respective service classifications based on electric and gas customer counts. The
electric and gas AMI costs reflected on a nominal value would flow through the electric and gas
delivery revenue requirement in upcoming rate cases and follow traditional allocated cost of
service study (“ACOSS”) cost allocation methodologies.
In general, the allocators applied to the costs are the same as the allocators filed in the
most recent rate case. However, in a few areas, such as costs of telecommunications, potential
transformers (“PTs”), current transformers (“CTs”), and IS, it is appropriate to have a more

see also Rate Case Proceeding, Joint Proposal at page 79.

specific allocation.
The development of more granular cost allocators in such instances is
consistent with the terms of the Joint Proposal, where the Company committed to considering
specific allocators for IS projects equal to or greater than $1 million.68
In this instance, the
Company allocated costs associated with AMI based on allocators filed in the most recent rate
case, or, where more refined cost allocators were developed, the Company allocated the costs to
the service classes based on cost-causation principles. The costs and associated allocators were
discussed in the AMI Collaborative, and led to the development of the proposed cost allocation
set forth in Exhibit G, which is intended to provide certainty for customers regarding cost
allocations associated with this proposal.
iii. AMI Benefits.
The Company identified four broad quantifiable AMI benefit categories: 1) avoided
AMR and VDER meter costs; 2) avoided operation and maintenance (“O&M”) costs; 3)
customer benefits; and 4) societal benefits.69
Collectively, the Company anticipates its AMI
proposal, including the opt-out TVP rate structure, will provide $752.87 million (20-year NPV)
in benefits across the aforementioned categories. In addition, the Company anticipates a number
of additional foreseeable benefits, such as reductions in theft of service and bad debt write-offs,
as well as potential advanced street lighting and gas operational applications that are not included
in the BCA.70 A complete discussion of the benefits is set forth in the AMI Business Case.71

b. Benefit-Cost Analysis.
Consistent with the BCA Handbook calculation methodology, the BCA ratio for the
Company’s proposed implementation of AMI using the SCT, as set forth in this Report is 1.15.72
The ratio considers the present value benefits and costs of the Company’s preferred TVP opt-out
approach. By way of comparison, the Company also provides the same BCA analysis for a TVP
opt-in rate scenario, which has a BCA ratio of 1.02.73 For both the opt-out and opt-in analysis,
the Company refined its prior approach, narrowing the focus to the mid-point of the high and low
TVP savings rates for each scenario. Based on the foregoing, the Company believes the TVP
opt-out rate scenario is best suited to deliver the most benefits to customers and the system. A
more detailed discussion of the BCA ratios and other financial metrics are included in the AMI
Business Case.74
c.
Mass Market75 Innovative Rate Design.
The Company’s AMI proposal includes a TVP opt-out structure consisting of TOU and
CPP components. Although the specific tariff leaves will be filed in the Company’s next rate
case, National Grid believes an opt-out TVP approach provides the best opportunity for
customers to realize the full panoply of benefits enabled by AMI.76
To that end, it seeks
approval of the TVP structure, which includes an on-peak TOU period from 10 a.m. to 9 p.m.
(excluding weekends and holidays), when electricity prices are typically higher, capturing more
expensive electricity as reflected in New York Independent System Operator (“NYISO”) load
zones.
The TVP structure also includes a CPP rate that is similarly designed to recover the
72 See supra note 53.
73 See id.
74 See AMI Business Case, Exhibit A at pages 36-38.
75 “Mass market” refers to residential and small commercial customers in the Company’s non-demand

capacity costs within 70 critical peak hours shown by NYISO conditions during the summer
period (June through September). The Company redesigned the TOU and CPP rate structure to
shorten the on-peak periods and reduce the number of critical peak event hours after receiving
feedback from Staff and stakeholders in the AMI Collaborative.
Generally, TVP rates, as
contemplated in the Company’s proposal, more closely reflect market variations while also
providing incentives to achieve system efficiencies such as peak reduction, which the
Commission identified as an “immediate” REV priority.77
In crafting the TVP proposal, the Company performed a customer TVP design impact
analysis, the details of which can be found in Exhibit H.78 To analyze the customer impacts from
the proposed new TVP structure, the Company compared delivery and supply charges under the
current rate structures to proposed delivery and TVP supply rate structures, on a total bill basis
(including surcharges and taxes).
The comparisons included both the SC-1 and SC-2 Non-
Demand (“ND”) service classes.
Specifically, the Company considered fifteen actual SC-1
customers and fifteen SC-2 ND customers selected from load research samples representing
three customers per usage stratum (i.e., kWh ranges) with a low load factor, medium load factor,
and high load factor, as well as nine residential low-income customers.
Generally, the rate
design impact analysis showed that most customers, using a conservative 4.5 percent on-peak
energy savings scenario, will see some modest benefit from the TVP rates, as proposed. The
Company believes these benefits can be further enhanced through focused education and
outreach as contemplated in the Customer Engagement Plan.

i. Mass Market Bill Guarantee.
In addition to the robust customer engagement activities included in the Customer
Engagement Plan, the Company also proposes a one-year period for customers to learn about
their meters before the new rate structure takes effect, followed by a bill guarantee for all
customers in the first year they are on TVP (i.e., the second year after meter installation).79 The
guarantee will be based on a comparison of the customer’s annual charges under the TVP model
and what the customer would have otherwise paid under the Company’s standard volumetric rate
for that same twelve month period.
If the customer paid more under the TVP structure, the
customer would be credited that amount to ensure he or she paid no more than the basic
volumetric rate in the first year. In addition, if a “new” customer (one who has not been at a
premise served by the Company since the introduction of TVP) moves into the service territory,
the customer will also receive the one-year bill guarantee.
For low-income customers, the Company plans to continue to offer an extended bill
guarantee period on a graduated basis.80 In year three after installation of the smart meter, the
Company will compare the low-income customer’s annual charges under the TVP model and
what the customer would have otherwise paid under the Company’s standard volumetric rate for
the same twelve month period. If the comparison shows the low-income customer paid more
than five percent above what he or she would have otherwise paid under the Company’s current
basic volumetric supply rate for the entire twelve months, the customer will be credited that
amount above 5 percent. The guarantee would extend to year four after meter installation with a
limit of ten percent above what the customer would have otherwise paid under the Company’s
basic volumetric supply rate for the entire twelve month period.
As with non-low-income

The Company will also analyze and file a report with the Commission detailing progress

ii. Mass Market Rate Design Pilot.
The Company intends to pilot the TVP rate approach, and is working with Staff to align
the Clifton Park Demonstration with this proposal. By doing so, the Company believes it can
further test the effectiveness of its customer engagement strategies as well as customer response
to the TVP design.
The Company will then incorporate additional lessons learned and best
practices from the reconfigured project into the specific opt-out TVP tariff leaves the Company
will file as part of its next rate case. The Company will separately file the revised proposal for
the Clifton Park Demonstration with the Commission.
iii. Additional TVP Rate Analysis.
The Company will also prepare a technical report in advance of the next rate case,
discussing mass market customer responses to TVP, as well as the Company’s thinking for how
current EAMs, such as system efficiency and energy efficiency, could evolve to reflect AMI and
TVP. In addition, the Company proposes to identify industry benchmarks for meter opt-out rates
and TVP opt-out rates, both of which impact the ability to achieve projected TVP benefit levels.
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)

Page 25 of 313


--- Page 61 ---
26
In situations where either the meter opt-out rate or TVP opt-out rate falls below the industry
benchmarks, National Grid would commit modest incremental funding for AMI marketing,
education, and outreach to drive further customer benefits.
This approach assumes the
Commission approves the meter opt-out fees (i.e., meter reading and change out) and opt-out
TVP rate design proposed herein, as well as upside-only EAMs in the Company’s next rate case.
To the extent any of the assumptions change, the Company would seek to re-visit this proposal.
The Company believes the opt-out TVP rate structure is best suited to deliver the most
benefits for customers in alignment with the REV objectives and the State’s clean energy goals.
Research from around the country indicates that participation rates (and therefore aggregate
benefits) are far higher for programs in which customers are offered TVP on an opt-out basis.81
This same research suggests that under an opt-in rate scenario, fewer customers would enroll in
TVP, and, although opt-in customers tend to be more active in terms of managing energy use, the
benefits in the aggregate are much smaller than under an opt-out scenario. A comprehensive
analysis of TVP consumer behavior studies by the U.S. Department of Energy (“DOE”) found
that on average, enrollment rates were over six times greater for opt-out designs than for opt-in
designs.82 Likewise, the Company’s affiliates employed an opt-out approach for TVP with the
Worcester Pilot, and experienced a 98 percent retention rate after two years.83
The DOE Report also found that retention rates across the opt-in and opt-out scenarios,
are roughly similar across multiple different utilities and did not change dramatically in the first
81 U.S. Department of Energy, Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory, American Recovery and
Reinvestment Act of 2009: Final Report on Customer Acceptance, Retention, and Response to Time-
Based Rates from Consumer Behavior Studies (“DOE Report”) (November 2016); see also Customer
Engagement Plan, Exhibit B at Section 5.
82 See id.; see also, Rate Case Proceeding, Staff AMI Testimony at page 27 (“opt-in programs had an
average participation rate of 15 percent, versus 93 percent for opt-out programs.”).
83 Navigant, National Grid Smart Energy Solutions Pilot: Final Evaluation Report (May 5, 2017)
(“Worcester Pilot Report”) at page 17.
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)

Page 26 of 313


--- Page 62 ---
27
two years of the studies.84 Finally, the larger enrollment numbers in opt-out scenarios lead to
more cost-effective program designs. The greater degree of participation in opt-out scenarios
also drives far higher benefits.
For example, over a 20-year NPV calculation, National Grid
estimates that the TOU / CPP pricing design would produce $49.33 million in benefits on an opt-
in basis and more than double that amount, $137.54 million, in benefits on an opt-out basis.
Similarly, the 20-year NPV of the CO2 benefits of the TOU / CPP rates are $8.29 million in an
opt-in scenario, but nearly three times that amount, $22.33 million, in the opt-out scenario. Costs
are similar across both scenarios, but the increased benefits from the opt-out scenario move the
overall SCT ratio from 1.02 for opt-in to 1.15 for opt-out.
d. Cross-Jurisdictional Synergies.

to deploy AMI / AMF technology, the Company proposes to share the costs associated with the
Service Company’s shared infrastructure, as well as ongoing operation and maintenance
(“O&M”) costs among such affiliates. The Company would share such common costs consistent
with the approved cost allocation methodologies provided for in the Company’s Service
Agreement with the Service Company and the Service Company’s Cost Allocation Manual, as
may be amended or modified from time to time.85 Achieving such cross-jurisdictional synergies
is in the public interest.
To further illustrate this, the Company provides additional BCA
scenarios in the AMI Business Case.86

IV.
Customer Engagement Plan.
The Company’s Customer Engagement Plan is a robust roadmap intended to guide the
Company and customers through the three phases of the planned AMI deployment: 1) customer
awareness and education prior to meter installation; 2) the customer experience through
deployment, including the ability to opt out of receiving a new AMI meter; and 3) empowering
and enabling customers with AMI meters to maximize the functionality and benefits made
possible by the new devices and future innovative pricing plans. Overall, the objective of the
Customer Engagement Plan is to inform and educate customers about National Grid’s AMI
implementation, increasing both acceptance of the new meters and participation in innovative
rate structures that align customer benefits with clean-energy objectives.
In developing this approach, the Company looked closely at its own experience with the
Clifton Park Demonstration, the experience of its affiliates’ Worcester Pilot, as well as the AMI
customer engagement plan filed by peer utilities Con Edison and O&R, gleaning and
incorporating best practices from each. Likewise, the AMI Collaborative stakeholder feedback
generated during the customer engagement small group sessions provided several key insights
discussed in further detail below.
i. Phase One: Awareness.
In Phase One, prior to deployment of new meters, the Company will focus on customer
and key stakeholder awareness, education, and outreach. During this phase, the Company will
develop pamphlets, flyers, door hangers, and other collateral to use in those efforts.
The
education materials will include information on the customer’s option to choose whether to have
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 28 of 313


--- Page 64 ---
29
the AMI meter installed. The Company will also train employees on AMI deployment, ensuring
that all facets of the customer experience are covered (e.g., customer service representatives,
account managers, community liaisons, etc.). Following the creation of the education materials
and internal training, the Company will commence outreach efforts through multiple channels
with customers and key community stakeholders. The AMI Collaborative proved particularly
valuable in shaping the Company’s thinking about this phase of AMI implementation,
identifying three key enhancements to the Company’s initial approach.
First, while the Company places an emphasis on knowing its customers, stakeholders
provided important suggestions on how to best craft a pre-deployment outreach strategy. This
includes a focus beyond digital communication, recognizing that some customers do not use such
channels.
Moreover, for all communications, stakeholders highlighted the importance of
offering multilingual materials, and using simple, easy-to-understand language. The Company
incorporated these helpful suggestions into the Customer Engagement Plan.
Second, stakeholders emphasized the importance of meeting with customers in their local
communities to educate them about AMI. While the Company already intended to hold “lunch-
and-learn” style sessions and workshops, the suggestions of AMI Collaborative stakeholders
prompted the Company to create a Mobile Sustainability Hub.87 Modeled on the success of the
Company’s Massachusetts affiliates’ Sustainability Hub, which is part of the Worcester Pilot, the
Company proposes to develop a mobile hub capable of enhancing AMI outreach with customers
in different communities.
It will serve as a place where customers, the community, and
interested stakeholders can learn about the Company’s AMI implementation plan, and how a
smarter grid can deliver greater choice, control, and convenience. Moreover, because the hub is
mobile, the Company can use it in select communities prior to deployment in those specific
87 See Customer Engagement Plan, Exhibit B at Section 4.3.3.
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 29 of 313


--- Page 65 ---
30
areas, and the hub can return during or after deployment to foster further learning during phases
two and three.
Third, calling upon recent experiences, stakeholders noted that community leaders, not
the Company, are often seen by customers as trusted advocates and therefore the Company
should actively engage elected officials, local government administrators, and community
organizers as part of the pre-deployment outreach. If properly informed, such leaders can be
helpful resources in identifying and addressing customer concerns. Likewise, they can play an
important role in helping the Company better understand customer perspectives, which will
allow for a more timely and personalized response to issues as they arise.
ii. Phase Two: Deployment.
In Phase Two, the Company proposes a Ninety-Sixty-Thirty Day Communications Plan
timed for communities immediately prior to AMI installation.88 Ninety days before deployment,
the Company will continue its direct and targeted education effort with customers, elected
officials, and community leaders in the area where the Company will soon deploy AMI. The
Mobile Sustainability Hub will also play a key role in these efforts.
Sixty days before
deployment, the Company will use direct-mail letters, emails, bill inserts, and digital media to
inform customers of the planned meter installation. Community meetings and local customer
outreach efforts (e.g., workshops) will also continue.
Finally, thirty days before meters are
installed, the Company will brief community leaders on the status of AMI deployment and
provide an updated frequently asked questions (“FAQ”) sheet. Customer communications will
also continue through meter deployment, and will include press releases, working with local
media, and proactively addressing social media comments.
88 See Customer Engagement Plan, Exhibit B at Section 4.3.2.
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 30 of 313


--- Page 66 ---
31
Finally, on meter installation day, field workers will attempt to make contact with the
customer. At this time, a customer may verbally opt-out of receiving an AMI meter.
If the
customer is unavailable and has not previously opted out, electricity to the home or business will
be disconnected to provide a safe working environment for the technician. Once the technician
completes the new AMI meter installation, National Grid will provide the customer with a
confirmation door hanger.
The Company believes AMI meters are foundational to achieving clean-energy objectives
and a necessary prerequisite for customers to more fully participate in the clean-energy economy
and capture the anticipated benefits. However, as with the prior deployment of AMR meters, the
Company recognizes that a certain segment of customers may not be ready to embrace the new
technology.
Such customers will have the choice to opt out of receiving an AMI meter.
Customers who opt out will be assessed a monthly meter reading fee to cover the Company’s
cost of manually reading the non-AMI meter.89
During the AMI Collaborative, stakeholders raised concerns about charges for late
adopters. The Company agreed with stakeholder sentiment that the goal should be to foster AMI
adoption in furtherance of the AMI benefits without penalizing customers who may make that
decision later than their peers. Accordingly, customers who initially opt out but later elect to
receive an AMI meter will not be charged a late-adoption fee or penalty. Rather, the Company
will install the AMI meter at no charge the next time there is a service vehicle in the area with a
crew capable of installing the new meter. Once the AMI meter is installed, the Company will
cease the monthly reading fee charge. However, customers who initially receive an AMI meter,
but later opt out of the program will be assessed a one-time meter replacement fee, as well as the

(“Electric Tariff”), Rule 25.6, Leaf 120.1 (effective July 1, 2014); see also P.S.C. No. 219 – Gas Service
Tariff Rule 13.6, Leaf 69 (effective July 1, 2014).

aforementioned monthly meter reading fees.90 Under the Company’s proposed implementation
plan, the first set of AMI meters will not be installed until after the Company’s next rate case;
therefore, the Company will propose the specific fees and associated tariff leaves as part of that
filing.
iii. Phase Three: Empowerment and Enablement.
The key feature of this phase will be launching and educating customers about the
Company’s TVP rates.91 The rates are designed to incentivize behavioral changes in energy use
that save money while advancing clean-energy goals, such as system efficiency. To fully realize
the benefits of smart-meter technology, the granular energy usage insights provided by AMI
meters must be paired with operational efficiencies, customer benefits, and proactive market
initiatives.
Phase Three is focused on empowering and enabling customers to use the functionality
provided by their new AMI meters to achieve these benefits.
To do this, National Grid will
develop and implement a web-based Customer Energy Management Platform that, when coupled
with innovative rate structures (i.e., TVP), can drive customer savings and clean-energy benefits.
Specifically, the Customer Energy Management Platform will allow customers to view energy
usage, including AMI meter interval data, better understand their bill, and act upon personalized
energy savings recommendations. This level of personalization will include items, such as the
energy efficient technologies currently available to customers on the Company’s E-Commerce
Platform and Solar Marketplace. In addition, by deploying AMI, the Company will animate the
market for third-parties to provide home-area network products that give customers real-time
energy usage information. Likewise, Green Button Connect functionality will enable customers
90 See id.
91 See Customer Engagement Plan, Exhibit B at Section 4.4.
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 32 of 313


--- Page 68 ---
33
to share their energy usage data directly with third-parties, who can use that information to offer
additional energy-management products and services.
iv. Vulnerable Populations.
Throughout the three phases, the Company will place a particular emphasis on ensuring
that vulnerable populations (e.g., low-income, elderly, and medical hardship customers) are well
educated about the available options (e.g., meter opt-out and rate opt-out), the resources
available (e.g., energy efficiency incentives) to help them fully capture the AMI benefits, and the
protections in place (e.g., bill guarantee) while they learn how to maximize AMI functionality.
For elderly customers, the Company learned from the Worcester Pilot that face-to-face
interactions are often preferred – the Company will incorporate this into its outreach efforts. For
customers identified in the Company system as possessing life support equipment or requiring
uninterruptable service for medical reasons, meters will not be changed without explicit
permission and a scheduled pre-approved time for AMI meter installation.
v. Metrics.
To measure the success of the Company’s AMI initiative, the Company proposes a
number of metrics and reporting elements focused on both customer engagement and overall
operational / programmatic performance.
A table of the proposed metrics and reporting
requirements is attached hereto as Exhibit I. In general, the Company proposes reporting the
items in either semi-annual or annual filings with the Commission, commencing with the
effective date of the particular metric (e.g., customer rate opt outs cannot be reported until meters
are installed and the rate plan becomes effective).
The proposed customer engagement metrics and reporting requirements fall into three
categories: 1) awareness; 2) enablement, empower, and tools; and 3) rates.
Each category is
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 33 of 313


--- Page 69 ---
34
designed to inform the Company’s understanding of how customers are responding to the
Company’s education and outreach efforts, which, in turn, will drive the success of the AMI
deployment. For example, the Company will survey customer awareness of AMI technology
and features, it will report on the number of customers accessing the Customer Energy
Management Platform, and track the number and percent of customers who opt out of the TVP
rate.
With regard to operational and programmatic metrics, the Company’s metrics, similar to
Con Edison, cover: 1) deployment; 2) billing; 3) outage management; 4) system operation and
environmental benefits; and 5) AMI program progress. These metrics will help to paint a picture
of the Company’s progress through implementation relative to the forecasted results. Some of
the key metrics include an analysis of how many meters the Company is installing relative to its
anticipated rollout targets, false outages identified using AMI functionality, and emission
reductions.
vi. Cybersecurity and Data Privacy.
The Company provided a confidential cybersecurity briefing to Staff, where the
Company discussed its risk-based threat-assessment approach to cybersecurity, as well as its data
privacy protections. As set forth in the Customer Engagement Plan, the Company developed a
Smart Grid Privacy Statement,92 and employs a stringent data privacy audit program to assess its
data protections, identify gaps, and ensure compliance with the Department of Energy Privacy
Program and other industry-leading best practices.
vii. Data Latency.
For electric customers, the Company proposes 15-minute raw interval data delivered to
customers every four hours. For gas customers, the Company proposes one-hour raw interval
92 See Customer Engagement Plan, Exhibit B at Appendix F.
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 34 of 313


--- Page 70 ---
35
data available to customers every eight hours. This information will be available to customers on
the Customer Energy Management Platform, with bill quality data available within 24 hours of
the end of the billing interval.
As suggested by the collaborative stakeholders, the Company
further agreed to reassess reporting intervals one year after deployment to determine whether to
move electric raw data reporting to every 30 minutes (as compared to the current 4 hour
proposal). Regardless of the data latency provisions, customers may access near real-time data
by procuring home-area network technology (e.g., an energy “picture frame”).
viii. Third-Party Data Access.
As part of the AMI Business Case, the Company proposes to implement the Green
Button Connect My Data initiative.
With this expanded functionality, customers and the
Company (at the customer’s request) can share energy usage data directly with vetted third
parties. With this new feature, customers will enjoy new opportunities to work with third parties
to better understand energy usage and take meaningful actions to manage their bills, including
customized energy solutions.
As with other New York utilities, data sharing through Green
Button Connect will be automated and provided in a secure format.
V.
Interim VDER Metering and Rate Design Plan for Mass Market Customers
As part of the VDER proceeding, the Commission is developing a net energy metering
successor tariff for mass market customers with on-site DER that would be effective January 1,
2020.93 To effectuate the new tariff model, the Commission anticipates customers will require
an interval metering solution.
Prior to (and in the absence of) approval for the Company to
deploy a mesh AMI network across its service territory, the Company will rely on a more costly
cellular AMI solution. This approach will allow the Company to implement the new VDER rate
