1 
Introduction 
 
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“National Grid” or the “Company”) 
proposes service territory-wide deployment of advanced metering infrastructure (“AMI”) to 
modernize its electric and gas meters and supporting infrastructure and systems, offering new 
capabilities to its customers.  
 
The deployment of AMI will position the Company to develop and deploy solutions aimed at 
achieving the State’s clean energy policy goals, such as reducing greenhouse gas emissions 80 
percent from 1990 levels by the year 2050 (“80x50”). Among other benefits, AMI will support 
grid modernization and distributed system platform (“DSP”) planning functions, including 
demand modeling, load forecasting, hosting capacity analysis, and capital investment planning.  
These functions will promote the development of a more dynamic, efficient, sustainable, and 
reliable energy network capable of serving future generations of utility customers.  AMI will also 
enable time-varying pricing (“TVP”) rate designs necessary to support Distributed Energy 
Resource (“DER”) valuation, new innovative services, and distribution system efficiency. 
Moreover, AMI will provide customers with more information and greater control over their 
energy usage and associated costs, and the ability to choose new and innovative solutions from 
authorized energy vendors.  Finally, implementation of AMI will help bring the Company in line 
with utilities throughout the country, where smart meters are installed on more than 55 percent of 
households as of 2016, with 90 million smart meter installations projected by 2020. The 
Company’s customers expect, and the Company is committed to delivering, expanded service 
and benefits that AMI is capable of providing. 
 
The Company’s AMI Business Case (the “Business Case”) is organized into three parts. The 
Company has taken a comprehensive approach in developing its proposal, collaborating with 
Department of Public Service Staff (“Staff”) and a variety of other interested stakeholders to 
refine its approach to AMI deployment. Part one introduces the background leading up to this 
proposal, highlighting the Company’s experiences since its initial AMI filing in June 2016. It 
includes a summary of the Company’s approach to leverage its learnings from demonstration 
projects and also emphasizes the important role of the AMI Collaborative process. Part two 
addresses how the Company will structure and launch its AMI program – the AMI deployment 
timeline, project management, and vendor selection. Part three discusses the benefit-cost analysis 
(“BCA”), explaining the methodology and summarizing the costs and benefits results. Part three 
also builds on the quantitative BCA analysis and qualitatively describes how AMI can be 
extended to support future use cases to provide additional customer value, and supports the 
Company’s role as the DSP provider, as reflected in its Distributed System Implementation Plan 
(“DSIP”) filings. 
 
1.1 
AMI Deployment Objectives  
 
AMI provides the foundation to support fundamental change in the energy future of the 
Company’s customers, the electric and gas distribution system, and the State of New York. By 
investing in AMI, National Grid will be taking a key step toward achieving the Commission’s 
Reforming the Energy Vision (“REV”) objectives:  
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 41 of 313


--- Page 77 ---
4 
 
 
 
• Enhanced customer knowledge and tools that will support effective management of the 
total energy bill; 
• Market animation and leverage of customer contributions; 
• System wide efficiency; 
• Fuel and resource diversity;  
• System reliability and resiliency; and 
• Reduction of carbon emissions.1 
 
In many ways, REV has expanded beyond its initial objectives to encompass nearly all elements 
of New York’s clean-energy policy agenda to include the decarbonization of electricity supply, 
reduced energy consumption, and electrification of the transportation sector. In support of REV, 
the Company has implemented its Connect21 initiative to modernize networks and introduce 
new technologies. This framework links customer needs and policy goals with technology and 
market solutions, driving the Company toward the utility model of the future. Additionally, the 
release of the Company’s Northeast 80x50 Pathway, lays out a vision for advancing a clean-
energy future throughout the State.2  
 
Any effort aimed at driving a fundamental change in the energy future of customers will require 
new business capabilities supported by modern technology. Absent AMI, the Company and its 
customers will be ill-prepared to drive the changes necessary to achieve the REV and clean-
energy vision. The implementation of AMI will allow the Company to empower customers with 
the knowledge and tools required to better inform energy decisions and effectively manage their 
energy bills, driving customer benefits while supporting clean-energy initiatives. AMI will also 
provide infrastructure and capabilities necessary to support distribution markets and their 
integration with wholesale markets as part of the Company’s role as the DSP provider. Lastly, 
the AMI infrastructure will enable improved planning and operations to support the integration 
of increasing levels of DER.  
 
1.2 
Current AMI State Characteristics 
 
AMI across the United States 
 
The Institute for Electric Innovation (“IEI”) has identified that over forty electric utilities across 
the United States have fully deployed smart meters as of the end of 2016.3 During this time,

smart metering has reached over 55 percent market saturation, with 72 million smart meters

AMI in New York 
 
On November 1, 2016, the Joint Utilities filed the Supplemental DSIP in Public Service 
Commission (“Commission”) Case 16-M-0411. In this filing, all but one of the Joint Utilities 
indicated that full AMI deployment was foundational for their respective DSPs. Subsequently, 
the Commission approved Consolidated Edison Company of New York, Inc.’s (“Con Edison”) 
and Orange and Rockland Utilities, Inc.’s (“O&R”) AMI proposals and is currently considering 
petitions from New York State Electric and Gas Corporation (“NYSEG”) / Rochester Gas and 
Electric Corporation (“RG&E”) to implement AMI.  Each of the filings is driven by similar 
considerations: support of the Commission’s REV goals to further empower customers, 
improved system efficiency, and enhanced customer service. 
 
National Grid is well positioned to deliver a comprehensive solution with mature AMI 
technology,4 which encompasses input from the AMI Collaborative and lessons learned from the 
Company’s pilots and other utilities’ deployment experiences. 
 
1.3 
Proposed AMI Solution  
 
As shown in Figure 1-2 below, National Grid’s service territory spans more than 25,000 non-
contiguous square miles. The service territory supports approximately 1.7 million electric and

640,000 gas metering points, including around 590,000 dual-fuel customers. Customer density 
varies significantly from heavily populated urban areas to isolated rural communities; 
particularly in the northern part of New York including the Adirondack State Park. 
 
 
Figure 1-2: National Grid's Service Territory 
 
1.3.1 Existing Metering Solution and Capabilities 
 
Automated Meter Reading 
National Grid’s service territory primarily uses automated meter reading (“AMR”) technology 
for its electric and gas meters. AMR meters are equipped with a one-way radio frequency (“RF”) 
communication device called an encoder receiver transmitter (“ERT”). A fleet of Company 
service vans is required to drive the entire service territory on a monthly basis to collect customer 
usage information. The electric AMR meters contain either a single or triple ERT configuration; 
each ERT supports the transmission of a single billing determinant. Gas metering technology 
supports the installation of only a single AMR gas ERT module because it requires an integrated 
battery to operate due to the absence of a power source at the meter. With this method of meter 
reading, the Company primarily collects total energy consumption on a monthly basis, as 
compared to the low latency, granular data supported by AMI.  
 
Interval Meter Reading 
Large commercial and industrial customers, who have rates that require interval data collection, 
are processed through the MV-90 system. A combination of phone line, internet protocol and 
cellular communication technologies support the collection of fifteen-minute interval data. By 
December 31, 2019, approximately 3,800 2G interval meters (compatible with 3G network) will 
be upgraded to an AMI 4G cellular solution, due to phase out of Verizon 3G cellular network.  
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 44 of 313


--- Page 80 ---
7 
 
 
The AMI 4G cellular solution was developed for the Clifton Park demonstration project and is 
the metering system the Company plans to migrate existing interval metered customers to over 
time, eventually phasing-out the MV-90 system.   All new commercial and industrial (“C&I”) 
cellular meters are expected to be compatible with the Company’s proposed AMI solution, but 
will not include remote disconnect functionality nor distributed intelligence cards.  
 
Value of Distributed Energy Resources (“VDER”) Metering Solution 
As part of the VDER proceeding (Case 15-E-0751), the Commission is developing a net energy 
metering successor tariff for mass market customers with on-site DER that would be effective 
January 1, 2020. The Company anticipates customers will require an interval metering solution 
to support the net energy metering tariffs defined by the VDER proceeding.  Prior to (and in the 
absence of) approval for the Company to deploy a mesh AMI network across its service territory, 
the Company will utilize its interval cellular AMI solution. This approach will result in higher 
per customer metering costs, as a cellular AMI solution is far costlier on a per customer basis 
than a broad-based mesh AMI solution. Leveraging this solution will allow the Company to 
implement the new VDER rate approach for customers who deploy DER in advance of (or in 
lieu of) the Commission approving the proposed AMI solution.  
 
AMR support of Time-Varying Pricing (“TVP”) 
To support electric TVP rates today, the Company utilizes AMR electric meter triple-ERT 
technology.  The technology can only support the most basic demand and TVP rates, as it is 
limited to providing three consumption period measurements. Although additional triple-ERT 
AMR meters could be installed to support the expansion of TVP, they provide limited pricing 
options, must be manually programmed, and carry a comparable unit cost to AMI meters if 
purchased in similar volumes. On the other hand, AMI meters can be remotely configured for 
any desired pricing design including hourly and critical-peak pricing. Additionally, an AMR 
solution does not provide the broader customer and grid-side benefits of AMI, such as more 
actionable granular customer energy usage data, third-party product and services enablement, 
improved customer service, improved outage management, and DER integration enablement to 
advance the REV objectives. 
 
AMR lifecycle 
The currently install AMR electric meters and gas modules are reaching the end of their twenty-
year useful life. In the absence of the AMI proposal, the Company will begin the required 
replacement of the majority of AMR assets. By fiscal year 2025, approximately 96 percent of 
electric meters and 80 percent of gas modules will have reached the end of useful life. The 
Company’s AMI proposal will avoid the replacement costs of the legacy AMR electric meters 
and gas modules while providing additional grid and customer benefits.5  
 
1.3.2 Alternative AMI solutions 
 
                                                           
5 Avoided AMR costs are discussed in further detail in Section 3.3.1. 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 45 of 313


--- Page 81 ---
8 
 
 
National Grid assessed numerous alternative AMI solutions as part of the AMI business case 
filed with the Company’s June 29, 2016, Initial DSIP.6 The Initial DSIP conducted in-depth 
analysis on three alternative AMI deployment options: 
 
1) Full deployment of both electric AMI meters and gas modules across National Grid’s 
service territory; 
2) Deployment of both electric AMI meters and gas modules across National Grid’s service 
territory in high-density population areas (assumed to be approximately 40% of total 
electric and gas meter points); and 
3) Deployment to customers in National Grid’s service territory who choose to opt-in 
(assumed to be approximately 10% of total electric and gas meter points). 
 
Based on the results of the BCA, the Company proposed full deployment as the recommended 
approach. A full AMI deployment allocates the program across the entire customer base, making 
it the most economical solution on a per meter basis. Mass deployment of AMI facilitates 
numerous customer benefits while providing the functionality required to achieve the REV 
objectives. 
 
A more refined and detailed AMI business case for full deployment was developed and included 
in the Company’s April 28, 2017 rate case filing.7   That business case has been further refined 
and enhanced as a result of party testimony in the rate case and the results of the AMI 
Collaborative. 
 
1.4 
National Grid’s Experience and Learnings 
 
To develop and refine the AMI Business Case, the Company: 1) drew on experiences from its 
Clifton Park Demonstration, as well as its affiliates’ Worcester Smart Energy Solutions Pilot 
(“Worcester Pilot”); 2) leveraged multi-jurisdictional resources and learnings; and 3) participated 
in a productive AMI Collaborative process with Staff and interested parties. 
 
Worcester Pilot and Clifton Park Demonstration 
National Grid’s Massachusetts affiliates developed the Worcester Pilot and the Company 
similarly launched the Clifton Park REV Demonstration Project in New York to better 
understand how to effectively deploy AMI and positively affect customer energy behavior 
changes. 
 
Worcester Pilot 
The Worcester Pilot began in 2013 with informational sessions followed by the deployment of 
AMI, as well as customer-facing technologies. In 2015, the Company introduced an innovative 
rate structure to the pilot participants, consisting of a time-of-use (“TOU”) critical peak pricing 
                                                           
6 Initial Distributed System Implementation Plan (“Initial DSIP”) (filed June 30, 2016, errata filed July 1,

(“CPP”) rate or a peak-time reward (“PTR”).8 The Worcester Pilot’s objective was to empower 
customers to take control of their energy usage and discover savings with new grid technologies 
while retaining customer service quality. A post-deployment assessment by Navigant shows that 
the pilot was successful.9 Table 1-1 reflects the key findings from the Worcester Pilot 
assessment. Additionally, Table 1-2 and Table 1-3 highlight the lessons learned during 
implementation and meter deployment. The Company has considered these lessons in developing 
its proposal for full-scale AMI implementation.

Key Theme 
Related Findings 
Energy and Demand 
Savings for Active 
Customers 
• Load reductions from 4% to 31% (0.12 to 0.60 kW) during CPP Events 
depending on the combination of rate and technology 
• 5.4% (35 kWh per month) weighted average energy savings across the 
technology groups for CPP customers over two years  
Demand Savings for 
Passive Customers 
• Savings increased from 1% to 4% for passive CPP customers and from 2% to 
5% for passive PTR customers 
Enabling Technologies 
Increased Demand Savings 
for Active Customers 
• Customers with programmable communicating thermostats had the highest 
load reductions (25% - 31% on CPP and 22% - 29% on PTR) 
• Customers with in-home displays load reductions were next (17% - 18% on 
CPP and 4% - 9% on PTR), followed by customers with only web portal 
access (12% - 15% on CPP and 10% on PTR) 
Bill Savings 
• Average bill savings of $236 / year over the two years for CPP customers 
• Average total rebates of $30 for Conservation Day Peak Events across both 
summers for PTR customers 
High Retention Rate 
• 98% customer retention rate at the end of 2016 (rates went live Jan. 1, 2015) 
Strong Customer 
Satisfaction 
• 69% of customers rated their satisfaction at least a 5 on a 7-point scale.

Customer Engagement

Tailor user profiles to fit individual users to increase customer participation

Clifton Park Demonstration 
In support of the REV policy objectives as discussed in Section 1.1, the Commission charged the 
Joint Utilities to launch demonstration projects, encouraging each major utility to pair with third 
parties to find ways to bring new value to the market. The Company launched an AMI-enabled 
demand reduction project in Clifton Park to: 1) evaluate demand reduction; 2) provide energy 
information to customers; and 3) animate the clean energy market.  
 
National Grid began AMI deployment in the town of Clifton Park during the first quarter of 
calendar year 2017. The Company installed AMI and Volt-VAR Optimization (“VVO”) 
infrastructure, giving customers actionable insights, tools, and services to incentivize peak 
electric load reductions and reduce overall electric and gas usage. In developing the Clifton Park 
Demonstration approach, the Company leveraged much of the learnings from the Worcester 
Pilot, especially the customer engagement tools and meter deployment communications. 
Although the Worcester Pilot used a small percentage of cellular-enabled AMI meters, the 
Clifton Park Demonstration exclusively used cellular-enabled AMI meters as opposed to RF 
mesh meters and focused on PTR-style incentives.  The Company has gained a number of 
customer engagement insights from the project to date including:  
 
• Communicate early and often; 
• Being present at local community events are great ways to interact with customers to help 
to create community champions to advocate for benefits to reach all customer segments, 
non-profits, schools, community groups, faith communities, community action councils 
(income-eligible services); 
• Utilize personalized messaging for a better customer experience; 
• Communicate options and provide notifications in simple, easy to understand language; 
• Continue to get feedback and measure customer experience (“Listen, Test, Learn”); and 
• Ongoing improvement and refinement is paramount. 
 
As a result of discussions during the AMI Collaborative, the Company is also looking for 
opportunities to transition the Clifton Park Demonstration to focus more on understanding the 
impact of AMI on innovative rate designs. 
 
Multi-Jurisdictional Experience & Savings 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)

11 
 
 
The Company’s affiliates are also pursuing full-scale AMI / advanced metering functionality 
(“AMF”) proposals in both Massachusetts and Rhode Island.10 This affords the Company and its 
customers a potential opportunity to leverage lessons learned from each jurisdiction while also 
capturing multi-jurisdictional cost synergies. This is the advantage of the multi-jurisdictional 
footprint – local utility focus coupled with large utility learnings and cost synergies. 
 
AMI Collaborative Process 
Pursuant to the Company’s 2017 rate case, the Commission approved a Joint Proposal directing 
the Company to initiate an AMI Collaborative.11 The Company launched the Collaborative 
process on April 27, 2018, spanning four months, with numerous stakeholder groups 
participating in eight formal AMI Collaborative large and small group meetings.  Additionally, 
the Company individually met with stakeholders who sought deep-dive discussions on areas of 
interest, such as rate design, pilot projects, cost allocation, as well as a confidential cybersecurity 
briefing. 
 
The collaborative process provided a valuable opportunity to gather and reflect stakeholder input 
in the development of this refined AMI Business Case, as well as the related Customer 
Engagement Plan and BCA.  To that end, the Company sought to drive meaningful discussion 
among the collaborative participants through transparency and responsiveness to stakeholder 
questions. Prompted by collaborative stakeholders, the Company ran multiple BCA scenarios, 
refined benefit and cost assumptions, and developed a robust approach to customer engagement.  
The exchange of ideas that occurred through the AMI Collaborative served to confirm the 
Company’s commitment to deploying AMI as a foundational clean energy investment, while 
also helping the Company refine the Business Case in a number ways, such as:  
 
• Refined benefit and cost assumptions; 
• Development of the Customer Engagement Plan; 
• Drafting a customer-facing Smart Meter Privacy Statement;  
• Removal of fees for AMI late adopters;  
• Modification of on-peak periods associated with the Company’s TOU and CPP 
proposals;  
• Committing to provide a one-year bill guarantee for all customers participating in the 
TVP rate program; 
• A cost allocation methodology; 
• Plans for a mobile sustainability hub to help customers through the deployment and 
enablement phases of customer engagement; and 
• Establishing a date certain (i.e., one year after deployment) by which the Company will 
evaluate a shift to 30-minute data latency.

2.2 
Program Management and Governance  
 
To support the AMI program management and governance process, the Company has defined a 
cross-functional team which includes: 
 
• The New York Jurisdiction 
• Account Maintenance and Operations (“AMO”) 
• Call Center Operations 
• Customer Intelligence/Engagement 
• Customer Metering Services (“CMS”) 
• Information Systems 
• Legal 
• Meter Data Services (“MDS”) 
• Meter Engineering/Lab 
• Pricing 
• Regulatory Strategy 
• Revenue Assurance 
• Telecom Engineering 
 
An AMI program deployment of this magnitude requires a robust management and governance 
model to facilitate program alignment and compliance with project deliverables.  To that end, the 
Company has a designated executive sponsor and will define a project management office 
(“PMO”) core team to mobilize resources and manage the implementation.  The PMO, in 
coordination with the executive sponsor, will ensure that the AMI Steering Committee and senior 
executives, including the Jurisdictional President, are properly briefed on AMI progress and 
involved in key implementation decisions.  Benefits of this management and governance 
framework include: 
 
• A well-defined cross-functional team, bringing potential cost synergies and lessons 
learned from the Company’s affiliates; 
• A core PMO team with an executive sponsor capable of managing all aspects of the AMI 
project while engaging key stakeholders to address implementation obligations; 
• Clear delineation and decision-making authority to drive project direction and to 
establish/approve allowed scope; 
• Defined operational (budget, time, and scope) and procedural (policies, processes, and 
standards) requirements; 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 52 of 313


--- Page 88 ---
15 
 
 
• Issue resolution and risk mitigation, leveraging stakeholder input;  
• Project compliance (e.g., milestones, budget, and scope); and 
• Support for all project administration functions. 
 
The PMO will establish regular program-level and project team meetings, at which execution 
will be reviewed and corrective action taken as required. Additionally, this team will provide 
overall project oversight; advising business units and the New York Jurisdictional team on any 
decisions that have a significant impact on the budget or schedule. 
 
As risk management is a key feature of the governance framework, the PMO team will maintain 
a risk register to provide necessary auditing and oversight of the entire AMI program throughout 
implementation. The risk register will be reviewed with the appropriate business units and the 
New York Jurisdiction on a regular basis. The Company will further establish a meeting cadence 
for reporting program status updates to Staff and the Commission.  To effectively implement the 
AMI program, the Company will undertake the following work streams outlined in Table 2-2: 
 
Table 2-2: AMI Program Management Work Streams 
Work stream 
Description 
Deployment  
Implement supply chain and warehouse activities, including First Article 
Testing; manage the deployment schedule and coordinate crews. 
Program Management 
Establish governance; manage status updates, project planning, resourcing and 
onboarding of additional resources, finances, issues and risk mitigation activities; 
coordinate work streams; lead steering committee and other ad hoc meetings and 
presentations. 
Detailed Design Support 
Develop additional use cases, requirements; generate detailed designs / 
configurations for traceability; plan preliminary testing. 
Business Process 
Develop and establish business processes for primary activities, such as 
deployment planning, customer service management, billing, and outage 
management. 
Change Management 
Track changes (baseline and ongoing), internal progress updates, develop content 
Organization Readiness 
Create job aids, execute roadshow for affected stakeholders, develop an impact 
assessment, facilitate resource changes, onboard new members, train on 
processes, and define procedures. 
Vendor Management 
Schedule development, issue tracking and resolution, risk mitigation, multi-
vendor coordination facilitation (mediation). 
Procurement 
Translating requirements to procurement events; drive vendor questions and 
facilitate vendor demonstrations; negotiate statements of work (“SOWs”),

concerns, etc.), rate analysis, and corporate communications.

2.3 
Vendor Selection and Management  
 
As discussed in Section 2.1, during phase one of the program, the Company will complete 
procurement activities through competitive requests for proposals or solutions (“RFP/RFS”) and 
perform vendor negotiations. This approach enables National Grid to obtain optimal pricing and 
value for customers. The Company will manage and oversee the vendor selection process within 
its governance framework, considering a range of factors, encompassing current and future 
delivery costs, prior industry experience, risk mitigation and reporting protocols. Additionally, 
consideration will be taken as to vendor experience with AMI system deployments, as well as 
large-scale manufacturing capability. 
 
The Company’s vendor selection process typically includes a Request for Information (“RFI”), 
which qualifies potential bidders who can support our defined business requirements. Vendors 
deemed qualified by the RFI were then invited to submit responses for a Request for Solution 
(“RFS”). Once all RFSs have been received, the Company engages each vendor to clarify 
outstanding questions or uncertainties regarding proposed solution, capabilities and pricing. 
Additionally, vendor site visits are performed for research and development facilities and 
manufacturing locations where applicable. To gain additional insight as to vendor capabilities, 
the Company will engage peer utilities who have installed similar software or equipment 
proposed by the vendors. After selection and onboarding, the Company’s Vendor Management 
work stream will coordinate delivery, mitigate risk and identify potential issues and resolution. 
 
Thus far, the Company has commenced an initial vendor selection process as described above, 
referred to as the Group One Procurement Event, in accordance with Figure 2-3. The Group One 
Procurement includes the AMI meters and gas modules, field area network communications 
equipment, head-end and meter data management systems, and associated professional services.  
The Company is currently assessing vendor solutions in response to the Group One RFS. 
National Grid will continue to review and analyze vendor responses, with the goal of selecting a 
vendor, preparing and negotiating contracts for execution in conjunction with the Commission’s 
consideration and approval of the Company’s AMI implementation plan. This work will also 
address diversity meter considerations and may extend into fiscal year 2020. Upon Commission 
approval, the Company will notify all vendors who have responded to the Group One RFS and 
begin the process of onboarding the selected vendor.

Data Privacy Commitment  
The implementation of AMI will generate a significant volume of new meter data with 
information on customer energy usage. The Company is committed to protecting customer data 
and, as such, has developed a thorough Data Privacy Plan as discussed in the Exhibit: Customer 
Engagement Plan, Section 7: Data Access, Sharing, and Privacy to further make clear its actions 
to protect customer data. The Plan involves a three-tiered approach with: 1) a commitment to 
core data privacy policies; 2) regular assessments of the Company’s performance; and 3) 
constant vigilance to protect customer data. National Grid has implemented reasonable physical, 
technical, and administrative security measures to safeguard smart meter energy usage data. 
 
National Grid only collects smart meter energy usage data: 1) as is reasonably required to 
provide utility services to customers; 2) as approved by regulatory agencies; and 3) as required 
by law. The Company does not collect any personally identifiable information (“PII”) through 
customers’ smart meters. Of the approved information, the Company will only collect, process, 
retain, use, and disclose smart meter energy usage data for legitimate National Grid utility-
related business purposes, or as specified by a customer’s express consent to disclose such 
information to a third party (e.g., Green Button Connect My Data or the electronic data 
interchange (“EDI”) used to send information to energy service companies (“ESCOs”)). 
 
Customers must provide consent prior the Company sharing their meter data with third parties. 
Customers are given the opportunity to choose whether to share their smart meter data with third 
parties through the Green Button Connect My Data program, arrangements with an ESCO, or 
some other secure technical or programmatic method that complies with applicable rules and 
regulations. Similarly, the Company retains smart meter data for the period reasonably necessary 
to carry out its business functions and services, unless a longer retention period is required by 
law.     
 
2.5 
Envisioned AMI Customer Benefits 
 
Consumers are enjoying new and higher levels of service and convenience owing to the 
application of advanced technology and big data across industries. Understanding how 
customers’ diverse and evolving needs manifest in their utility experience is the key to 
developing a modern energy experience for our customers. While reliability and affordability 
remain the foundation of the Company’s services, the Company must respond to an expanding 
customer demand for visibility, control, choice and convenience in their energy experience. AMI 
enables a modern customer experience by providing granular, time-variant energy price signals, 
which enables a reduction in energy consumption and usage shift from high cost periods to lower 
cost periods, while also driving system savings.   
 
Customer Benefits: 
 
More actionable information and enhanced energy management  
The deployment of AMI will enable customers to access their energy usage data in near real 
time, with granularity at sub-hourly reading intervals. As part of its proposal, National Grid will 
build and operate a Customer Energy Management Platform, to include an energy monitoring 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 57 of 313


--- Page 93 ---
20 
 
 
portal, which will act as a hub for residential, commercial and industrial customers to view their 
energy usage (i.e., AMI interval data). The Customer Energy Management Platform will also 
unlock new features and enhanced tools for customers to better connect them to personalized 
programs and services, such as the Company’s existing E-Commerce Platform, Solar 
Marketplace, and Dynamic Load Management programs.  
 
New and innovative pricing options 
AMI deployment, coupled with an enhanced customer platform, will enable the Company to 
offer innovative mass-market TVP rates. This will provide customers with pricing incentives to 
manage energy costs, through actions such as reducing energy consumption or shifting energy 
use to lower-cost periods. This has the additional benefit of reducing overall system demand and 
energy costs by displacing peak period consumption for activities such as electric vehicle (“EV”) 
charging. Enhancing customer knowledge and control over energy use and the total energy bill is 
at the heart of REV and next-generation customer engagement. AMI will provide the ability to 
deliver flexible rate options that are unattainable with the Company’s current AMR meters.  
 
Access to third-party services 
Company-developed solutions, tools, and services enabled by AMI must also prioritize third-
party connectivity. By animating the market for new industry participants, third-parties will be 
able to bring additional value to customers through products and services such as home/building 
energy management and smart EV charging. Collaboration between the Company and external 
partners can enable a robust and personalized customer experience. Customers and third-party 
vendors will likewise have increased access to more granular energy data through Green Button 
Connect My Data and home-area network functionality. National Grid has deployed Green 
Button Download My Data for current customers and plans to provide customer-authorized 
Green Button Connect My Data functionality in advance of AMI deployment. 
 
Enhanced customer alerting and personalization tools 
Customer access to timely, granular energy consumption information is an important driver and 
enabler of behavioral changes. Enhanced data can reduce energy consumption, increase energy 
affordability, and reduce bill volatility through services such as high-bill alerts. In addition, the 
Company’s customer service representatives can leverage this data to proactively identify 
relevant programs and offers for customers, enabling more accurate, personalized and actionable 
responses to customer needs.  The Company anticipates that this, in turn, will drive greater 
program participation and improved program outcomes. 
 
Enablement of smart-home devices 
AMI will allow customers to better manage their energy consumption through the use of smart 
devices such as thermostats, water heaters and other appliances that can be integrated with AMI. 
Capabilities enabled through a home-area network, inclusive of home energy management 
systems, will be configurable to send and receive secure communications to and from the 
Company or third-party market entities. With a customer’s authorization, the system can 
automatically adjust energy consumption in response to pricing signals and curtailment events. 
The Company currently executes similar functionality through its Dynamic Load Management 
programs but envisions more advanced capabilities following the deployment of AMI.  
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 58 of 313


--- Page 94 ---
21 
 
 
Easier move in/out process  
AMI provides the ability to connect and disconnect13 electric service remotely and in near real 
time, reducing the need for manual connects and disconnects. This can improve the experience 
for customers when starting and stopping service. 
 
Customer Service Enhancements 
Customer service representatives can use AMI data to enhance customer interactions. For 
example, AMI will: 
• Allow customer service representatives to send a signal to the meter to determine voltage 
levels or whether an outage is due to customer-owned equipment; 
• Allow for real-time reconnects of electric meters; 
• Provide historic information about prior outages and voltages; and 
• Provide for personalized insights, additional rate plans, and other energy management 
options for customers seeking flexibility for their energy management needs. 
 
Outage Management  
AMI has the ability to report a customer outage in near real time, without the need to rely on 
notification from a customer or substation monitoring. The functionality also allows the 
Company to send a signal to AMI meters to identify areas that still require restoration and 
confirm when all outages have been restored. This functionality will improve situational 
awareness contributing to reduced restoration costs and improved outage response. 
 
2.6 
Metrics for Measuring the Success of AMI  
 
AMI will enable both customer and operational benefits as meters are deployed, beginning in 
year three of the AMI program. These benefits will continue to grow as the Company and third-
party market participants leverage the capabilities of AMI. To measure the progress and 
effectiveness of the Company’s planned AMI deployment, the Company proposes the following 
19 customer engagement and operational metrics described in Table 2-4 and Table 2-5 below. 
The proposed metrics consist of eight sub-categories based on the Worcester Pilot and the 
Company’s experience with the Clifton Park Demonstration, along with consideration of the 
Commission’s previously approved AMI metrics from the Con Edison 2017 Rate Order.

3 
AMI Benefits and Costs  
 
The Company utilized feedback from multiple parties, including Staff and engaged stakeholders, 
and conducted various levels of analysis, expanding the detail and comprehensiveness of its 
BCA as part of the collaborative process. This collaboration led to the evaluation of additional 
deployment scenarios, adjustments to a number of BCA costs and benefits, and an improved 
shared understanding of the components of the BCA. 
 
This section presents the approach and results of the BCA and is organized into four parts: 
section 3.1 describes the scenarios that were evaluated; section 3.2 describes and provides the 
basis for the costs of the base proposal scenario and includes cost benchmarks; section 3.3 
describes the base proposal benefits; and section 3.4 presents the BCA scenario results, including 
the BCA test results, key benefit and cost variable sensitivity analysis, and other project 
economic measures.  A more detailed description of the BCA elements and related assumptions 
used in this analysis can be found in the Appendix.  
 
3.1 
BCA Scenarios Evaluated 
 
National Grid has evaluated numerous scenarios to address stakeholder feedback and provide a 
comprehensive assessment of implementation options.  The following variables were considered 
in the development of the AMI deployment scenarios which are described in Table 3-1:  
 
1) Multi-jurisdictional scenarios that reflect the opportunity for cost-sharing between New 
York and its Rhode Island and Massachusetts affiliates; 
2) A range of program deployment durations and BCA terms (e.g., four-year vs. six-year 
deployment, 15-year vs. 20-year net present value (“NPV”)); and 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 61 of 313


--- Page 97 ---
24 
 
 
3) Varying customer participation levels and associated commodity savings based on an opt-
out and opt-in TVP programs as discussed in additional detail in the Customer 
Engagement Plan.14

Multi-jurisdiction AMI Implementation  
The Company’s affiliates in neighboring jurisdictions are in various stages of advancing similar 
AMI business case proposals (referred to as advanced metering functionality or AMF in other 
jurisdictions).  Table 3-2 below provides a brief overview of the last AMI / AMF proposal filed

smart meter proposals in those areas; rather, the table represents a snapshot in time to give a

would create cost synergies from fixed cost sharing opportunities and increased purchasing scale. 
Table 3-3 summarizes the various components where cost synergies can be realized along with 
factors driving the synergy. Multi-jurisdictional scenarios allow the Company to draw upon the 
experiences and synergy savings across its affiliates, while crafting an AMI solution that best 
addresses the needs of its New York customer base.

Duration of Deployment and Program 
The Company has evaluated the impact of a compressed deployment timeline and condensed 
AMI program lifetime, including a six-year and four-year deployment timelines and a fifteen-
year and twenty-year lifetime. For the six-year implementation plan with a twenty-year BCA 
analysis, there would be a two-year pre-deployment period preceding a four-year meter 
deployment phase (as described in section 2.1). For the 4-year implementation plan the meter 
deployment period was reduced from four to two years, while reducing the BCA duration to 
fifteen-years. A reduction in deployment period and BCA duration accelerates programs costs 
while simultaneously reducing benefit accrual.   
  
Customer TVP Program Participation and Savings  
AMI technology will provide more granular energy usage information than what is allowed by 
legacy AMR technologies. This enhanced data is the foundation by which new pricing programs 
can be implemented. Through the provision of granular, time-variant energy price signals, 
customers will have new opportunities to reduce energy consumption and/or shift usage from 
high cost periods to lower cost periods, while also driving system savings. The Company intends 
to deploy TVP rates on an opt-out basis, to customers in conjunction with the AMI program, 
with the expectation that the rate will consist of two pricing components: 1) TOU; and 2) CPP. 
 
Time of Use (“TOU”) 
Supply prices would vary by specific times of the day with on-peak (higher price) and off-peak 
(lower price) periods defined. In response to TOU rates, customers save by reducing 
consumption during higher cost peak periods and/or shifting use from peak to off-peak periods.  
The Company’s proposal includes an on-peak TOU period from 10 am to 9 pm on non-holiday 
weekdays.  
 
Critical Peak Pricing (“CPP”) 
Supply prices would increase further by time of day on a limited number of specific days 
(typically during high demands on the electrical system, where customers are notified in 
advance) designated as CPP events. The Company proposes to use CPP as a means of recovering 
generation capacity costs in the hours that have the greatest need for peak capacity. CPP events 
would be limited to 70 peak hour events during specific hours of certain days, which gives 
customers a greater level of flexibility relative to a set critical peak price period. 
 
Based on learnings from the Worcester Pilot, the Company understands the importance of 
gradual and ongoing customer outreach and education to introduce new concepts and 
technologies. The capabilities enabled by AMF will result in fundamental changes in customer 
access to energy information and their ability to control and manage energy usage. For TVP to 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 64 of 313


--- Page 100 ---
27 
 
 
be effective, customers must receive sufficient information to interpret and act on the new data 
and master the new capabilities that will be available to them. 
 
The Company has proposed an opt-out program for TVP, which affords customers the option to 
abstain from participating in the innovative rate program. The Company also considered an opt-
in scenario, which would afford customers the option to participate in a TVP program. The 
Company calculated estimated energy and capacity savings under each scenario as the mid-point 
energy savings between low and high customer responsiveness to price signals, which provides 
an expected level of customer savings. The assumptions used to project anticipated savings for 
the scenarios are summarized in Table 3-4. 
 
In an opt-out scenario, the Company assumed an 85 percent participation rate in TVP, with 15 
percent customers choosing to opt-out of the program. Whereas, the opt-in scenario projects 20 
percent of customers will enroll in the new rate structure. Achievement of the steady state 
customer savings will be phased in over a number of years, recognizing that as customers 
become familiar with the new TVP program and gain experience, savings are expected to 
increase.

The cost of smart electric meters and natural gas modules, capable of capturing customer usage 
data and other characteristics at defined intervals; their installation; an inventory of meters and 
modules; and the necessary support infrastructure is anticipated to be $289.86 million. 
 
 
.

The communications network equipment, its installation, and the associated backhaul network 
costs for transmitting meter data is projected to be $21.64 million. 
 
.

The cost of project management and ongoing business operations, including equipment and 
installation refresh, CPP and TOU rate implementation, comprehensive customer engagement, is 
estimated at $119.60 million. Project management and customer engagement costs are the largest 
cost elements estimated at $31.65 million, and $26.11, respectively.

The total cost of an IT platform for data collection, monitoring and control of the communication 
system; an expanded cybersecurity system; meter data management system (“MDMS”) and AMI 
head end system (“HES”); an analytics platform to convert raw data into intelligent information 
for use in decision making by customers and the Company; and customer engagement solutions 
is projected to be $226.3 million.

encompassing the electric meters, gas modules, HES, MDMS, FAN equipment, and professional 
support services related to these components.  The Company also leveraged its experience with 
past large-scale meter deployments, as well as Accenture’s consulting experience in this space.

Benchmarking 
National Grid conducted a deployment period nominal cost benchmark analysis comparing 
utilities that have either deployed or filed plans to deploy AMI technology. The results are 
summarized in Table 3-6. National Grid’s costs are at the lower end of the benchmark range and

Utility District.  Factors that may support the lower National Grid costs include dual fuel electric 
meter and gas module installation synergies (94 percent of gas customers are duel fuel), IS 
Infrastructure cost sharing with other projects, and the utilization of IS capabilities already 
developed from the AMI demonstration projects.

3.3.1 Quantified Benefits  
 
Customer Benefits  
AMI data gives customers visibility of their individualized meter data. With AMI, the Company 
can also offer specific energy insights (e.g., opportunities to shift load) based on such data. 
Customers would also have the freedom to change energy consumption behaviors to realize 
greater savings. The customer benefits include savings from: 1) reduced energy loads from 
VVO; 2) customer behavior response to energy insights / high-usage alerts; 3) shifting customer 
consumption behavior to take advantage of the TVP rate structure; and 4) an avoided demand 
cost of customers charging electric vehicles during off-peak periods. For the $281.04 million of 
customer benefits in this AMI Business Case, the energy insights and high-usage alerts, as well 
as TVP are the driving factors behind the benefit with $122.94 million and $137.54 million in 
savings for the customer, respectively. 
 
Avoided O&M Costs 
AMI implementation allows the Company to avoid specific O&M costs, including: 1) AMR 
meter reading vehicles, personnel, and annual software and maintenance; 2) meter investigations; 
3) meter visits to connect and disconnect service; 4) damage claims; 5) outages; 6) the Field 
Collection System (“FCS”) for AMR meter reading; and 7) upgrading the MV-90 interval meter 
system. The total avoided O&M cost benefit is $145.49 million with AMI’s capability to 
remotely connect and disconnect service ($82.60 million) and avoided AMR meter reading costs 
($35.42 million) driving the majority of this benefit. 
 
Avoided AMR Replacement Costs and Avoided Alternative Metering Costs to Support VDER 
In total, the avoided AMR and avoided VDER cost benefits equal $264.40 million. The avoided 
AMR cost line item represents the avoided cost to replace the aging AMR meters and gas 
modules which are approaching end of life, which is primarily driven by the capital costs of the 
AMR meters and gas modules including installation ($240.87 million). Similarly, the avoided 
VDER cost benefit ($10.58 million) represents the avoided incremental cost to implement a more 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 71 of 313


--- Page 107 ---
34 
 
 
costly cellular metering solution as compared to the AMI proposal to compensate customers 
using DER in accordance with the Commission’s VDER Proceeding (Case 15-E-0751). 
 
Societal Benefits  
The Company derived the total societal benefits of $61.94 million from reduced greenhouse gas 
emissions due to AMI-enabled energy conservation and reduced vehicle trips to read meters, 
connect and disconnect service, and investigate service anomalies. The energy insights and TVP 
benefits drive the highest savings of $34.10 million and $22.33 million, respectively.  
 
Revenue Benefits 
The increased frequency of data at shorter time intervals enables the Company to prevent meter 
abuse, such as theft of service and inactive meter consumption, because the Company can 
identify anomalies earlier. The Company estimates revenue benefits of $81.46 million. The cost 
of theft of service and unaccounted for energy results in increased customer rates and is 
considered in the RIM BCA test.    
 
3.3.2 Additional Non-Quantified Benefits 
AMI enhances planning and operational capabilities that support the Company’s role as the DSP 
provider, as reflected in its DSIP filings.  It can also be extended over time to integrate other end-
point devices to provide additional customer value. No costs or benefits have been included in 
the BCA for these future opportunities which are described below.  
 
Enhanced Planning and Operational DSP Capabilities  
As part of the larger REV initiative and in parallel to its AMI planning efforts, National Grid has 
been pursuing a variety of grid modernization technologies.  These efforts include field devices, 
telecommunications, back-office systems and analytics capabilities.  In aggregate, the digital 
investments will help enhance the grid to achieve the following objectives: 
 
1. Create a flexible, adaptable grid to enable new and emerging technologies; 
2. Minimize service disruptions with a resilient system that has the capacity to withstand a 
variety of events;  
3. Optimize grid operations to reduce customer energy consumption without negatively 
impacting customers; 
4. Maintain reliable power delivery while accommodating intermittent resources like DER; 
5. Protect customers’ data and utilities’ systems;  
6. Enable customers and promote customer satisfaction, allowing customers to maintain 
control over their own energy usage; and 
7. Focus on sustainability and integration of clean technologies.15  
 
National Grid’s detailed plan for grid modernization is detailed in separate DSIP filings.  Many 
of the envisioned grid modernization capabilities use remote sensor information to either 
function autonomously or in a remotely coordinated manner, which allows grid operators to 
                                                           
15 See U.S. Department of Energy, Office of Electricity, Delivery, and Energy Reliability, Modern 
Distribution Grid, Volume 1: Customer and State Policy Driven Functionality (March 27, 2017). 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 72 of 313


--- Page 108 ---
35 
 
 
quickly implement changes.  While there are significant benefits when operating in a stand-alone 
manner, integrating AMI data with grid-facing capabilities can produce enhanced functionality.  
The following is a description of the areas where AMI data will enhance grid modernization 
capabilities: 
 
• System Maintenance and Planning – Enhanced data availability for back-office 
analytics on load growth and distribution infrastructure to better understand upgrade 
requirements relative to changing customer usage (e.g., EV adoption, load growth, etc.); 
• Grid Modernization Technologies – A collection of field devices and back-office 
systems focused on “self-healing grid” and non-customer-impacting energy reductions; 
• Outage Management – Capabilities to increase control room situational awareness about 
customer power outages and restorations; and 
• Operations Optimization – Enhanced capabilities to provide operations with new 
options for DER integration and demand response controls. 
 
National Grid believes that many of the capabilities can be combined in various ways for greater 
collective value.  For example, VVO algorithms may be able to achieve greater levels of 
optimization when applied against DER-enhanced load profiles with smart inverters conforming 
to centrally coordinated operational parameters. The specific use cases for AMI enhancements to 
DSIP capabilities are listed and described in the Appendix. 
Integration of Other End-Point Devices 
The AMI communications network and back-office systems can be leveraged over time to 
integrate other end-point sensory devices to provide additional customer value.  A current list of 
future opportunities is included below: 
 
1. Water Utility / Municipality Revenue Opportunities with Joint Use: Water utilities 
could leverage the technical umbrella of National Grid’s proposed infrastructure to 
support overlapping metering efforts, offering a “Metering-as-a-Service” to interested 
jurisdictions. The Company’s platform could serve as the wireless FAN, backhaul, and 
back-office validation systems for a smart water metering capability.  
 
2. AMI for Street Lights and Ancillary Devices: Many metering technology vendors offer 
both lighting control technology applications and metering capabilities for street light 
infrastructure, complementing the other proposed metering capabilities. Instead of using a 
light sensitive photoelectric control that controls the dusk-to-dawn operating schedule, 
the lighting control could incorporate dedicated solid-state AMI chip-meter technology, 
allowing the control device to integrate with the AMI electric metering mesh to transition 
street lighting from an unmetered to a metered billing application while also 
incorporating smart street lighting ability including dimming and enabling smart city 
applications. National Grid’s proposed communications infrastructure and potentially 
back-office systems, could be leveraged to support network services to interested third 
parties. 
 
3. Gas Remote Service Valve: The AMI solution is expected to integrate with gas remote 
service shutoff valves that are in the vendor development phase, allowing the Company 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 73 of 313


--- Page 109 ---
36 
 
 
to remotely disconnect the customer’s service for safety reasons, such as residential 
methane detection alarms, gas leaks, storm hardening, and customer natural gas calls. 
This capability enables improved management of storm restoration with a specific focus 
on the affected customers and regions, leading to efficient service restoration and 
improved customer satisfaction while ensuring the safety and reliability of the system.  
 
4. Residential Methane Detectors: Residential methane detectors (“RMD”) equipped with 
communication devices, also known as smart residential methane devices, are currently 
in research and development for AMI deployment. In the event the smart RMD senses 
methane at a customer location, it would be able to send a notification to the Company 
through a fixed communication network, expediting National Grid’s response even if a 
customer has not called to report the issue.  
 
3.4 
AMI Benefit-Cost Analysis 
 
This section presents the BCA results for the scenarios described in section 3.1 including the 
BCA test results, key benefit and cost variable sensitivity analysis, and other project economic 
measures.  
 
BCA Tests 
AMI is subject to BCA evaluation as set forth in the Company’s BCA Handbook.16 The societal 
cost test (“SCT”), utility cost test (“UCT”), and RIM test help evaluate proposals, such as AMI, 
from alternate viewpoints. Each of the tests attempts to address the complexities involved in 
large-scale investments, offering a unique understanding of how utility expenses translate into 
tangible savings and improvement for all impacted parties. Consistent with the BCA Order, the 
Company relied on the SCT as the basis for presenting the costs and benefits in Section 3 of the 
AMI Business Case.  Each of the tests is described in additional detail below: 
 
1. Societal Cost Test: The SCT considers whether or not the utility, state, or nation is 
better off as a whole (i.e., do the benefits, including externalities, outweigh the costs). 
It counts operational utility benefit, customer benefits, reductions in resource 
requirements (e.g., generation capacity, energy use) and reductions in externalities 
such as carbon emissions as benefits. It does not treat transfers between parties as 
costs.  
 
2. Utility Cost Test: The UCT considers whether or not the cost to the utility/program 
administrator will increase (i.e., is the project self-funding or are additional funds 
needed). The UCT does not include benefits experienced by customers or 
externalities but does include costs such as customer incentives since the utility would 
need to determine a way to fund these programs.

3.4.2 Sensitivity Analysis  
 
The Company performed a sensitivity analysis for the base opt-out and opt-in scenarios to 
identify the cost and benefit variables that have the greatest impact on the BCA results. As seen 
in Figures 3-5 and 3-6, the largest variability exists for both the energy insights and TVP benefits 
as the benefit relies heavily on customer adoption. This analysis visualizes the extremes of 
customer responsiveness, representing the lowest and highest ranges of customer benefit 
realization. Likewise, the VDER benefit has a significant potential positive BCA impact, as the 
Company has taken a conservative approach to the solar adoption forecasts in comparison to 
what may be required to achieve the State’s clean energy goals.

1 
AMI Benefit – Cost Analysis Methodology 
 
BCA Tests 
 
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid’s (“National Grid” or the “Company”) 
advanced metering infrastructure (“AMI”) is subject to a benefit-cost analysis (“BCA”) 
evaluation as set forth in the Company’s BCA Handbook.1 The SCT, UCT, and RIM tests 
(described below) help evaluate proposals, such as AMI, from alternate viewpoints. Each of the 
tests attempts to address the complexities involved in large-scale investments, offering a unique 
understanding of how utility expenses translate into tangible savings and improvement for 
affected parties. Consistent with the BCA Order, the Company used the SCT as the basis for 
presenting the costs and benefits in Section 3 of the AMI Business Case.  Each of the tests is 
described in additional detail below: 
 
1. Societal Cost Test (“SCT”): The SCT considers whether or not the utility, state, or 
nation is better off as a whole (i.e., do the benefits, including externalities, outweigh 
the costs). It counts operational utility benefit, customer benefits, reductions in 
resource requirements (e.g., generation capacity, energy use) and reductions in 
externalities such as carbon emissions as benefits. It does not treat transfers between 
parties as costs.  
 
2. Utility Cost Test (“UCT”): The UCT considers whether or not the cost to the 
utility/program administrator will increase (i.e., is the project self-funding or are 
additional funds needed). The UCT does not include benefits experienced by 
customers or externalities but does include costs such as customer incentives since the 
utility would need to determine a way to fund these programs. 
 
3. Rate Impact Measure (“RIM”): The primary purpose of the RIM test is to provide 
an indication of how AMI will affect customer rates, so it measures whether or not 
volumetric utility rates will increase. 
  
 
Table 1-1 and Table 1-2 below summarize the various AMI benefits and costs that are included 
in each of the BCA test perspectives.

2.0 Benefit-Cost Analysis (“BCA”) Handbook (Filed July 31, 2018) (the “BCA Handbook”).

6 
 
Discount Rates  
 
The present value of costs and benefits are discounted using a pre-tax weighted average cost of 
capital (“WACC”) of 7.99 percent, which equates to a post-tax WACC of 6.45 percent.  The 
WACC assumptions are based on the Commission-approved WACC for fiscal year 2021, the last 
year of the Company’s rate plan, which is also consistent with past practice and the Company’s 
deployment schedule, because no plant closings are forecasted prior to fiscal year 2022.  The 
pre-tax WACC (7.99 percent) is used for the UCT and RIM, whereas the after-tax WACC (6.45 
percent) is used for the SCT, as taxes are considered income transfers and excluded from the 
societal test. 
 
2 
Benefit – Cost Analysis  
 
The Company’s base scenario for presenting BCA results is defined as a New York-only, six-
year deployment, 20-year NPV term, with an opt-out time-varying pricing (“TVP”) scenario. 
The opt-out program will educate customers on the new default time varying pricing including 
the process to opt-out of the program if a customer decides they do not want to participate 
initially or after being on the program for a period of time. Additionally, an opt-in scenario was 
evaluated, which requires all customers to affirmatively enroll in the TVP program. Table 2-1 
provides the costs and benefits for the base scenario and Opt-In pricing scenarios, with Table 2-2 
summarizing the full BCA test results.  
 
The Company’s benefit-cost analysis produced positive test results across both our Opt-Out and 
Opt-in scenarios. The higher BCA ratio summarized in the opt-out analysis is consistent with the 
belief that TVP opt-out deployment will drive stronger customer benefits. A final BCA ratio of 
1.15 demonstrates AMI is a valuable investment in New York’s infrastructure while providing 
the ability to align customer energy savings with clean energy goals.

2.1.2 Alternate Deployment Timeline 
The Company’s base scenario for presenting BCA results is defined as a six-year deployment 
with a 20-year NPV term. Additional analysis was performed to identify both the cost and 
benefit impacts of a 4-year implementation plan, where the meter deployment period was 
reduced from four to two years, along with shortening the BCA term to 15 years. The expedited 
deployment schedule and shorter BCA term reduced NPV costs by $41 million while decreasing 
benefits by $121 million as compared to the base scenario.  Customer benefits were impacted 
significantly by the shorter BCA term, reduced by $82 million, as these benefits are projected to 
increase over time with rising levels of customer engagement.   The BCA results are summarized 
in Table 2-6 below.

2.1.3 BCA Enhancements 
In support of the Company’s rebuttal testimony of the Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel in 
the Company’s 2017 Rate Case, an updated BCA model was submitted which incorporated 
various Staff AMI Panel recommendations. Since that filing additional enhancements have been 
made to the BCA model as a result of a detailed request for solution (RFS) procurement and the 
AMI collaborative.  
 
Request for Solution (RFS) 
The Company has commenced an initial vendor selection process referred to as the Group One 
Procurement Event. The Group One Procurement includes the AMI meters and gas modules, 
field area network communications equipment, head-end and meter data management systems, 
and associated professional services.  The Company has included updated costs from the RFS in 
its projection of AMI costs.  The Company will continue to review and analyze vendor 
responses, with the goal of selecting a vendor, preparing and negotiating contracts for execution 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 88 of 313


--- Page 124 ---
11 
 
in conjunction with the Commission’s consideration and approval of the Company’s AMI 
implementation plan.  
 
AMI Collaborative Process 
Pursuant to the Company’s 2017 rate case, the Commission approved a Joint Proposal directing 
the Company to initiate an AMI Collaborative.2 The Company launched the “AMI 
Collaborative” on April 27, 2018, spanning four months, with fifteen stakeholder groups 
participating in eight formal AMI Collaborative large and small group meetings.  Additionally, 
the Company individually met with stakeholders who sought deep-dive discussions on areas of 
interest, such as rate design, pilot projects, cost allocation, as well as a confidential cybersecurity 
briefing 
 
The collaborative process provided a valuable opportunity to gather and reflect stakeholder input 
in the development of a refined AMI Business Case, Customer Engagement Plan, and benefit-
cost analysis.  As a result of collaborative stakeholder inputs, the Company ran multiple BCA 
scenarios, refined its BCA benefits and costs.

3.1.1 AMI Electric Meter Equipment and Installation  
 
An AMI meter is an electronic device used to measure electricity consumption at residential, 
commercial, and industrial locations, communicating interval data using a two-way 
telecommunications infrastructure. These devices can be equipped to leverage a cellular radio, 
power line communication (“PLC”) or a radio frequency (“RF”) mesh network to interface with 
the utility backhaul and back-office systems. 
 
The AMI electric meters support the following functionality: 
 
Table 3-3: AMI Electric Meter Capabilities 
Capability 
Description 
Flexible Two-Way 
Communications 
Execute all supported meter reading, configuration update and firmware 
download functionality; customize targeted meter firmware updates; support on-
demand readings from the meter. 
Upgradable Firmware 
Customize firmware upgrades with the ability to automatically roll-back if 
activation fails; create multiple firmware images including primary and pending. 
Bi-Directional Metering 
Store received and delivered data metrics in the meter; support customers who 
own renewable energy facilities or participate in vehicle to grid systems with 
real-time data being sent back to the utility. 
Energy Quantities 
Wh Delivered, received, Net and Uni-Direction; VARh Delivered and Received; 
VARh Q1-Q4; VAh Delivered, Received and Net. 
Demand Measurements 
Max Watts delivered, received; Max VA Delivered, Received; Max VAR 
Delivered, Received; Max VAR Q1, Q2, Q3, Q4; Min Power Factor. 
Enhanced Meter Reading 
Receive and transmit meter billing data including interval data, register reads; 
transmit recorded events and exceptions with each interval to the head-end 
software, which interprets them and logs appropriate messages (such as time 
adjustments) 
Real-Time Meter Event 
Automated alarms received by the head-end system via e-mail to a specific user 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 92 of 313


--- Page 128 ---
15 
 
Capability 
Description 
and Alarm Retrieval 
or group of users. 
Tamper Detection 
Detect and report exceptions for events such as magnetic fraud attacks; 
communicate tamper indications in real time through the system. 
Remote Disconnect/ 
Reconnect 
Support integrated disconnect switch; perform remote disconnects/reconnects 
through the Operations Center. 
Integration & Installation 
Fully integrated solution under-the-cover allows for plug and play installation in 
the field; shipped from the factory as one complete unit, ready for field 
deployment. 
Meter Security 
Platform security with an encrypted file system and secure boot; standard DLMS 
Security; Application Layer Enhanced Security; Local Access Signed 
Authorization. 
Adaptive Communications 
Support both RF and PLC for “last mile” communication to the meters via the 
Mesh; support standards based, true mesh communication where each meter is 
assigned a global routable address; RF links implement standard 4g/e; meters 
dynamically select the optimal link based on channel conditions and the target 
QoS; mesh network uses adaptation layers and RPL as a mesh routing protocol; 
embedded Wi-Fi communications for local access using common security model 
with network communications. 
Radio Specifications 
Radio Output Power; configured at time of manufacture: – 500mW-1W. 
Network Communications 
Possesses the ability to communicate with and operate within Home Area 
Network (HAN) and Business Area Network (BAN) technologies. 
 
Cost Components 
This cost element includes: 1) AMI electric meter equipment cost; 2) AMI electric meter 
installation expenditures; 3) AMI demonstration period; and 4) AMI electric meter cost of 
removal. 
(1) AMI electric meter equipment cost 
The Electric AMI meter will have technologies that measure interval consumption, advanced 
telecommunications, solid-state memory, processing allowance for firmware upgrades, 
consumption recording and remote ping support. Additionally, sensors for power quality 
measurement such as last gasp notifications, voltage violations, autonomous algorithms for 
abnormal operation to identify tamper detection and the ability to remotely connect and/or 
disconnect electrical service for customers are included. 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 93 of 313


--- Page 129 ---
16 
 
Figure 3-2: AMI Electric Meter 
 
Through a request for solution (RFS), the Company has defined electric meter costs for 
residential and commercial and industrial (C&I) customers. The Company assumes an AMI 
meter opt-out rate of one percent; for customers who opt out of AMI, the Company will install a 
manual meter, requiring a physical visit by the Company to read the meter. Additionally, this 
element also includes an eight percent New York state sales tax. 
 (2) AMI electric meter installation expenditures 
In addition to purchasing electric meters, costs will be incurred associated with meter 
installation. The Company has defined distinct installation costs for both electric only and dual-
fuel customers. Electric AMI meter installation costs include travel time, vehicle costs, a blended 
labor rate across regular time and overtime while accounting for productive, unproductive and 
unable to complete time. Additionally, labor synergies were defined for dual-fuel customers, 
where both an AMI electric meter and a new gas module will be installed. Only the electric 
portion of the dual-fuel installation cost is defined in this element. 
 
(3) AMI demonstration period 
The Company has defined an AMI demonstration period, which is in line with the practices of 
peer utilities across the country. During the pilot period, the Company can establish processes 
and demonstrate technical capabilities, while enhancing the AMI program on a smaller volume 
of meters to maximize customer experiences. This will provide the tools to build robust business 
processes, ensuring staff are prepared to perform the expected business functions on a larger 
scale.  
 
 
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 94 of 313


--- Page 130 ---
17 
 
(4) AMI electric meter installation COR portion 
The final element of this category is the cost of removal of the AMR meters. As the AMR 
solution is being retired, there are costs encompassing the removal and disposal of legacy 
metering devices. 
 
3.1.2 AMI Gas Module Equipment and Installation  
 
New gas meter modules, which are RF communication devices capable of securely and 
efficiently transmitting data packets, will be installed on all existing gas meters. Additionally, it 
is anticipated ten percent of the meter population will require replacement to accommodate 
module installation. The scope of this program does not include the programmatic replacement 
of gas regulators or meters, which will continue to be replaced per current O&M schedules. 
 
The AMI Gas module supports the following functionality: 
 
Table 3-4: AMI Gas Module Meter Capabilities 
Capability 
Description 
Continuous  
Continually stores and updates the last 40 days of hourly interval data which can 
be read via handheld, mobile and fixed network. 
Bubble-up Mode Operation 
Operates in bubble-up mode and does not require a license from the Federal 
Communications Commission (FCC). 
Low O&M Costs 
Designed for a 20-year battery life regardless of data collection solution to 
ensure low operating and maintenance costs. 
Easy Installation 
Module design makes installation fast and easy, especially when gas is flowing 
through the meter. 
Communication with Gas 
Devices 
Ability to communicate with other gas devices (e.g., methane detectors). 
Compact Design 
The compact design and direct engagement with the meter drive assure the 
unparalleled accuracy that makes gas modules the industry standard. 
Tamper Detection 
The two-way 100G/500G DLN offers improved tilt tamper detection. 
 
Cost Components 
The AMI gas module and meter equipment and installation consists of six cost elements: 1) the 
AMI gas module equipment; 2) the installation of the AMI gas module, 3) the cost of removal of 
the AMI gas module; 4) replacement gas meters; 5) and the installation of replacement gas 
meters; and 6) the cost of removal of the gas meter 
  
(1) AMI Gas Module Equipment 
The AMI gas module equipment element identifies the cost of the communications module 
attached to the gas meter. The cost of the gas meter is calculated separately, as only a small 
portion of the gas meter population will require replacement. 
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 95 of 313
