Figure 3-3: AMI Gas Module 
 
This module natively includes: 
• 
technologies to measure interval consumption;  
• 
telecommunications to interface with an Advanced Metering network; and 
• 
solid-state memory and processing, allowing for firmware upgrades, consumption 
recording, and ping support. 
 
(2) Expenditures for the installation of the AMI gas module 
The installation of gas modules captures the labor and vehicle costs associated with the 
installation of the communication module. The Company has defined distinct installation costs 
for both gas only and dual-fuel customers. Gas AMI module installation costs include travel 
time, vehicle costs, a blended labor rate across regular time and overtime, while accounting for 
productive, unproductive and unable to complete time. Additionally, labor synergies were 
defined for dual-fuel customers, where both an AMI electric meter and a new gas module will be 
installed. Only the gas portion of the dual-fuel installation cost is defined in this element. 
 
(3) Cost of Removal of the AMI Gas Module 
Similar to the cost of removal of electric meters, this cost element captures the cost of removal of 
the existing gas module.  
 
(4) AMI Gas Meter Equipment 
This cost element includes the cost of replacing the gas meter. In some instances, the gas module 
may not be compatible with existing gas meters, so the meter must be replaced. The Company 
has projected the replacement of ten percent of the gas meters during AMI implementation.  
 
(5) Gas-Meter-Related Installation Cost 
The installation of gas meters captures the labor and vehicle costs associated with the installation 
of the physical meter. The Company has defined distinct installation costs for both gas only and 
dual-fuel customers. Gas meter installation costs include travel time, vehicle costs, a blended 
labor rate across regular time and overtime, while accounting for productive, unproductive and 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 96 of 313


--- Page 132 ---
19 
 
unable to complete time. Additionally, labor synergies were defined for dual-fuel customers, 
where both an AMI electric meter and a new gas meter will be installed. Only the gas portion of 
the dual-fuel installation cost is defined in this element. 
 
(6) Cost of Removal of Gas Meters  
Similar to the cost of removal of gas modules, as discussed above, this cost element captures the 
cost of removal of replaced gas meters. 
 
3.1.3 AMI Inventory  
 
National Grid will maintain an inventory of AMI electric meters and Gas modules to support 
ongoing day-to-day operations. This inventory will be phased in, consistent with the AMI meter 
deployment schedule, and placed in warehouses and local operating areas for use. The inventory 
level is assumed to be 2.5% consistent with existing inventory levels. 
 
3.1.4 Support Infrastructure 
 
National Grid will augment the existing support infrastructure to efficiently deploy AMI meters, 
while minimizing unnecessary costs and impacts to the customer. Specifically, the support 
infrastructure includes: 1) increased field workers and facility space for meter deployment; 2) 
back office and clerical support to manage the increased activity; and 3) labor to conduct a 
quality assurance check once the meter is installed. 
 
Cost Components 
The support infrastructure cost components consist of 9 elements: 1) the CMS deployment center 
facility; 2) the total CMS back office and clerical; 3) total service representative tools and 
uniforms; 4) total installed meter quality assurance / quality check; 5) total CMS deployment 
coordination labor; 6) total CMS cellular communication; 7) CMS field installer initial training; 
8) handheld devices; and 9) MDS system development testing. 
 
(1) Total CMS Deployment Center – Facility Cost 
Deployment of AMI meters will require the coordination of a large number of personnel, 
dispatch activities, new meter staging, CGR staging and disposal of removed legacy AMR 
devices. While costs are sought to be minimized through “just-in-time” equipment deliveries, 
some additional facility costs will be incurred. 
 
(2) Total CMS Back Office & Clerical cost  
AMI will require supplemental staff to support field personnel, back office activities and clerical 
functions associated with meter deployment. While existing staff will assist with these efforts, 
insufficient bandwidth exists for the increased volume of activity during deployment; additional 
staff is estimated for these functions. 
 
 
 
 
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 97 of 313


--- Page 133 ---
20 
 
(3) Total Service Representative Tools / Uniform cost  
Staff deploying Advance Meters will require field support tools during installation activities and 
company uniforms, which are required for all meter deployment personnel to ensure proper 
identification.  
 
(4) Total Installed Meter Quality Assurance / Quality Check cost 
Once AMI meters have been deployed, efforts are undertaken from the back office to ping 
meters and ensure that the deployment was performed correctly. This quality control check 
confirms that meters can communicate with central systems and are correctly installed. These 
quality assurance labor cost estimates are only allocated during the meter deployment period. 
 
(5) Total CMS Deployment Coordination Labor cost 
Due to the complexity of the deployment, AMI field installation personnel will be supported by a 
chief foreman associated with each region. The foremen will assist both supervisors and clerical 
staff in both installation and operations management.  
 
(6) CMS Field Installer Initial Training 
AMI field installation personnel will require training prior to meter deployment. This training 
cost will occur during both pre-deployment and during deployment expansion, to ensure all 
personnel are properly trained. 
 
(7) Total CMS Cellular Communication Cost 
Staff deploying Advance Meters will require cell phones to communicate with their peers, back 
office support and local authorities if safety concerns should arise. 
 
(8) Handheld Devices Cost 
Handheld devices are required to support meter installation activities. This item captures the cost 
of handheld device that can interface with the chosen vendor’s meter asset management system. 
These devices will have the capability to manage installation and capture meter information such 
as serial number, GPS coordinates and premise address.  
 
(9) MDS system development testing 
During systems implementation, the AMI project will leverage Meter Data Services staff to 
assist in reviewing configurations, testing deployed back office integrations and validating 
overall system components and processes. 
 
3.2 
Communication Network Equipment and Installation 
 
The Company will utilize communications technology to create a strong, secure mesh network, 
ensuring mitigation of obstacles such as high-rise buildings, changing RF conditions, 
underground meter vaults and low-density environments.

manage mass data collection, while relay routers extend the range of meter communications. 
Collectors and routers have several characteristics that enable communications efficiency and 
effectiveness as seen in Table 3-6. 
 
Table 3-6: Characteristics of Collectors and Routers 
Characteristic 
Description 
Dynamic Replies 
The network can rearrange itself dynamically to maintain the most efficient 
communications pathways across seasons, varying weather conditions and 
vegetation cycles 
Power Outage Response  
In the event of a power outage, the field area network (FAN) comprised of meters, 
routers and collectors will stay up long enough to transmit power-off notifications to 
back-office systems 
Sub-System Support 
Devices / Antennas / Sensors 
 
When possible, the electric meter will also serve as the communications platform for the gas 
module. This will enable communication between the gas modules and the back-office systems, 
while minimizing the impact to the gas module battery life. 
 
Cellular Radio 
National Grid will utilize cellular radio communication when a RF mesh network is not a viable 
solution. In areas where population density or service territory topology is insufficient to support 
a mesh network or the Company will instead leverage a cellular radio module. The Company 
assumed up to five percent of devices will leverage cellular communication.  
 
Data Latency 
Figure 3-5 depicts the flow of the AMI data. The smart meter will collect electric and gas raw 
usage data at fifteen-minute and one-hour intervals, respectively. Then, with the help of relay 
devices where needed, that raw data travels to the collector at specific intervals; every four hours 
for electric data and every eight hours for gas data. From the collectors, the information is 
transmitted over backhaul communications to the Company Operations Center.  The raw data 
will then be made available to customers and third-parties through the Company’s web site and 
green button download my data and connect my data applications.   
 
Therefore, under the Company’s program, electric customers will have access to their raw usage 
data within four hours after the data interval. Gas customers will have access to this raw usage 
information within eight hours due to battery limitations. In both cases, customers will have bill 
quality or validated, estimated and edited (VEE) data within 24 hours of a given interval which is 
the general industry standard for AMI implementations in the United States. As a result of the 
Stakeholder Collaborative the Company has committed to reassess data latency one year after 
deployment to determine whether to move electric raw data collection every 30 minutes. 
 
The electric meters will also include functionality to communicate usage data directly to a home 
area or business area network providing additional options for customers and third-party service 
providers looking for real time data access.

(2) Network communications equipment for electric and gas meters 
Advanced meters communicate with each other through mesh technologies for local 
communications but rely on more robust communications equipment for backhaul to back-office 
systems. Network collectors and routers can aggregate data from the metering mesh and convey 
pertinent data through the communications backhaul. This cost component considers the cost of 
collector and relay router units to support advanced metering communication for gas modules 
and electric meters.  
 
 (3) Network communications installation cost for electric and gas meters 
This cost element considers the installation costs for personnel to properly configure and install 
each device. This cost calculation multiplies the number of collector and router units required to 
support the expected quantity of electric meters and gas modules by the cost of the unit 
installation. 
 
(6) Network Communications Equipment Cost Upgrade 
This cost component considers a one-time communications equipment upgrade to support 
advances in the electric and gas metering and includes the associated equipment and installation 
cost for the upgrade. This cost includes the router relay and collector equipment and installation 
costs for electric and gas.  The Company assumes an approximate 10-year lifecycle for the 
communications equipment.  
 
3.2.2 Communication Network Installation Management 
 
During this network installation and meter deployment phase, the AMI program management 
team will pair internal Company resources with meter vendor resources to manage and 
coordinate the communications infrastructure, meter deployments and initial stabilizations as 
appropriate. The paired resources will also be responsible for troubleshooting any meter-related 
issues that occur during this phase. Once the meter deployment phase is complete, the 
appropriate departments will permanently assume these responsibilities. 
 
Cost Components 
 
The communication network installation management cost element includes: 1) the field 
deployment support work stream; 2) make ready cost; and 3) AMI communications equipment 
software maintenance. 
 
(1) Professional Services – Field Deployment Support Work Stream 
This cost element covers resources that will oversee meter and field area network deployment, as 
well as assist in determining the best location for router relay and collector units. The specific 
resources included in this cost element are an installation manager with technical team and field 
engineering support.

(2) Make Ready Cost for Network Communications 
The network communication equipment may require additional efforts to facilitate the

(3) AMI Communications Equipment Software Maintenance 
This cost element captures the fee for the maintenance on the communications equipment 
software, such as patches, firmware upgrades and customer support. The software vendor 
charges a percentage of the fee associated with the software on a go-forward basis.  
 
 
3.2.3 Backhaul 
 
The backhaul network is a wide area network (“WAN”) that is the high-speed, high-bandwidth 
communications structure between the collectors and the AMI Head-End. This network can 
either be public or private depending on several factors, including cost (both upfront and 
reoccurring), security, meter density and proximity to existing fiber networks. 
  
Regarding private communication, National Grid has a SONET fiber communications system 
that ties a number of transmission substations and other corporate facilities together. In some 
instances, distribution-level substations also leverage this network to send operational data to our 
corporate facilities. In addition to fiber optic systems, the Company operates many licensed and 
unlicensed microwave point-to-point links that provide backhaul connectivity for multiple 
operational and corporate systems. 
 
AMI collectors will backhaul their data utilizing 4G cellular public networks or company private 
networks when located at substations or other company facilities with private network 
connectivity. 
 
Cost Component 
The backhaul cost component is the annual service fee to operate each FAN unit, allowing it to 
communicate with the public cellular backhaul.

Meter Data Management System; 2) enhancements to the Customer Service System (“CSS”); 3) 
Customer Engagement Products and Services; 4) IS Infrastructure; and 5) Cyber Security.

3.3.1 AMI Head-End and Meter Data Management Systems 
 
An AMI platform requires both a Head-End and meter data management system (MDMS) to 
facilitate the collection, storage, validation and processing of meter data to and from back-office 
systems.  
 
The AMI Head-End (“HES”) is the communication, command and control system which 
interfaces the field communications infrastructure to the back-office systems. Serving as the 
main point of data collection and disbursement, the HES facilitates data transmission, bi-
directionally, to and from meters. The AMI Head-End processes meter data, readings and events, 
while supporting operational functions such firmware updates across the network. For electrical 
systems, it can ping individual meters and remotely initiate the connection and disconnection of 
the meter.  
 
The MDMS processes meter data delivered from the HES and also provides meter data storage 
and archival capabilities. During processing, the MDSM will validate, edit and estimate (“VEE”) 
raw meter reading data to identify any anomalies. For example, if incoming usage data is higher 
or lower than an expected range, the data will fail validation, resulting in further data analysis. 
Similarly, the system will also flag any data intervals with incomplete or missing data and can 
populate estimated intervals. If estimated, data will be updated once additional data is received or 
the original data can be validated. Back-office systems can leverage VEE data for billing, 
customer service and data analytics. 
 
Cost Components 
The AMI Head-End and Meter Data Management System cost element includes: 1) solution 
program management; 2) the systems implementation work stream; 3) software setup fees; 4) 
software fees for HES and MDMS software; and 5) system and meter firmware upgrades. 
(1) Professional Services – Solution Program Management  
This cost element covers program management resources associated with the technical solution. 
Resources included in this cost element are a solution architect, security specialists, project 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1

management and business readiness support. This cost element also covers continuous 
management across work streams, as well as coordination with all National Grid program 
management processes. 
 
(2) Professional Services –Systems Implementation Work stream  
This cost element covers the systems implementation resources supporting systems project 
management and business/technology consulting, encompassing the testing and integration of the 
software solution.  
 
(3) Software Setup Fees – One Time Setup  
This element covers the initial cost to procure the software solution from the selected AMI 
vendor. The solution components would include HES, MDMS, network management (“NMS”) 
and field deployment software fees. 
 
(4) Software Fees – Head-End Software  
This cost element covers the annual per meter vendor fees associated with the software solution. 
The solution components would include ongoing costs to support the HES, MDMS, NMS, and 
field deployment software. 
 
(5) Professional Services – System and Meter Firmware Upgrade cost 
This element covers a one-time cost to upgrade key meter firmware capabilities. 
 
3.3.2 Customer Service System 
 
The CSS is a set of applications utilized to manage customer-facing activities. This set of 
programs leverages meter data to perform billing and payment processing, collections, rebates 
and discounts for EE and DR. As part of the AMI deployment, CSS will be modified to interface 
and support the chosen AMI solution. As CSS is the billing interface for National Grid’s 
customers, these added capabilities will be critical to maintain customer satisfaction. Moreover, 
as DER penetration increases, CSS must be adaptable to support the dynamic energy 
environment. 
 
CSS will also provide a more personalized service to foster the customer relationship and assist 
in customer retention. Using a variety of back-office IS sources, the system can help facilitate a 
more personalized customer engagement. For instance, when a customer calls in to the call 
center, CSS call center operators can leverage customer history and real-time meter information, 
giving National Grid employees greater insights to help customers. Service representatives will 
also have a new suite of tools at their fingertips to perform diagnostic services, meter pinging 
and remote connection for electric customers initiating new service or restoring service 
disconnected for non-payment or seasonal usage. 
 
Cost Components 
The Customer Service System cost element includes: 1) CSS enhancements CapEx; 2) CSS 
enhancements OpEx; 3) CSS enhancements and run-the-business (“RTB”) costs; 4) CSS 
enhancements CapEx hardware.

3.3.3 Customer Engagement Products and Services 
 
For the customer to realize the benefits of smart meter technology, the Company recognizes the 
importance of pairing this technology with proactive customer and market engagement 
initiatives. As part of the AMI deployment, National Grid will develop and implement a 
Customer Energy Management Platform. The AMI benefit cost analysis includes the cost of 
these solutions.  
 
• The Customer Energy Management Platform will act as a hub for residential, 
commercial, and industrial customers to view their energy usage, including AMI meter 
interval data. This platform will allow customers to view raw data representing their 
electric and consumption within, four and eight hours respectively, of the end of a given 
billing interval, with the ability to view billing quality data within 24 hours. Access to 
this data will enable customers to make better informed decisions about how they use 
energy. The Platform will power customer choice, giving customers the option to enroll 
in programs that can leverage the more granular AMI-provided data. These include EE, 
DR, and other pricing programs. Customers can also access educational and safety 
information, material on energy efficient consumer products, and analysis on home 
energy usage. The platform will integrate with smartphone applications, allowing 
customers to access their data while on the go and to create customizable alerts, notifying 
them of grid conditions (including outages, reductions or curtailments), unusual usage, 
and bill pay. 
 
 
Cost Components 
The Customer Engagement Products and Services cost element includes: (1) Customer Energy 
Management Platform OpEx and RTB cost.

3.3.4 IS Infrastructure 
 
The IS infrastructure includes four elements: 1) improved telecommunications; 2) enterprise 
service bus (“ESB”); 3) information management and advanced analytics; and 4) cloud 
computing and data lake.  
 
For the telecommunications cost, the Company must enhance and upgrade existing backhaul 
capabilities to support the required bandwidth for the data transfers. Similarly, the ESB must be 
modified to support AMI data streams while interfacing new and legacy corporate systems.  
 
Costs in the information management and advanced analytics category must account for the 
configuration and deployment of data quality and analytic capabilities. This will allow for the 
analysis of the data gathered from existing and third-party data sources to provide valuable 
output, reflecting current state as well as predictive and prescriptive outcomes. 
 
Cloud computing and data lake captures the cost of setting up a cloud data lake environment. 
Rather than hosting these data management capabilities on servers within National Grid data 
centers, outsourcing this function results in greater cost efficiencies, redundancies, and security 
protocols.  
   
Cost Components 
The IS infrastructure includes: 1) telecom CapEx software and hardware, OpEx, RTB; 2) ESB 
CapEx, OpEx, RTB, and CapEx hardware; 3) Information Management CapEx software, OpEx, 
RTB Cost, and CapEx hardware; 4) Data Lake CapEx software, OpEx and RTB, and CapEx 
hardware, (5) Licensed Infrastructure CapEx, OpEx, and RTB, (6) Meter Inventory Management 
Upgrade, and (8) Load Disaggregation Software cost. 
 
(1) Telecom CAPEX for software and hardware, OPEX, RTB 
In preparation to align with REV objectives, National Grid is enhancing several of its 
telecommunication capabilities to support AMI as well as initiatives such as ADMS and 
substation automation. These enhancements will require the installation of a new backhaul with 
enhanced bandwidth to support transfer of the new data.  
These cost elements represent the telecommunications capital and operating expenses, including 
the run the business costs, attributable to AMI.  
(2) ESB CAPEX for software and hardware, OPEX, RTB 
National Grid will implement a dedicated ESB to move data between systems, automate and 
manage business processes and enable real-time and batch integration. Additionally, to 
implement several of the AMI use cases, the ESB must communicate with legacy systems that 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 108 of 313


--- Page 144 ---
31 
 
currently use a corporate ESB. This element reflects the capital costs, operating expenses, run-
the-business costs associated with the ESB attributable to AMI.  
 (3) Information Management CAPEX for software and hardware, OPEX, RTB 
The Company will use the data to identify trends and other insights which could indicate 
potential areas to create value for both the customers and National Grid. This value is flexible 
and can take the form of customer satisfaction, expense reduction, etc. In some cases, algorithms 
to process this data may come in pre-packaged software suites; in other cases, the Company can 
pursue proprietary National Grid-specific approaches. This cost element reflects the capital costs, 
operating expenses, and run-the-business costs associated with Information Management.  
 (4) Data Lake CAPEX for software and hardware, OPEX, RTB  
The Company will set up and establish a data lake with a scalable enterprise data warehouse. 
This data will support the information management and advanced capabilities and provide a 
centralized data repository for AMI data. The data lake is assumed to be hosted in a cloud 
environment. 
 
The benefits of a cloud environment are: 
• reduced provisioning time of computing resources through administered governance,  
• quicker delivery of applications and business capabilities, 
• ability to dynamically scale/flex computing resources to meet business demand, and  
• infrastructure at competitive costs.  
 
This element captures the costs associated with setting up and maintaining a cloud data lake 
environment, including the capital costs, operating expenses, and run-the-business costs 
associated with the data lake attributable to AMI. 
 
 (5) Licensed Infrastructure CAPEX, OPEX, and RTB  
Depending on the selected vendor, the Company may incur additional costs for the hardware 
(e.g. servers, wires) to stand up and run the software. This cost element reflects the capital costs, 
operating expenses, and run-the-business costs associated with this additional infrastructure. 
 
(6) Meter Inventory Management Upgrade  
For the meter inventory, the Company uses a handheld device to scan each meter and annotate its 
associated characteristics. This data set is then uploaded into the meter inventory management 
portal. This cost element reflects the upgrade to the system that maintains each meter’s features.  
 
(8) Load Disaggregation Software 
Load disaggregation software offers the customer an option to separate out their aggregate 
energy use by appliance using a combination of sensors and algorithms. With this view, the 
customer can alter their behavior based on the energy profile of a specific appliance. The load 
disaggregation software cost assumes 50 percent of the customer base will utilize the load 
disaggregation software. This cost element includes the cost to procure the software per 
customer.

3.3.5 Cyber Security 
 
The Company understands that in an evolving technology landscape, there are growing cyber 
security risks. To best secure AMI, a comprehensive cyber security plan will incorporate proper 
end-to-end security controls into all aspects of design, implementation, and deployment of AMI 
meter technology. These security controls will secure and monitor all AMI meter devices, the 
communications infrastructure, and back-office systems supporting them, along with user portals 
and other critical infrastructures.  
  
The Company will leverage industry-leading best practices to meet the goals of a robust cyber 
security program, such as defining the Company’s encryption practices to ensure the encryption 
of any data transmitted across the network follows nationally recognized standards and 
protocols. Other practices include robust training, change control, configuration management 
security, access monitoring, incident management, end-to-end encryption, network segmentation, 
and firewalls. These cyber security measures enable the Company to maintain confidentiality, 
integrity, and availability of the systems and its information in both the short and long-term 
future of AMI.  
 
The business case reviewed the high-level cyber security service domains based on the 
cybersecurity strategy, services and controls as defined by National Grid in its Distributed 
System Implementation Plan (“DSIP”) filing. The scope of the DSIP covers Advanced Meter 
Infrastructure (AMI), Grid Modernization (including systems and components to enable VVO, 
FLISR, etc.), and the overall Distributed System Platform (DSP).  
 
All systems, components, and integrations from the AMI Business Case were considered as part 
of this review in consideration of the following service domains: 
 
• 
Network Security, 
• 
Data Security, 
• 
Identity & Access Management, 
• 
Threat and Vulnerability Management, 
• 
Security Operations Center, 
• 
Host and Endpoint Security, 
• 
Security Policy Management, 
• 
Cryptography, 
• 
Change & Configuration Management, 
• 
Security Awareness & Training, 
• 
Application Security, 
• 
Third Party Assurance, 
• 
Remote Access, and 
• 
Privacy. 
 
National Grid’s cyber security approach follows the recognized and widely-accepted National 
Institute of Standards and Technology’s (“NIST”) Cybersecurity Framework to align the 
Company’s IS policies with the business objectives and the technological approaches to manage 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 110 of 313


--- Page 146 ---
33 
 
cybersecurity-related risk. Specifically, the Cybersecurity Framework includes five core 
elements as described in Table 3-8. 
 
 
 
Table 3-8: Cyber Security Framework Core Elements 
Core Element 
Description 
Identify 
Know the associated risks and recognize the threat landscape to National Grid’s systems, 
people, assets, data, and capabilities 
Protect 
Select and implement controls to prevent identified and unknown threats  
Detect 
Set-up and monitor the appropriate safeguards to reveal a cybersecurity event 
Response 
Develop the initial response plan to use when responding to a detected cyber security 
incident. 
Recover 
Establish necessary plans to recover and restore any capabilities or services that were 
impaired due to a cyber security incident. 
 
Using these core elements, the Company: 1) is conducting a risk assessment to identify the threat 
actors and the potential impact; 2) then i will create a security plan to prioritize the potential 
impact; 3) integrate that plan with the solution architecture; 4) test the implemented controls, and 
5) determine the actions around residual risk based on the business needs. 
First, the risk assessment will consider the threats, potential bad actors, scenarios, and the 
impact, all due to AMI deployment. For example, if an actor breaches a meter and under-reports 
the energy usage for that one meter, then the threat would be a loss of revenue and the impact 
would be minimal financial impact. However, a breach of multiple meters would have a more 
harmful effect, because, while the threat still is revenue loss, the impact in aggregate would be 
much deeper revenue losses. Additional risks include firmware pushing down incorrect or 
corrupted data, bypassing cyber security requirements.  
 
Once the risk assessment is complete, the Company will create the Security Plan as its response 
to the risk assessment. In the Plan, the Company will enact the controls that have the best overall 
probability of either preventing or diminishing the impact. 
 
The Company will then integrate the Plan with the solution architecture to understand how the 
systems will work together. The system will then test the implemented controls against the 
solution, confirming the controls meet the requirements and expected outcomes. Finally, the 
business must decide how to proceed with any residual risks. Depending on the risk and its level 
of impact, the business must decide to avoid, transfer, mitigate, accept, or share the remaining 
risk. 
 
Cost Components 
Advanced metering and other grid modernization capabilities include many systems and 
components that are susceptible to potential vulnerabilities from cyber threats. To protect this 
new corporate infrastructure the Company has envisioned various proactive and reactive 
capabilities and services, including but not limited to: Network Security, Data Security, Threat 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 111 of 313


--- Page 147 ---
34 
 
and Vulnerability Management, and Identity and Access Management. Specifically, the cost of 
cyber security is comprised of five elements: 1) initial CapEx for software and hardware, (2) 
initial OpEx; 3) routine operating and maintenance costs to RTB; 4) refresh and removal CapEx 
for software and hardware; and 5) refresh and removal OpEx. 
 
These elements capture the initial capital expenditures and operating expenses, along with the 
annual cyber security costs to ensure the service functions are supported to run the business.  
 
Over time, the Company will refresh hardware and software to reflect recent advances in 
protective approaches and dynamics.

3.4.1 Equipment and Installation Refresh 
 
The Company recognized that over time electric meters, gas modules and field area network 
equipment will fail. Although this equipment is supported by a vendor provided warranty, costs 
have been allocated to facilitate the replacement and installation of failed AMI equipment. 
 
 
Cost Components 
The equipment and installation refresh cost element includes: 1) AMI failed meter equipment 
replacement cost; 2) AMI meter cellular service cost for electric meters; 3) AMI communications 
failed equipment replacement and O&M cost (outside warranty). 
 
(1) AMI failed meter equipment replacement cost 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 112 of 313


--- Page 148 ---
35 
 
 
AMI meter replacement cost recognizes that over time meters will fail. While the meters have a 
five-year warranty period, it will be National Grid’s responsibility to procure replacements after 
this period expires. This cost is applicable to both electric and gas metering capabilities. 
 
(2) AMI meter cellular service cost, Electric Meters 
A subset of electric meters will be located in rural areas with insufficient density to form a stable 
and consistent mesh. For these electric metering locations, an electric meter with a cellular radio 
will be installed instead of one with a mesh radio. The electric meters that use a cellular radio for 
communication have a corresponding ongoing service fee with public cellular providers. 
 
 (3) AMI communications failed equipment replacement and O&M cost 
AMI meters communicate with each other through mesh technologies for local communications 
but rely on more robust communications equipment for backhaul to back office systems. The 
core piece of equipment to perform this function is a Collector which can aggregate data from 
local metering mesh clusters and convey pertinent data through the WAN. Over time, it is 
expected that these devices will fail and require replacement. This cost element addresses the 
costs of the replacement equipment and the installation cost associated with replacing failed 
equipment throughout the duration of the program. 
 
3.4.2 Ongoing Business Management 
 
AMI deployment will require additional operational support to monitor and manage system 
performance and oversee numerous AMI processes, such as the VEE process, FAN performance, 
investigation and remediation of meter and communications infrastructure problems and 
firmware deployments. The Company has leveraged AMI pilot and business experience to 
estimate these costs. 
 
Cost Components 
The ongoing business management cost element includes: 1) back-office revenue assurance 
analyst cost; 2) cost of theft investigations; and 3) AMI additional meter data services labor cost. 
 
(1) Total Back-Office Revenue Assurance Analyst Cost  
The AMI implementation will increase the visibility on consumption. It allows for more granular 
data, providing greater depth and more frequent theft monitoring. AMI will be able to identify 
theft patterns quicker (i.e. flags/indicators from the MDM that indicate consumption on inactive 
or locked meters). Additionally, theft with greater pattern intricacy will also be detected. This 
increase in both the complexity and volume of information will create the necessity of an 
increase to the number of FTEs on the Revenue Assurance team to assess findings. Supplemental 
FTEs are derived from historical data on number of theft cases investigated annually.  
 
(2) Field Service Worker Revenue Assurance Theft Recovery Cost 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 113 of 313


--- Page 149 ---
36 
 
 
Today, the top limiting factor for revenue protection is the ability to track and pursue theft 
activity. Following a review and analysis of current-state revenue assurance practice areas, it was 
brought to light that Field Service Workers prioritize outages and other customer issues over 
theft, which hinders the Revenue Assurance process, which requires physical investigation in 
response to suspected theft. Committing Field Service Workers directly to the theft recovery 
effort would promote more effective meter tampering investigations and quicker response to 
potential theft. In the future-state, tampering/theft cases will be directly assigned to resources 
following a thorough investigation process in the back-office. Prioritization conflicts with this 
refined Field Service Worker organization will be virtually nonexistent (except in storm flag 
scenarios) because theft investigation orders will be assigned to each resource on a case-by-case 
basis.  
The number of theft cases that are necessary to be pursued are calculated based on the ability to 
recover 0.25 percent of the total annual electric revenue. This number is then multiplied by a 
calculated average recovered cost per case to find the number of cases that must be pursued.  
 (3) Total AMI Additional Meter Data Services labor cost 
Deployment of new Advanced Meters will result in more meter data that needs to be validated, 
estimated, and edited to support the meter to cash process. Past experience with pilots has shown 
that extra labor is required to support this effort for timely data processing. This element captures 
the number of FTEs required each year to support the incremental AMI data. 
 
3.4.3 Customer and Market Engagement Plan 
 
The Company has developed a comprehensive Customer Engagement Plan, in collaboration with 
stakeholders. Pairing AMI with proactive customer and market engagement initiatives will 
enable greater participation and customer benefits. The Program will take a staged approach, 
similar to its smart metering demonstration pilots. The customers will have access to additional 
technology solution initiatives to enhance AMI data access, to provide education and energy 
management insights, and to connect customers with new product and service offerings. These 
solutions will be in place prior to meter deployment so that customers can begin to immediately 
realize the benefits of AMI.  
 
The overall objective of the Customer Engagement Plan is to transparently inform and educate 
National Grid customers on the benefits of smart meters, in order to increase participation in the 
acceptance of new smart meters and the eventual adoption of new services and innovative rates. 
The increased granularity of usage data and reduced data latency enabled by smart meters 
coupled with products and services designed to take advantage of this information will enhance 
customers’ energy experience now and in the future, providing greater control, convenience and 
choice. In order to achieve this, National Grid will focus on 1) building awareness, transparency, 
and interest through education and outreach; 2) managing the customer experience through AMI 
meter deployment to foster customer participation; and 3) engaging customers to access new 
tools and options that will unlock the benefits of more actionable information and enhanced 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 114 of 313


--- Page 150 ---
37 
 
 
energy management, new and innovative pricing options, access to third-party services, 
enhanced customer alerting and personalization tools, and enablement of smart-home devices.  
Throughout this plan, the Company references education and outreach materials including 
samples from both the Clifton Park and Worcester AMI pilots.  For the full deployment of smart 
meters, the Company will develop new materials to support a territory wide campaign that 
incorporates the valuable learnings from the Collaborative sessions, stakeholder feedback, 
industry best practices and Company pilots.  The education and outreach will be aligned with the 
staggered deployment of each cohort so that customers receive timely information that coincides 
with their own deployment. 
National Grid has developed a robust education and engagement plan structured around three 
distinct phases and messages: customer AMI awareness prior to deployment, the customer 
experience through deployment and continued empowerment and enablement following 
deployment and in preparation for the introduction of TVP.   
• Phase One (AMI Awareness): The Company will build an internal foundation of 
resources to support communication and engagement activities, with the education and 
training of internal employees. Additionally, the Company will initiate a territory-wide 
customer and stakeholder outreach effort to transparently build awareness of the smart 
metering program, generate interest and support, while proactively addressing customer 
concerns prior to meter installation. 
• Phase Two (Deployment): The Company plans to build on the broad education initiated 
in Phase One and narrow the focus of communication toward individual customers. 
Information will be provided which will guide customers leading up to and through the 
day of smart meter installation, including the timeline of events, what to expect, and 
alternate choices available. 
• Phase Three (Customer Empowerment and Enablement): Post mete installation, 
customers will be able to take full advantage of the enhanced Customer Energy 
Management Platform and future TVP pricing plans enabled by AMI meters. The 
sustaining nature of this phase will focus on empowering and enabling customers, 
including how to gain or share access to their interval energy data, how to interpret their 
energy consumption and how to manage/optimize their energy usage to reduce energy 
and costs. This phase will also introduce customers to how smart meters can animate the 
energy markets by connecting customers to third-party vendors who can supplement 
customer needs with new and innovative products and services that are incremental to the 
new utility-provided offerings enabled by the smart metering program.  
 
This phased approach will support the customer journey and will include specific marketing 
strategies with defined messages, audiences, channels, and educational materials to generate 
customer interest, increase customer acceptance, and drive customer engagement. The materials 
will reach a diverse audience and be presented with consumer-friendly messaging to allow for 
easily understandable text and images.

3.4.4 Critical Peak Pricing and Time-of-Use Pricing 
 
The deployment of Critical Peak Pricing (“CPP”) and Time-of-Use (“TOU”) pricing programs 
will result in additional costs. The Company estimated the cost to achieve the outlined 
participation rates and energy savings for each program to be twenty percent of the overall 
projected benefits and will be incurred commensurate with benefit realization. 
 
3.4.5 Project Management  
 
AMI Project Management will provide the necessary framework for the successful integration of 
interdependent technology components and processes through the proposed six-year AMI 
program. The project management team will consist of internal project management leadership 
and business support, supplemented by external support.  
 
Cost Components 
The Project Management cost element includes: 1) external project management labor cost 
CapEx and OpEx; 2) internal project management leadership staff CapEx and OpEx; 3) internal 
project management business support CapEx and OpEx; 4) call center and account maintenance 
and operations during implementation. 
 
(1) External Project Management Labor CAPEX and OPEX 
Advanced Metering requires the design, configuration, testing, deployment, and management of 
numerous components and systems. National Grid expects to augment internal efforts with the 
support of external project management resources to advance project objectives. This cost 
element captures the external cost of contract labor to facilitate and assist National Grid staff 
through the requisite program lifecycle. For this cost calculation, the Company multiplied the 
total number of external resources used for AMI project management by an external resource 
annual salary and allocated the costs to CapEx and OpEx based on the activities anticipated to be 
performed in each year of the deployment.  
 
(2) Internal Project Management Leadership Staff – CAPEX and OPEX 
The Company expects to have internal employees dedicated to advancing project objectives. 
This cost element captures the cost of National Grid Leadership Staff that will manage the 
program through its lifecycle. The costs are allocated to CapEx and OpEx based on the activities 
anticipated to be performed in each year of the deployment. 
 
(3) Internal Project Management Business Support – CAPEX and OPEX 
The Company expects to have internal employees dedicated to advancing project objectives. 
This cost element captures the cost of National Grid business support Staff that will manage the 
program through its lifecycle. The costs are allocated to CapEx and OpEx based on the activities 
anticipated to be performed in each year of the deployment. 
 
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 117 of 313


--- Page 153 ---
40 
 
 
(4) Call Center and Account Maintenance and Operations 
National Grid believes that supporting customers with a robust change management program is 
vital to the overall successful adoption of this program and understands that AMI deployment 
will have customer impacts. Customers may have questions regarding AMI technology, the 
installation schedule, or how to access their detailed consumption data. To address this increased 
call volume, National Grid believes the most personal point of contact for many customers will 
be the Company call center.  
 
Therefore, during the deployment years of the AMI program, call volumes are expected increase. 
This cost element addresses the incremental cost associated with increased call volumes and 
training across various call center facilities.  
 
Installation of the AMI meters in the field will also require parallel back-office support to ensure 
timeliness and accuracy of the initial billing on the new meter as well as regular maintenance to 
support meter changes, new customer connections and rate programs. National Grid has found in 
recent pilots that proactive review and assurance of meter installation to the point of first bill 
accuracy creates a successful customer program. It is expected that account maintenance 
resources largely increase during the installation of the meters and then begin to ramp back down 
after meter deployment.  
 
4 
Benefits of Proposed AMI Program 
 
For the AMI Business Case, the Company categorized benefits into six segments listed below.  
The first four segments are included in the societal BCA test and are included in Table 4-1. 
 
1) Customer benefits; 
2) Avoided operations and maintenance (“O&M”) costs;  
3) Avoided AMR Replacement Costs and Avoided Alternative Metering Costs to Support 
VDER; 
4) Societal benefits; 
5) Revenue benefits (impacting the RIM test only); and 
6) Other non-quantifiable benefits not reflected in the BCA. 
 
4.1 
Avoided O&M Costs 
 
As seen in Table 4-1, the total avoided O&M cost benefit is $145.49 million and comprised of 
seven elements: 1) AMR meter metering; 2) meter investigation; 3) remote connect and 
disconnect; 4) reduction in damage claims; 5) storm OMS benefit; 6) FCS meter reading; 7) 
interval meter reading.

4.1.1 AMR Meter Reading 
 
National Grid’s electric and gas territories currently use AMR meters, requiring monthly reads 
via radio frequency technology. To obtain these meter readings, a fleet of radio equipped service 
vans drive along specific routes to collect the information. Therefore, by phasing out AMR 
meters with AMI-enabled technology, beginning in fiscal year 2022, the Company will eliminate 
the costs associated with AMR meter reading vehicles, personnel, annual software costs and 
annual maintenance costs for the replaced AMR meters.  
 
Benefit Components 
The AMR Meter Reading benefit is comprised of two elements: 1) total benefit from eliminated 
AMR meter readers; and 2) total benefit from eliminated AMR meter reader vehicle costs. 
 
(1) Total Benefit from Eliminated AMR Meter Readers 
As the Company deploys AMI meters, the reduction in AMR meters will eliminate the need for 
AMR meter reading staff. This benefit calculation takes the annual cost per AMR Meter Reader 
and multiplies it by the number of AMR meter readers that will be eliminated and includes 
reduction of the Annual AMR maintenance bill for upstate New York. 
 
 (2) Total benefit from eliminated AMR meter reader vehicle costs. 
In addition to avoiding the need for the AMI meter readers as a result of the new technology, 
National Grid will also be able to reduce the number of related company vehicles formerly 
utilized by this function. This benefit calculation multiplies the number of full-time meter 
reading employee reductions by the vehicle cost per employee.  
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 119 of 313


--- Page 155 ---
42 
 
 
4.1.2 Meter Investigation 
 
Since smart meters provide automatic and on-demand meter reads and diagnostic checks to alert 
and inform the Company about anomalous situations, there will be fewer visits for manual meter 
investigations, thus reducing labor and vehicle costs. This benefit only applies to electric meters 
as the Company will investigate gas meters for safety reasons. However, the Company can avoid 
in full or in part the following types of manual meter investigations:  
 
• Check Electric Meter Multiplier Investigations,  
• Irregular Electric Meter Investigations,  
• Electric Meter Number Verification Investigations,  
• Use on Inactive Electric Meter Investigations,  
• Meter Reads, and  
• High Bill Meter Reads. 
 
Benefit Components 
This benefit calculation includes two parts:  
 
1) reduction of labor associated with performing investigations; and 
2) reduction of vehicle costs associated with performing investigations.  
 
In aggregate, the labor and vehicle cost reductions added together. 
 
4.1.3 Remote Connect & Disconnect 
 
Advanced Metering provides the ability to connect and disconnect electric service remotely and 
in near real-time, resulting in reduced labor and vehicle costs for avoided meter visits. This 
capability avoids initial and, in some cases, repeat visits to the meter for manual meter connects, 
providing the customer with quicker service, and disconnects. The estimated savings assumes the 
Company would need to continue manual field connects and disconnects for dual-fuel customers 
since the gas modules would not have the remote connect functionality upon meter deployment. 
With respect to collections-related disconnects, the Company will comply with all Home Energy 
Fair Practices Act (HEFPA) requirements, including visits to the customers’ premises.  
 
Benefit Components 
This benefit calculation is generally comprised of two parts: 
 
• Reduction of vehicle costs associated with certain meter disconnects and reconnects 
• Reduction of labor costs associated with certain meter disconnects and reconnects  
 
In aggregate, these two cost reduction components are added together and apply a labor 
repurpose rate to account for labor which will be re-allocated to perform additional functions.  
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 120 of 313


--- Page 156 ---
43 
 
 
4.1.4 Reduction in Damage Claims 
 
The reduction of service visits lessens the potential for accidents or damage to third-party 
property to occur on the way to or at the service site, thus decreasing the number of damage 
claims. Despite mindfulness and a commitment to safety, accidents while driving to and from 
service orders, routine meter reading routes, or other day-to-day activities can occur. The remote 
interaction capability of advanced metering technologies limits the number of metering service 
representatives on the road and reduces the risk for accidents and damage at the customers’ 
premises.  
 
Benefit Components 
This benefit calculation identifies the average number of damage claims per meter, determines 
the monetary value of these claims and allocates a percentage reduction as a result of AMI meter 
functionality. 
 
4.1.5 Outage Management 
 
The Company spends millions of dollars annually on storm restoration efforts to include 
procurement of external crews, meals and lodging, and overtime. AMI would increase visibility 
during major and minor storms due to the ability to contact meters remotely and determine 
outage status. This enhanced situational awareness creates efficiencies with crew management 
and deployment as well as the avoidance of false outages, thereby reducing costs. Additionally, 
AMI will provide the same benefits for non-storm days. 
 
This benefit was determined by reviewing key outage information over 2.5 years to identify 
outages where a truck roll could likely be avoided with AMI. Specific comments attributed to 
each outage were analyzed used to determine avoidable outages. Costs were then assigned to the 
identified outages by extracting the outage duration and multiplying by personnel and fleet costs 
based on the crew type and truck type rolled out, respectively. Total benefits were then 
annualized. Roughly half of the benefit was determined to be attributable to Major Storm events 
by aligning outages to dates of major storms. 
 
4.1.6 Avoided Field Collection System Costs 
The Field Collection System (FCS) is currently utilized to perform manual and AMR meter 
reading for both residential and commercial customers. With the implementation of AMI meters, 
the FCS back-office costs will be phased out as the AMI system utilizes new back office systems 
to manage data collection and processing. The calculation assumes the percentage of annual FCS 
maintenance costs allocated to NY (forecasted for historical inflation).

4.1.7 Avoided Interval Meter Reading Upgrade Costs 
 
Similar to the avoided AMR meter reading costs (Section 4.2), the Company will avoid costs 
associated with licensing and managing the MV90 interval meter reading system. This benefit 
component includes the avoided costs of MV90 licensing and IS support and avoided field visit 
costs.

elements: (1) avoided AMR program capital costs, (2) avoided AMR program O&M costs, and 
(3) avoided VDER costs. Table 4-2 summarizes the benefit for avoided AMR and VDER costs.

4.2.1 Capital 
 
Without the implementation of an AMI program, the company would be required to continue its 
AMR program. This would result in capital costs to replace meters reaching the end of their 
lifecycle, meter installation, field equipment upgrades and project management. Thus, the AMI 
program avoids the need to replace the existing AMR meters and associated systems. The 
avoided cost of these activities is categorized as a benefit in the AMI model. The Company 
assumed the electric AMR meters and gas modules would be replaced when they reach 20-years 
of age, the manufacture projected life.  
 
4.2.2 Operations & Maintenance 
 
The AMI program also avoids the associated O&M costs of the life-cycle replacement program 
for the existing electric AMR meters and gas modules, such as call center calls and customer 
communications. The BCA assumed similar program cost elements between an AMR 
replacement program and the proposed AMI program.

4.2.3 Avoided Interval Meter Costs to Support VDER  
 
The compensation mechanisms for Distributed Energy Resources (“DER”) with in-service dates 
after January 1, 2020, are expected to require hourly meter usage data. Without AMI, the 
Company or customers would incur additional costs to implement an alternative metering 
method. Specifically, National Grid would extend the program implemented as part of the 
Clifton Park demonstration project to support VDER. The Clifton Park system utilizes cellular 
interval meters which cost approximately four times the standard AMI mesh meters. Thus, the 
benefit recognizes that the Company would avoid the VDER metering program costs if AMI is 
implemented.  
 
 
Benefit Components 
To forecast DER adoption (Figure 4-2), the Company analyzed the historical trends from 2014 to 
present-day while incorporating various market conditions such as: 
 
• VDER meter forecast beyond 2024 held constant at 2024 level 
• 2018 (96 projects) & 2019 (80 projects) value stack forecast is based on Complex 
projects connecting under Value Stack 
• 2018 & 2019 overall forecast is number of DG projects connecting in those respective 
years; this forecast is based on number of applications in our queue and assuming only 70 
percent will connect 
• Seasonality assumption of two months into the forecast, as most of the residential 
systems are constructed during the summer, resulting in higher volumes in those months 
• All meters installed for all types of DG systems after 2019 assumed to be interval meters 
• 2014 through 2017 are actual number DG applications connected and not necessarily 
total interval meters installed 
• The Company has only 70 percent confidence in its forecast, because, in New York, DG 
is still unsettled and the Company does not have steady state in order to rely on 
mathematical formulas 
 
The Company has taken a conservative approach and anticipates a far higher saturation could be 
achieved.

4.3.1 Volt-VAR Optimization (“VVO”) 
 
As AMI meters can read and report usage data multiple times a day, they can act as end-of-line 
sensors to centralized control systems. These systems can then make voltage adjustments for the 
time of day and year or temperature to define more precise load models of individual circuits. By 
fine-tuning grid operational characteristics to be more efficient, the system provides an 
acceptable level of voltage without wasting excess energy, resulting in overall savings to the 
customer.  
 
For the purposes of the business case, the Company recognizes VVO benefits would be 
incremental to those achieved by Grid Modernization. Therefore, to calculate this benefit, the 
Company multiplied the cumulative pre-CVR load on circuits where CVR was deployed by the 
improved percentage due to CVR attributable to AMI data assumed to be 0.5 percent Then, 
multiplied that cost per megawatt hour. 
 
4.3.2 Customer Energy Management Platform / High Usage Alerts 
 
Through the deployment of AMI smart meters and associated back-office infrastructure, the 
Company will have enhanced access to customer usage data, with granularity at sub-hour reading 
intervals. National Grid will build a Customer Energy Management Platform (“Platform”) that 
will act as a hub for residential, commercial and industrial customers to view their energy usage, 
including the smart meter interval data. This platform will allow customers to view raw data 
illustrating their consumption within four to eight hours of the end of a given billing interval and 
billing quality data within 24 hours. With this data, customers can compare their usage and costs 
against certain variables such as weather, historic consumption and neighbors’ usage to 
understand factors that may be driving their energy use.  
 
Customers have the option to act on information from the Platform. Additional Platform 
capabilities are summarized in Table 4-4.

Benefit Components 
Disaggregation of high resolution meter data can provide appliance-level energy usage and 
detailed information that can provide greater insight for customers to understand how their 
appliances, business buildings, and homes use electricity. This detailed data enhances energy 
efficiency and demand response programs with actionable insights and information. For this 
benefit, the Company assumed electric consumption forecasts and Platform participation to be at 
50 percent. Reduction due to real-time data visualization is projected at low range of 1.5 percent 
with a high range of 3 percent. These ranges are supported by various industry demonstration 
projects, and the Company’s Worcester pilot results. 
 
Total commodity savings 
This benefit is calculated for both electric and gas.  The calculation is comprised of two parts: 1) 
the reduction of GWh/Therms consumed; and 2) the value of avoided GWh/Therms. 
 
To calculate the electric savings, the Company first established the aggregate demand for electric 
of both residential and commercial customers after EE programs. Then, this aggregate demand 
was multiplied by the cumulative AMI deployment, the percentage of Customer Energy 
Management Platform participation, and by the reduction due to real-time data visualization 
(assumed to range between 1.5 percent and 3 percent) to find the reduction of GWh consumed, 
which can be used to derive electric commodity savings. 
 
Likewise, to find the gas commodity savings, the Company first established the gas residential 
aggregate demand in Therms. The total gas residential demand was then multiplied by the 
cumulative AMI deployment by the percentage of Customer Energy Management Platform 
participation and then by the reduction due to real-time data visualization (assumed to range 
between 1.5 percent and 3 percent) to get the gas consumption reduced in Therms. This 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 130 of 313


--- Page 166 ---
53 
 
 
reduction in gas consumption is then multiplied by the SC1 Average Price ($/Therm) to 
determine the total commodity savings. 
 
4.3.3 Innovative Rate Structures (TVP) 
 
AMI deployment enables National Grid to provide time-variant price signals to customers. TVP 
options for customers provide them with the opportunities to reduce energy consumption and/or 
shift usage from high-cost periods to lower-cost periods, resulting in savings on the system and 
advancing the goals and objectives of REV.  
 
The Company proposed two example designs and net benefits analysis of TVP using (1) Time-
of-use (“TOU”) pricing and (2) Critical Peak Pricing (“CPP”): 
 
1) Time of Use (“TOU”) Pricing: supply prices will vary by specific times of day, 
every month, with peak (higher price) and off-peak (lower price) periods defined. 
In response to TOU rates, customers save by reducing consumption during higher 
cost peak periods and/or shifting use from peak to off-peak periods.  
 
2) Critical Peak Pricing (“CPP”): supply prices will adjust to a higher level during 
certain operational conditions designated as CPP events, which are typically 
during high demands on the electrical system and where customers are notified in 
advance. CPP events are limited to a specific number of days and during specific 
hours of the day, which gives customers a greater level of flexibility. 
 
Creating an optimal TVP program will be achieved over multiple years of phase-ins with 
introductions of new rate designs, software tools, data availability and customer education. This 
means an optimal design will evolve over time. For this reason, the Company has considered an 
opt-out scenario where, by default, a significant percentage of customers will be enrolled in these 
pricing programs, as well as an opt-in scenario, in which customers must choose to enroll on the 
rate. 
 
Through educational initiatives and pricing signals designed to encourage efficient consumption 
behavior, customers will, over time, proactively shift portions of their energy consumption to 
times of day where energy rates are lower, thereby resulting in reductions in their electric bills. 
In addition to incentivizing customers’ savings, consumers shifting their energy usage will 
flatten the overall load curve. This shift, combined with other programs such as VVO and energy 
efficiency, will lower energy peaks, thus reducing expenditures on generation capacity. 
 
Benefit Components 
This benefit is calculated using (1) the total benefit from CPP peak shaving and (2) the total 
benefit from avoided energy due to TOU program. 
(1) Total Benefit from Critical Peak Pricing (CPP) peak shaving 
Supply prices will increase at certain times of day on a limited number of specific days (typically 
during high demands on the electrical system, where customers are notified in advance) 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 131 of 313


--- Page 167 ---
54 
 
 
designated as CPP events. CPP is designed to recover most of the costs for generation capacity in 
the hours that have the greatest need for peak capacity. When customers avoid consumption 
during the highest peak loads of the year, future generation capacity costs, as determined through 
ISO-NE’s Forward Capacity Market auction, are reduced relative to what they otherwise might 
have been, resulting in capacity cost savings that are included in supply rates for customers. CPP 
events would be limited to a specific number of days and during specific hours of the day, which 
gives customers a greater level of flexibility relative to a set critical peak price period.   
  
The benefits from the Company’s illustrative TVP program will result from savings in 
generation capacity costs described above as well as savings in energy costs. Energy cost savings 
result from a reduction in energy consumption during higher-cost peak periods, and the resulting 
reduction in the hourly marginal generation cost. 
 
This benefit calculation is generally comprised of a multiplication of forecasted annual peak load 
multiplied by an achievable load reduction percentage (based on AMI deployment and CPP 
adoption) multiplied by an anticipated CPP Capacity Payment $ per MW avoided. 
(2) Total Benefit from Avoided Energy due to Time-of-Use Program 
Supply prices will vary by specific times of day, every month, with peak (higher price) and off-
peak (lower price) periods defined. In response to TOU rates, customers save by reducing 
consumption during higher cost peak periods and/or shifting use from peak to off-peak periods.  
This benefit calculation is generally comprised of a multiplication of forecasted load during peak 
hours multiplied by achievable load reduction percentage (based on AMI deployment and TOU 
adoption) multiplied by avoided average fuel costs per MWh of generation. 
 
4.3.4 Electric Vehicles 
 
The Company expects the introduction of AMI and TVP for electric vehicle charging to enable 
demand savings and avoided energy charges. The estimate for the electric vehicle integration 
benefit assumes customers will shift a certain percentage of electric vehicle charging from peak 
to off-peak periods. The Company has developed a model to estimate avoided demand costs and 
the avoided energy cost from shifts to off-peak charging.

4.4.1 Reduction in CO2 Emissions from Reduced Truck Rolls 
 
AMI will reduce the number of truck rolls and miles driven to read meters and complete certain 
types of work orders that can be carried out from the office with AMI functionality.  The 
reduction in company vehicles miles in turn reduces diesel fuel consumption, which reduces CO2 
emissions. CO2 benefits have been calculated for the following benefits areas: 
 
1) AMR Meter Reading – AMI eliminates the need for meter readers to perform drive-by 
readings using company vehicles as described in section 4.1.1. 
 
2)  Meter Investigations – AMI meters will provide automatic and on-demand meter reads 
and diagnostics to alert and inform the Company about anomalous situations, reducing 
number of visits to the meter for manual meter investigations as described in Section 
4.1.2. 
 
3) Remote Connect and Disconnect – With the ability to remotely connect and disconnect 
electric service in near real-time, the Company can avoid initial and repeat visits to the 
meter for manual meter connects and disconnect as described in Section 4.1.3. 
 
4.4.2 Reduction in CO2 Emissions from Energy Consumption Reductions 
 
AMI will reduce CO2 emissions where energy consumption reductions are achieved in the 
following areas:  
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 133 of 313


--- Page 169 ---
56 
 
 
1) Energy Insights Savings/High Usage Alerts - As described in Section 4.3.2 customer 
access to detailed energy consumption data, insights, and high usage alerts will enable 
customers to reduce their energy consumption 
 
2) Time-Varying Pricing (“TVP”) - Based on the annual MWh reductions calculated from 
the TVP program, a CO2 benefit can be calculated. This benefit calculation multiplies the 
total benefit from Avoided Energy due to Time-of-Use Program calculated in Section 4.3 
by the price of carbon dioxide per MWh. 
 
3) Volt-VAR Optimization (“VVO”) - Use of VVO as described in section 4.3.1 reduces 
distribution energy losses and associated CO2 emissions. 
 
4.5 
Revenue Benefits 
 
Theft of service and bad debt costs are socialized among the entire customer base and result in 
increased rates. These benefits are included in the RIM BCA test but not the SCT or UCT. For 
the purposes of the BCA, the Company categorizes reductions in these costs as Revenue 
Benefits.
