4.5.1 Reduction in Theft of Service 
 
Smart meter technology combines greater frequency of readings with sophisticated algorithms to 
ensure that electric and gas consumption is accurate. AMI provides tamper alarms after detecting 
usage that attempts to bypass the meter, and also produces customer level data that can be 
analyzed for reasonableness in order to identify unusual patterns that may reflect theft of service. 
If discrepancies are proven to be theft, the Company can take action to address the situation, thus 
minimizing a cost that would normally be socialized across the customer base, thereby saving all 
customers money.  
 
In early 2017, the Company examined and found the tamper information from the AMR program 
to be within industry standards. The AMR meters within the NY state service territory currently 
trigger a tamper flag whenever the meter records non-standard meter handling and 
manipulations. The AMR meter then sends this information to the customer information system 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 134 of 313


--- Page 170 ---
57 
 
 
monthly. Thus, the AMI program will allow for significantly greater realization of theft benefit 
because: 
(1) More regular data at shorter time intervals allows the Company to better monitor and 
intervene earlier at locations of suspected tampering;  
(2) Data analytics enable better theft detection to prioritize instances of greater potential 
revenue loss or importance to National Grid’s customer organization; and 
(3) Ability to pursue and investigate more suspected theft/tampering cases, thus 
improving revenue protection operational process flows and staff augmentations. 
The increased meter scrutiny may shift customer behavior away from meter tampering. While 
not quantified the tampering prevention capability would reduce overall revenue loss, a loss that 
is ordinarily distributed amongst ratepayers. 
EPRI’s 2008 white-paper titled “Limiting Non-Technical Distribution Losses in The Future” 
estimated Company’s experience a 0.5 to 3.5 percent loss of annual revenue from theft and meter 
tampering. Citing this study, advanced meter program public filings from Avangrid, 
Consolidated Edison, and Ameren Illinois assumed 0.25 percent of annual revenue can be 
recovered from advanced metering capabilities and efficiencies. In this benefit, National Grid 
also assumed a conservative theft recovery benefit of 0.25 percent of annual electric revenue. 
4.5.2 Reduction in Write-offs and Inactive Meter Consumption 
 
Reduction in Write-Offs 
National Grid incurs bad debt when customers are unable or unwilling to pay their billing 
obligations. While the Company makes every reasonable attempt to collect those outstanding 
bills, the Company must eventually classify this unrealized revenue as a loss, write it off, and 
spread it across all customers.  
 
However, the Company can reduce these socialized costs because of smart meters’ ability to 
remotely disconnect service, working within the existing approved parameters and in 
consideration of consumer protection processes. The remote disconnect function reduces the 
period when an electric customer defaults on a payment to when the Company disconnects the 
meter, thus reducing the loss incurred. 
 
Although the smart meters cannot fully cut bad debt write-offs, the impact of this functionality 
will prompt a change in customer behavior, resulting in a significant reduction in overall bad 
debt and operational expense. 
 
Reduction in Inactive Meter Consumption 
The remote connect and disconnect capability of smart meters also mitigates costs associated 
with inactive meters or “soft off” unoccupied premises. A “soft off” inactive meter occurs when 
the meter registers use while the linked account is inactive. National Grid estimates there are 
around 170,000 inactive electric meters within its service territory.  
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 135 of 313


--- Page 171 ---
58 
 
 
As an example, an inactive meter occurs when a customer moves into a previously unoccupied 
property and does not notify the company to change the account name. Although energy flows to 
the premises, the company does not bill use on that account until the company reads the meter to 
discover the use, meaning the interim period between inactive meter activity and the 
confirmation of a new account name is a loss to the Company.  
 
Therefore, the ability of smart meters to read daily usage and remotely disconnect minimizes 
inactive meter usage on vacant property. As a result, National Grid can reduce these unbillable 
energy costs that the Company once had to distribute across the entire customer base. 
 
This benefit calculation takes the cumulative AMI Deployment and multiplies it by the total 
residential and commercial growth in bad debt mitigation attributable to AMI data to get the total 
incremental annual bad debt mitigation. The analysis first recognizes the value in FY2024 and 
adjusts for inflation on an annual basis. 
 
4.6 
Non-Quantifiable Benefits 
 
Water Utility / Municipality Revenue Opportunities with Joint Use  
 
For many years, electric utilities have employed the “Joint Use” principle of sharing parts of the 
infrastructure when available, such as utility poles that host electric, telephone, and cable 
television lines. Similarly, when applied to metering technology, water utilities can leverage the 
technical umbrella of National Grid’s proposed infrastructure to support overlapping metering 
efforts. While the respective water agency will procure and install water meters independently, 
National Grid could provide “Metering-As-A-Service” to interested jurisdictions, sharing the 
wireless radios, backhaul, and back-office validation systems and reducing the water utilities 
costs. In this way, while REV is strictly applicable to energy, the concepts of greater customer 
information and empowered decision making can be expanded as a more holistic capability for 
customers located in upstate New York. 
 
AMI for Street Lights and Ancillary Devices  
 
Many metering technology vendors offer both lighting control technology applications and 
metering capabilities for street light infrastructure, complementing the other proposed metering 
capabilities. Street lights have a universal industry standard receptacle for a light sensitive 
photoelectric control used to facilitate the changing dusk-to-dawn operating schedule throughout 
the year. This lighting control can be replaced with a new control device that incorporates 
dedicated solid-state AMI meter chip technology. At a minimum, this control device can 
integrate with the metering mesh to transition street lighting from an unmetered to a metered 
billing application while also incorporating smart street lighting ability including dimming and 
enabling smart city applications.   
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 136 of 313


--- Page 172 ---
59 
 
 
The instant on/off and dimming capabilities of solid state lighting technology (e.g. light emitting 
diode (LED)) drives customer demand for customized, variable operating schedules and 
illumination levels based on application needs. AMI data enables the additional energy savings 
of these tailored usage applications, beyond the savings achieved through conversion from 
legacy lighting technologies, because of the ability to provide more detailed consumption data.  
 
Additionally, these devices provide additional communication contact nodes to reinforce and 
strengthen data routing. Further, by virtue of the inherent elevation and location logistics, the 
additional nodes can also reduce communication hop counts and minimize the urban concrete 
canyon effects by increasing the number of direct communications to the nearest wireless router. 
 
Street Light AMI also has several benefits independent of the broader metering platform. These 
include: 
  
• Minimizes customer/company interaction for operation condition reporting through 
preemptive maintenance based on: 
 
o Luminaire diagnostics to identify imminent failure characteristics for lamps, 
ignitors, ballasts, surge suppression and photocontrol sensors to avoid “outages” 
or “day-burners”; 
o Circuitry diagnostics used to identify electric operating conditions; 
o Detection of errant (stray) voltage conditions and inadequate grounding capacity; 
 
• Promotes the application and accurate energy metering of advanced technologies such as: 
Wi-Fi, surveillance and detection cameras, sensors (e.g. motion, temperature, humidity, 
hazardous chemicals, radiation), distributed antenna and small cell technology, 
interactive parking meters, vehicle charging stations and other emergency notification 
systems; 
 
• Establishes a real-time, GPS global position for all street lighting and ancillary device 
locations; 
 
• Supports active asset management of street lighting and associated infrastructure for 
accurate inventory and billing requirements;  
 
• Enhances customer accessibility of street lighting /device information through a secure 
interactive internet interface for inventory information, operational scheduling/dimming, 
installation/removal/relocation requests and scheduling, maintenance service reporting 
and performance;  
 
• Enables customer control of advanced lighting technologies facilitating dynamic use of 
the lights while experiencing actual energy consumption billing optimizing all energy 
efficiencies;  
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 137 of 313


--- Page 173 ---
60 
 
 
• Enables smart city application deployment by providing a secure and reliable network for 
smart city sensors to communicate on; and 
 
• 
Strengthens the overall AMI network by providing additional hop points within the mesh 
that can reduce the overall latency of the network. 
 
Gas Remote Service Valve  
 
The AMI solution will integrate with gas remote service shutoff valves, allowing the Company 
to remotely disconnect the customer’s service for safety reasons, such as residential methane 
detection alarms, gas leaks, and customer natural gas calls. Remote Service Shutoff Valves can 
also be outfitted with flood sensors to enable improved emergency response in flooding 
situations. In this targeted approach, the remote service valve with flood sensors will 
automatically shut off gas only to the structures that experience flooding (as opposed to the 
larger gas service districts) and give an accurate count of services impacted by the flooding. The 
Gas Remote Valve with AMI can isolate the system and alert both the impacted customers and 
the Company on the loss of service in real time. This capability enables improved management 
of storm restoration with a specific focus on the affected customers and regions, leading to 
efficient service restoration and improved customer satisfaction while ensuring the safety and 
reliability of the system.  
 
Residential Methane Detectors  
 
Residential Methane Detectors (RMD) equipped with communication devices, also known as 
Smart Residential Methane Devices, are currently in research and development for AMI 
deployment. In the event the Smart RMD senses methane at a customer location, the Smart SMD 
would be able to send a notification to the Company through a fixed communication network, 
expediting National Grid’s response regardless if a customer calls. Because of the remote service 
valve as described in Section 0, National Grid will also have the ability to remotely turn off a 
customer’s service when methane is detected, ensuring the customer’s safety prior to a potential 
leak investigation. Moreover, the Smart RMD has the capability to systematically detect any type 
of methane or leak across multiple customer locations in a shared area, including customer-
owned equipment or piping. This is especially critical in multiple unit dwellings (i.e.-apartment 
buildings, multistory structures, etc.). 
 
Electric Vehicle Impact 
 
As the adoption rate of electric vehicles increases, the Company will have an opportunity to 
understand the impact of electrified vehicles on the system and the capacity of EV batteries as an 
alternative source of energy. With more electric vehicles, the demand on the grid will increase. 
Therefore, depending on the rate of charge and the battery capacity of the electric vehicles, the 
local distribution network may become strained or overloaded. Thus, the Company will study 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 138 of 313


--- Page 174 ---
61 
 
 
how to efficiently manage this demand to avoid local power quality issues by, for example, using 
an electric vehicle’s battery to store and feed energy back into a home of the local distribution 
grid during outages or times of high electricity demand.  
4.7 
Supporting the DSP with AMI 
 
As part of the larger NY REV initiative and in parallel to its AMI planning efforts, National Grid 
has been pursuing a variety of Grid Modernization technologies. These efforts include a variety 
of field devices, telecommunications, back-office systems and analytics capabilities. In 
aggregate, these digital investments will help enhance the grid to achieve the following 
objectives: 
 
1) Create a flexible, adaptable grid to enable new and emerging technologies; 
2) Minimize service disruptions with a resilient system that has the capacity to withstand a 
variety of events;  
3) Optimize grid operations to reduce customer energy consumption without negatively 
impacting customers; 
4) Maintain reliable power delivery while accommodating intermittent resources like 
Distributed Energy Resources (DER); 
5) Protect customers’ data and utilities’ systems;  
6) Enable customers and promote customer satisfaction, allowing customers to maintain 
control over their own energy usage; and 
7) Focus on sustainability and integration of clean technologies.4  
 
National Grid’s detailed plans for grid modernization are set forth in DSIP filings. 
 
Many of the envisioned grid modernization capabilities use remote sensor information to either 
function autonomously or in a remotely coordinated manner, which allows grid operators to 
quickly implement changes. While there are significant benefits when operating in a stand-alone 
manner, integrating AMI data with grid-facing capabilities can produce enhanced functionality. 
The following section outlines the business functions which AMI data can enhance over time as 
Grid Modernization investments are made and capabilities evolve: 
 
• System Maintenance and Planning – Enhanced data availability for back-office 
analytics on load growth and distribution infrastructure to better understand upgrade 
requirements to accommodate changing customer usage (e.g., EV adoption, load growth, 
etc.) 
                                                           
 
4 Modern Distribution Grid: Volume 1, U.S. Department of Energy 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 139 of 313


--- Page 175 ---
62 
 
 
• Distribution Automation Technologies: A collection of field devices and back-office 
systems particularly focused on “self-healing grid” and non-customer-impacting energy 
reductions 
 
• Outage Management: Capabilities to increase control room situational awareness about 
customer power outages and restorations  
 
• Operations Optimization – Enhanced capabilities to provide operations with new 
options for DER integration and Demand Response controls 
 
The use cases for AMI enhancements to DSP capabilities are independently listed and described 
below, but National Grid believes that many of the capabilities can be combined in various ways 
over time for even greater collective value. While an extensive exploration of these capabilities 
is beyond the scope of this filing, it is important to highlight them as they support achievement of 
the REV objectives. 
 
System Maintenance and Planning  
 
National Grid has a responsibility to monitor and plan for distributed generation and 
consumption trends that are likely to impact the design, capacity, and operation of the 
distribution system. AMI meters provide enhanced energy information through granular data at 
the individual premise level. Emerging analytic methods applied to this data provide planning 
personnel with new potential insights about 1) Customer Service Quality and 2) Improved 
Forecasting for Operational Analysis and Investment. 
 
Data Quality 
Foundational to National Grid’s System Planning efforts is a solid understanding of field 
equipment configuration. However, due to the dynamic nature of the distribution system, 
reconfiguration can occur frequently, resulting in misaligned configuration data. AMI meters, 
acting as widespread sensors throughout the service territory, can be leveraged to reconcile 
deployed system configurations with back office records and models as follows:  
 
• 
Phase Connectivity Confirmation – In the United States, electricity delivery is 
standardized, with the most common distribution circuits divided into three parallel 
wires, which operate in distinct phases. Grid control equipment and customer load is 
planned to be relatively balanced across each of these phases. Occasionally, actual 
physical connectivity can deviate from plans, which can lead to difficulty in diagnosing 
the location of a connectivity error. As a result, 1) equipment operations may not have the 
fully desired impact or 2) reliability / power quality issues can ensue. AMI meters can be 
used as sensors to confirm phase connectivity and justify appropriate actions to resolve in 
the field or update records. 
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 140 of 313


--- Page 176 ---
63 
 
 
Service Quality & Asset Life 
With more detailed information about customer usage, National Grid can understand and 
improve the quality of service it provides to its customers. National Grid also manages and 
maintains its assets to maximize their useful lives. Unfortunately, legacy tools are limited; 
service quality can generally only be assessed through aggregate reliability metrics and many 
pieces of infrastructure have limited, if any sensor capabilities.  
 
AMI data is able to provide customer specific insights regarding power quality trends and can be 
aggregated in new ways to serve as a proxy for infrastructure usage as follows: 
 
• 
Customer Voltage Violations – Voltage is an important characteristic of power delivery 
and if insufficient, can result in dim lights and other negative impacts to the customer 
experience. In certain scenarios, customers may receive energy at voltage levels outside 
the established standards. National Grid currently investigates and attempts to mitigate 
voltage quality concerns in response to specific customer requests. Using AMI data, 
National Grid can proactively assess power quality characteristics for all customers and 
potentially identify ways in which the grid can be enhanced to eliminate these power 
quality issues. 
 
• 
Customers Experiencing Multiple Interruptions (CEMI) - As discussed above, 
National Grid prioritizes real-time power restorations as part of Outage Management, but 
National Grid also analyzes trends for overall reliability through metrics such as SAIFI, 
CAIDI, MAIFI. While these metrics provide insight to the overall system, they do not 
provide individualized visibility. As AMI is deployed across the country, reliability 
metrics, such as Customers Experiencing Multiple Sustained Interruptions (CEMI), are 
evolving to provide individualized reliability perspectives. CEMI, as the name implies, 
can collect and aggregate AMI initiated power outage notifications to identify which, if 
any, customers have had an exceptional frequency of outages allowing for possible 
mitigation. 
 
• 
Prevent Service Transformer Over-Loading: A critical piece of distribution 
infrastructure is the transformer. As one of the most common devices on the grid, 
transformers are vital in reducing voltage levels from higher, primary voltages along the 
main circuit, to lower secondary voltages for use at a customer premise. Occasionally, 
aggregate usage from customers connected through a specific transformer can exceed 
operating thresholds. Over time, consistent over-loading can reduce the service life of 
transformers or introduce safety risks through failure. AMI data can be aggregated and 
correlated to specific transformers, identifying overload situations. This allows 
transformers to be proactively upgraded to maintain safety and power delivery reliability.  
 
 
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 141 of 313


--- Page 177 ---
64 
 
 
Forecasting, Scenario Modeling, and Impact Assessments 
In addition to historic and current situational assessments, National Grid has a responsibility to 
forecast estimated electrical loads for various purposes. This encompasses generation and load 
balancing to support reliable energy delivery and potential infrastructure replacement / upgrades 
resulting from system expansion and other organic load growth. 
 
In decades past, these forecasts could be effectively generated from a foundation of aggregate, 
circuit-level data and limited customer load profiles gathered through select load research efforts. 
However, the distribution grid is now facing a period of unprecedented change with accelerating 
levels of demand response capabilities, electric vehicles, distributed generation assets and overall 
customer behavior changes. National Grid must be proactive to analyze and accommodate 
potential impacts. 
 
AMI data will be vital in establishing circuit level detailed and accurate profiles of energy 
requirements and infrastructure impacts as follows: 
 
• Load Forecasting Models – A key foundation to all forecasting analysis is a robust data 
set, defining how much energy has been historically used at various times, locations and 
in operating conditions. This has historically been generated based on circuit level meter 
data, combined with general customer information (number of customers on the circuit, 
customer type, etc.). The resulting virtual representations make many assumptions and 
estimates concerning operating characteristics at various points of each circuit. AMI data 
provides significantly higher resolution information regarding voltage drop and energy 
consumption enabling enhanced model accuracy.  
 
• Energy Efficiency / Demand Response Forecasting – Energy Efficiency (EE) and 
Demand Response (DR) programs are powerful tools used to help ensure reliable power 
delivery at times when the grid is most strained. Energy Efficiency programs seek to help 
customers levelize and/or reduce their overall consumption. Demand Response programs 
identify specific peak consumption times and proactively engage customers with a 
request to reduce usage. These programs have traditionally been structured for broad 
participation and efficacy has been based on aggregate load impact. AMI data allows 
program participant energy usage to be analyzed for greater insight about customer 
specific kWh reduction and endurance. This will support the creation of segmented 
programs and incentives which foster greater participation and effectiveness. Further, 
analysis of prior DR event performance can be used assess the impact of DR activation 
for subsequent events with similar characteristics. 
 
• Load Balancing and Infrastructure Impacts – National Grid electrical engineers use 
various tools to assess the ongoing integrity of electricity delivery infrastructure.  
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 142 of 313


--- Page 178 ---
65 
 
 
• As-built Infrastructure - Operations of existing circuits are analyzed to ensure that the 
as-built infrastructure is adequate for the purposes of the connected customers, whose 
usage patterns may have evolved over decades. Various location-specific electrical 
stressors may exist which can gradually and subtly erode asset integrity. These can 
remain undiscovered for years until assets fail prematurely, creating difficulty with root-
cause analysis.  
 
• New Construction – National Grid is obligated to analyze and determine how best to 
provide electricity to newly constructed facilities being served by existing circuits. This 
analysis identifies which phase(s) should be used to ensure balanced electricity delivery. 
New load points can make existing problems worse and potentially accelerate asset 
failure.  
 
AMI data can enable enhanced modelling which will allow engineers to make better 
infrastructure decisions. For as-built infrastructure, engineers will be better equipped to assess 
electrical stress points and take proactive steps which can extend asset life. Additionally, 
engineers can leverage new technology to assess the impacts of new construction on the grid, 
ensuring reliability will be maintained post-construction. 
 
• 
Electric Vehicle Impact Assessment – Electric Vehicles (EV) are an emerging trend 
that can have significant impacts to load profiles and forecasting models. Each EV can 
require significant energy for battery charging, which in aggregate, could negatively 
impact grid performance. National Grid requests EV owners provide installation 
notification to the Company, which allows for proactive load analysis, ensuring 
consistently reliable energy delivery. AMI data will support incorporation of this asset 
class in the following ways: 
 
o Power Quality Impacts – AMI enhanced load profiles will prove enhanced 
forecasting, reducing power quality degradation for downstream customers when 
EVs are charging. This impact will be greater with EVs attempting higher voltage 
“fast-charging”. 
 
o Impact Confirmation – As an evolving technology, with limited real-world 
analysis, National Grid has historically been conservative in estimating 
infrastructure impacts. AMI data will provide National Grid with the information 
to validate and adjust estimates, enabling informed planning of continued EV 
incorporation. 
 
• 
Distributed Energy Resource Impact Assessment – Distributed Energy Resources 
(DER) include a wide variety of emerging technologies, which typically focus on 
distribution grid-connected solar generation and battery storage. These assets have highly 
variable characteristics regarding size (single residence rooftop solar vs multi-MW 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 143 of 313


--- Page 179 ---
66 
 
 
community solar) and dispatchability (solar does not produce at night, production can 
drop by more than ninety percent if a cloud passes over). This variability and 
unpredictability adds significant complexity to National Grid’s load forecasting 
capabilities and implies new strategies for maintaining reliability under differing 
operating conditions. AMI provides significant insight regarding DER load forecasting: 
 
o Hosting Capacity / Interconnection Analysis – Customers wanting a DER must 
submit an application to National Grid detailing electrical characteristics of the 
proposed asset to be interconnected with the grid. National Grid must then 
perform an assessment of power quality impacts on the target circuit to ensure 
that it has sufficient capacity to host the DER asset. In response to these 
applications, National Grid can leverage AMI enabled load profiles to establish a 
more precise model and less conservative assessment of grid impacts  
 
o Impact Confirmation – Each DER that is interconnected with the grid will have 
a unique operational profile concerning energy generation potential to the grid.. 
Leveraging AMI data for all meters of a given circuit allows for synthesis of DER 
and non-DER loads, generating a comprehensive load profile and holistic 
assessment of impacts.  
 
Distribution Automation Technologies 
 
Much of National Grid’s electricity distribution infrastructure is designed and built around 
analog technologies, which possess a proven history and strong operational dependability. 
However, digital technologies have had a transformational impact on many industries; with the 
electricity distribution encountering similar impacts. New digital sensors, field devices, and 
back-office systems provide National Grid’s Control Center staff with never-before-available 
capabilities to monitor and control devices in the field. 
 
Volt / VAR Optimization (“VVO”) 
VVO is a capability that remotely coordinates various field devices to manage voltage along a 
distribution circuit, reduce voltage at the substation while ensuring that no customers have power 
quality issues and reduce grid-wide power generation needs. AMI can be used to enhance VVO 
by 1) optimizing field device placement, 2) using high-resolution voltage data to improve load 
profile estimates, and 3) enabling bellwether meter feedback loops. 
 
At its core, using a variety of technologies, VVO can perform the following: 
 
• 
Voltage Stabilization – Utilize sensors and devices in the field to monitor voltage levels 
at various locations along the circuit and dynamically activate devices (e.g. Capacitor 
Banks and Voltage Regulators), which can progressively increase or decrease voltage 
levels at locations further away from the substation.  
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 144 of 313


--- Page 180 ---
67 
 
 
 
• 
Substation Voltage Reduction – Once Voltage has been stabilized and increased 
towards the end of circuit, power quality analytics can be performed, and as applicable, 
devices in the substation (Load Tap Changers) can be remotely adjusted to lower the 
voltage for the entire circuit.  
 
These functions can operate without historically influenced or real-time AMI data, but the AMI 
data provides for more robust analytical context and situational awareness: 
 
• Optimize Field Device Placement – AMI data can be leveraged when new circuits are 
being selected for VVO enhancement. At a minimum, voltage violations can be 
proactively identified and circuits with higher prevalence can be prioritized for 
mitigation. Once a given circuit has been identified, AMI data can establish voltage 
behaviors, allowing National Grid engineers to identify field device deployment locations 
to minimize customer impact. 
 
• Improved Load Profiles – VVO coordination functions operate based on a 
reconciliation of real-time measurements compared against estimated voltage erosion 
profiles (known as Load Profiles). Absent AMI data, load profiles are usually generated 
from substation measurements and augmented with data approximations regarding 
volumes and types of customers on a given line. AMI data facilitates enhanced load 
profiles which reflect actual voltage erosion as measured by the meters. Using more 
precise load profiles allows for more aggressive substation voltage reduction while 
minimizing the risk of voltage violations for customers. 
 
• Bellwether Meter Feedback – VVO functions ultimately provide the greatest value to 
customers when voltage can be lowered by the maximum amount while also ensuring 
that customer power quality is not compromised. A select group of customers at the end 
of upgraded circuits could potentially experience voltage problems. However, by 
designating their AMI meters as “bellwether” meters, the voltage from these meters is a 
priority constraint on the VVO function. As a result, a minimum acceptable power 
quality for all customers is maintained while allowing voltage to be managed as 
aggressively as possible. 
 
Fault Location, Isolation, and Service Restoration (“FLISR”) 
FLISR is a capability that remotely coordinates various field devices to restore power to outage-
impacted customers within minutes or even seconds. AMI can enhance FLISR with 1) enhanced 
sensors and remote monitoring, 2) expanded analytics to maximize potential customer 
restorations, and 3) alerts confirming restorations and detecting possible nested outages. 
 
Using various technologies, FLISR is comprised of three primary functions: 
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 145 of 313


--- Page 181 ---
68 
 
 
1. Fault Location: uses field sensors to monitor operating conditions and measure possible 
electrical fault characteristics to calculate the most probable location of a damaged 
circuit. 
 
2. Isolation: opens circuit switches upstream and downstream of the calculated fault 
location using analytics and remotely operated switches in an effort to protect field crews 
and the distribution infrastructure from rogue electrical current. 
 
3. Service Restoration: closes tie switches using other analytics and remote option to 
reenergize parallel unimpacted circuits, returning service for end-of-circuit fault-
impacted customers.  
 
These functions can operate without historical or real-time AMI data; however, AMI data 
provides far more robust analytical context and situational awareness: 
 
1. Fault Current Detection: AMI devices can act as remote, grid-connected sensors. When 
AMI meters detect certain abnormal operating conditions, the meters can transmit alert 
notifications to back-office systems. Identifying the AMI meters that have detected power 
surges can better refine fault location estimates. 
 
2. Load Transfer Impact Assessment: AMI data can provide a rich data set of detailed 
customer usage data for fault-impacted segments of a given circuit. This data can be 
considered in combination with real-time operational characteristics to assess restoration 
options. Analysis can confirm and, in some cases, prioritize which unimpacted potential 
tie circuit can sustain the reenergization of impacted customers. 
 
3. Power Status Verification: During power restoration, the utility can use AMI 
capabilities to remotely connect with meters to confirm service status. In some instances, 
additional damage to a circuit may prevent a subset of customers from service restoration. 
Therefore, by proactively communicating with the AMI meters, National Grid will be 
able to quickly identify these nested outages and deploy crews as needed. 
 
Outage Management  
 
Outage Management is a capability at the core of National Grid’s operational framework. When 
outages occur, particularly as part of large storms, restoring power becomes the top priority. 
Personnel from all areas of the company are leveraged to ensure that power is restored as quickly 
and safely as possible. To this end, many National Grid staff members have “storm duty” to 
assist in performing restoration efforts. Crew surveys, combined with well-trained customers 
who call to report outages, represent a tried and true method for situational awareness. 
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 146 of 313


--- Page 182 ---
69 
 
 
In an AMI-enabled future, each meter can function as an end-point power sensor, supporting 
outage data aggregation and enhanced control as follows: 
 
• 
Real-Time Outage Notifications – AMI meters include several real-time alert 
capabilities, including communication identifying a power outage at a specific premise. 
When aggregated, these outage notifications can be mapped in real-time and displayed 
for control room operators to create detailed situational awareness, supporting efficient 
dispatching of personnel. This is particularly useful for smaller, targeted groups of 
customers experiencing an outage (e.g. downstream of a blown fuse, downstream of a 
blown transformer, etc.) 
 
• 
Power Problems on Customer Side of the Meter – In some cases, customers will report 
an outage, yet once a National Grid technician is deployed to investigate the problem no 
longer persists or is determined to be out of the scope of National Grid’s mitigation 
responsibility (e.g., customer wiring, customer circuit breaker, etc.). With remote 
communications to AMI meters, National Grid call center representatives can confirm 
power status with a customer in real-time. For instances where the problem is on the 
customer side of the meter, field crews can avoid field visits and customers are able to 
more quickly engage with an electrician who can help resolve their issues.  
 
• 
Emergency Load Shedding – In certain extreme situations, the grid can become 
destabilized due to an imbalance between electrical generation and consumption. If this 
balance is not restored quickly, widespread blackouts can impact large numbers of 
customers. Traditionally, load shedding can be initiated through manual calls to large 
customers or by disconnecting specific circuits at the substation. AMI meters provide an 
additional tool to grid operators by allowing targeted groups of customers to be 
disconnected for load shed events. Unlike traditional customer load shedding, remote 
AMI disconnects can be performed quicker and more efficiently. Similarly, once the 
situation is resolved, these customers can be quickly reconnected. With targeted load 
shedding, the number of potentially impacted customers can be minimized. 
 
Operations Optimization 
 
Historically, Outage Management was the primary responsibility of the Distribution Control 
Center. While staffed twenty-four hours a day, seven days a week and ready to respond to 
outages in real-time, Outage Management activities represent a small portion of this overall time. 
As new field devices are deployed to the grid and new back-office systems become capable of 
coordinating functions, the Distribution Control Center has an emerging mission to optimize the 
operating conditions of the grid. 
 
Grid modernization capabilities are one approach to optimization, but these largely represent 
National Grid’s autonomous ability to monitor and control assets that it owns. Incorporation of 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 147 of 313


--- Page 183 ---
70 
 
 
other assets such as DR, DER, and EV present additional capabilities which can be proactively 
initiated to advance the optimization objective, but customer owned asset communications for 
monitoring and controlling these assets have historically be difficult to manage. 
 
Unlike grid modernization assets which require frequent, high speed communications with back-
office system to enable their core functions, customer assets like DR, DER, and EV have 
different operational characteristics. These device types can be periodically monitored and 
support simple controls such as load shedding, with characteristics that highly align to 
communication qualities inherent to AMI. These monitor and control functions could combine 
the following three capabilities of the AMI infrastructure: 
 
• Back office meter communication – Specialized commands and/or meter level apps 
could be transmitted to the meter for localized control. 
 
• Grid Edge Computing – Specialized apps could be deployed to target meters where DR, 
DERs, and/or EVs are known to reside; apps could perform local functions to align 
customer asset performance with utility optimization objectives. 
 
• Localized Meter Communications – Each meter has an array of short-range 
communications technologies that can be used communicate between the meter and DR, 
DERs, and EVs; this local communication can convey simple controls like load shedding 
activation, injection curtailment, or voltage / frequency point adjustment.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“National Grid” or the “Company”) is 
pleased to provide this “Customer Engagement Plan” or the “Plan” in support of its Report1 
seeking approval of full-scale deployment of advanced metering infrastructure (“AMI” or 
“smart meter” technology). The Company is focused on delivering a simplified customer 
experience, making the benefits enabled by smart meters intuitive and the functionality easy to 
manage. 
The value of smart meter technology lies in the granularity and timeliness of the data it 
provides. This increased information will help to correlate energy usage directly to the cost of 
energy, fostering increased customer control over energy management, and incentivizing 
changes to energy usage through bill savings. In addition, access to this energy usage 
information supports system efficiency by driving step changes in peak energy reduction among 
customers and animating the market for further system-efficiency solutions.   
National Grid’s Vision for the Customer in the New Energy Landscape 
The Company found it must respond to an expanding range of customer needs for visibility, 
control, choice, and convenience in their energy experience. Based on recent surveys and 
industry intelligence, National Grid customers: 
 
 Expect their energy experience to be affordable; 
 Show a willingness to alter energy use to achieve savings; 
 Want to easily access their energy usage data from a variety of channels; 
 Use connected devices for greater control of the power coming into their 
homes; 
 Request tailored, personalized choices for energy consumption options; and  
 Need convenient solutions.  
 
Grounded by these findings, the Company launched a Customer Experience Transformation 
project, an omni-channel, digital engagement strategy focused on how the Company interacts, 
serves, and communicates with its customers. With multiple work streams dedicated to 
executing this multi-year initiative, this project, coupled with smart meter deployment, will 
empower and enable enhanced customer energy management.  
 
 
                                                          
1 See Cases 17-E-0238 and 17-G-0239, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules 
and Regulations of Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid for Electric and Gas Service (“Rate 
Case Proceeding”), Report of Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid on the Proposed 
Implementation of Advanced Metering Infrastructure (filed November 15, 2018) (the “AMI Report”).

Development of the Customer Engagement Plan 
To build this Customer Engagement Plan for smart meter deployment, the Company relied on: 
1) internal and external learnings, such as smart meter pilot programs, surveys, and research; 
and 2) a comprehensive collaborative process.  
Internally, the Company used its primary research learnings from two smart meter pilot 
programs and two wide-ranging customer surveys. National Grid and its Massachusetts 
affiliates conducted AMI pilots in Clifton Park, New York and Worcester, Massachusetts, 
respectively, to test AMI meters and to better understand customer needs and experiences 
with an AMI deployment. The Company also relied on two targeted customer surveys, which 
were based on the responses of 6,000 customers, representative of the customer base in New 
York, Massachusetts, and Rhode Island. As the Customer Engagement Plan continues to evolve 
throughout AMI deployment, the Company will carry out additional surveys to gauge 
deployment and post-deployment customer perspectives and make adjustments as needed. 
Externally, the Company also used secondary research sources, including extensive consumer 
research performed by the Smart Energy Consumer Collaborative, customer engagement plans 
from peer utilities, and industry reports to validate its findings. 
In addition, during the period of April through August 2018, the Company hosted eight formal 
AMI collaborative sessions consisting of three large multi-topic sessions and five smaller group 
meetings to focus on discrete smart meter deployment issues.  Four of the small-group sessions 
focused on the development and refinement of this Customer Engagement Plan, proposed 
metrics, and associated impacts to the Company’s AMI Business Case.  The sessions afforded 
the Company the opportunity to work with Staff and interested parties, a total of seventeen 
stakeholder groups, to not only explain elements of the plan, but also obtain their valuable 
input and feedback while developing certain components of the Plan.   
Phases of Customer Education and Engagement 
Overall, the objective of the Customer Engagement Plan is to inform and educate National Grid 
customers on the benefits of smart meters to increase participation in accepting the new 
technology and the eventual adoption of new services and innovative rates. Using the learnings 
from its research and collaborative sessions, the Company developed a three-phased approach 
to customer education and engagement as outlined in Section 4.  
Specifically, in Phase One (Awareness), the Company will build an internal foundation of assets 
to support communication and engagement activities, educate and train internal resources, and 
begin a territory-wide customer and stakeholder outreach effort to build smart meter 
awareness, generate interest prior to meter installation, and address customer concerns.  
In Phase Two (Deployment), the Company plans to build on the broad education initiated in 
Phase One and narrow the focus of communication toward individual customers with 
information that will guide customers through the day of meter installation, including the 
timeline of events, what to expect, and alternate choices available. 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 155 of 313


--- Page 191 ---
6 of 144 
Finally, for Phase Three (Empowerment and Enablement), after smart meters are installed, 
customers will be able to take full advantage of the enhanced Customer Energy Management 
Platform, where more granular energy usage data will be available, as well as future innovative 
pricing plans enabled by AMI meters. The sustaining nature of this phase will focus on 
empowering and enabling customers, including how to gain or share access to their energy 
data, how to interpret their energy consumption, and how to manage their energy usage to 
reduce energy and cost. This phase will also introduce customers to how AMI meters can 
animate the energy markets, connecting customers to third-party vendors who can help 
supplement customer needs with new and innovative products and services that are 
incremental to the new utility-provided customer service offerings enabled by AMI.  
Innovative Rate Design 
The Company’s current rate structure of charging basic residential customers a flat, volumetric 
charge plus a fixed monthly customer charge only slightly reflects how most electric system 
costs are incurred, leading to economic inefficiencies and inaccurate price signals. The Company 
addresses this misalignment in Section 5 by outlining its intention to improve supply rates 
based on lessons learned from pilot projects. For example, drawing from its affiliates’ pilot in 
Worcester, the Company proposes an optional time-varying pricing (“TVP”) rate structure, 
including time-of-use (“TOU”) and critical peak pricing (“CPP”) rate components.  Experience in 
Worcester has shown that such an approach can produce savings for customers. However, the 
Company understands this is a significant shift from traditional rate models, which is why 
customers will have the option to stay on the Company’s standard rates.  In addition, the 
Company proposes a bill guarantee for all customers during the first year under the new TVP 
rate.  This means that if the customer bill is greater with the TOU/CPP rate based on when 
energy was consumed, compared with what the customer's bill traditionally would have been 
with fixed pricing rates, the customer will receive a bill credit. For low-income customers, the 
bill guarantee will extend for an additional two years on a graduated basis.   
Metrics of Success 
To determine the success of National Grid’s Customer Engagement Plan, the Company will 
measure customer awareness of AMI benefits and tools, customers’ usage of AMI tools, and 
customer reception of the pricing program. As set forth in Section 6, National Grid has created a 
number of customer engagement metrics to measure awareness, 
enablement/empowerment/tools, and pricing rates.  
Data Access, Sharing, and Privacy 
With the increased frequency of data uploaded from AMI meters, the Company will process 
more data and, thus, must continue to take appropriate precautions and safeguards to ensure 
customer privacy. The Company’s core data privacy principles regarding regular audits, lifecycle 
data management, data governance, and data retention, underscore the Company’s 
commitment to protect customer data. The Company also maintains breach response and 
notification event plans as part of its commitment to customers.  
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 156 of 313


--- Page 192 ---
7 of 144 
In all, National Grid’s Customer Engagement Plan for smart meter implementation supports its 
vision to enable greater customer choice and savings in the new modern energy landscape. This 
Customer Engagement Plan is based on robust collaborative feedback and on lessons learned 
from smart meter pilots. The resulting three-phased approach of AMI Awareness, Deployment, 
and Customer Empowerment and Enablement, aims to increase customer participation and 
ensure further adoption of smart meters and innovative rate designs in furtherance of the 
Commission’s Reforming the Energy Vision (“REV”) objectives and the State’s clean-energy 
goals. This Plan is a roadmap that will guide the Company and customers throughout smart 
meter deployment, turning a complex, multifaceted program into an opportunity for a valuable, 
positive customer engagement.

NATIONAL GRID’S VISION FOR THE CUSTOMER IN THE NEW 
ENERGY LANDSCAPE 
2.1 
Overview 
Today’s customers expect more from their utility. Analysis of industry research suggests that, in 
general, customers not only expect the utility to provide affordable, reliable, and safe energy, 
but also increasingly expect access to actionable information, greater choice and control over 
their energy use, and delivery of energy services in a simple and convenient way. National Grid 
will deliver on these expectations for a modern energy experience by enabling its electric and 
gas customers with insights into their energy consumption patterns, offering more innovative 
energy management and pricing options (e.g., time-of-use rates, critical peak pricing), and 
facilitating the integration of smarter devices and distributed energy resources (“DER”), all 
while maintaining affordable, safe, and reliable energy service.  
As articulated in the Company’s AMI Report, National Grid is proposing to implement AMI 
across its service territory to enable its customers with the knowledge and tools needed to 
better inform their energy decisions and manage their energy costs. AMI will help establish a 
cleaner, more resilient and efficient system, consistent with the Commission’s REV objectives, 
New York State’s clean-energy goals, and the Company’s own recently released clean-energy 
vision – the Northeast 80x50 Pathway.2 Supported by this Customer Engagement Plan, smart 
meter deployment will help empower the Company’s customers to make more informed 
energy choices, animate the market for third parties to develop new customer-focused 
products and services, and further the Company’s progression as the distributed system 
platform (“DSP”) provider as articulated in its Distributed System Implementation Plans 
(“DSIP”). In short, deployment of AMI is a significant enhancement to the Company’s current 
metering environment, and one that enables grid modernization, increased personalized 
information for each customer, and improvement of the overall customer experience. It is an 
opportunity the Company welcomes to deepen its relationship with customers to help deliver 
more value at the household/business, town, and State levels. 
 
2.2 
Frameworks 
Prior to the commencement of REV, National Grid adopted Connect21 – a strategic framework 
for a 21st century utility that, among other priorities, highlights developing customized solutions 
for customers and for the Company to become a market enabler that facilitates the efficient 
integration of third-party resources by sharing key information with customers and market 
participants and sending appropriate price signals. These tenets are echoed throughout REV, 
and the Company believes AMI, when paired with a comprehensive customer engagement

strategy, is the right framework upon which customers are better enabled to take meaningful 
action, as well as cost-effectively connect with third-party market offerings, services, and 
solutions. Additionally, National Grid has embarked on a comprehensive Customer Experience 
Transformation initiative that aims to fundamentally change how the Company interacts, 
services, and communicates with customers via a more integrated digital approach, including a 
responsive website, social media outreach, and Customer Service Center. This initiative is 
structured around delivering maximum customer value during the “Moments that Matter,” as 
illustrated in Figure 2-1 below.  
 
Figure 2-1: Customer Experience Transformation – Customer “Moments that Matter”

these moments that matter for customers. More information and application of the Customer 
Experience Transformation initiative to the Customer Engagement Plan is provided in Section 
2.4: Customer Experience Transformation. 
 
2.3 
AMI Enables the Customer’s Modern Energy Experience 
Retail consumers today are enjoying new and higher levels of service and convenience thanks 
to the application of advanced technology and big data across industries. Understanding how 
customers’ diverse and evolving needs manifest within their utility experience is key to 
developing a more modern energy experience that creates sustainable value. While reliability 
and affordability remain the foundation of the Company’s services for its customers, the 
Company has found through its research that it must respond to an expanding range of 
customer needs for visibility, control, choice, and convenience in their energy experience. 
According to an extensive study of customer expectations, including a survey of customers 
across National Grid’s service territory (and the service territories of its Massachusetts and 
Rhode Island affiliates), the customer’s modern energy experience, enabled by AMI, must 
address numerous customer needs. 
Affordability: When given the right tools, customers will take a more active role to minimize 
total energy costs. For example, customers have shown a willingness to temporarily adjust or 
shift energy use in return for bill credits or other incentives, as indicated by customer research,3 
the Company’s experience with its Clifton Park REV Demonstration Project (“Clifton Park 
                                                          
3 National Grid, Value Proposition Research: A Study of 3 Energy Solution Areas (2017).  
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 159 of 313


--- Page 195 ---
10 of 144 
Demonstration”) and, its affiliates’ experience with the smart grid pilot in Worcester, 
Massachusetts (“Worcester Pilot”).4  
Visibility: Customers express a need for personalized insights and information on specific 
actions that can help reduce their usage.5 They also expect this information to be easily 
accessible and available through numerous channels (e.g., the Company’s website, mailings, 
Customer Service Center). More visible usage and cost information enables better customer 
energy management and helps achieve cost savings.6 This is particularly valuable in a future 
environment where default pricing plans evolve from a traditional flat rate to a time-varying 
pricing (“TVP”) construct. 
Control: Customers want better control over how and when they use power in their homes, 
businesses, and facilities.7 A majority of both residential and commercial customers express 
interest in tools and devices that can help them manage their energy use, such as "connected" 
thermostats that can be controlled remotely and learn a customer’s habits and preferences 
based upon usage patterns.8 The Company has already taken initial steps in this field through its 
existing multi-sector energy efficiency (“EE”) and Dynamic Load Management (“DLM”) 
programs and it expects AMI to enable this need further through the provision of personalized, 
granular usage data.   
Choice: Greater levels of choice empower customers – from home/business energy 
management to clean energy solutions to pricing.9 Customers also want the ability to choose 
what communication they receive, when they receive it, and through which channels, in order 
to best personalize their modern energy experience. While consumers express an interest in 
greater levels of choice, behavioral science research also suggests that overloading consumers 
with too many choices can result in decision inertia and less satisfaction with an eventual 
choice.10 Default options help facilitate better customer decision-making as opt-out settings 
drive higher participation levels, such as with dynamic pricing plans like TVP.11,12

Convenience: Building upon “Choice,” the Company recognizes that a key aspect of customer 
convenience is delivering information and solutions to customers through their most preferred 
channels. In today’s connected world, this requires an increased focus on web-based and 
mobile solutions, allowing customers to manage and optimize energy usage via an “anytime, 
anywhere” experience. However, National Grid is cognizant that not all customers share a 
preference for engagement via digital solutions, and thus a multi-channel outreach strategy 
throughout smart meter deployment is critical.  
In expanding on convenience, automation offers further decision optimization by simplifying or 
reducing repeatable actions; for example, the enablement of “auto-bill pay” and/or "set and 
forget" features. An industry study on "The New Energy Consumer" showed that 60 percent of 
those surveyed would be interested in technology that could completely automate the 
management of their electricity or gas use.13 This type of automation is not fully utilized 
without an enabling AMI foundation and a platform that can connect with smart devices. 
  
2.4 
Customer Experience Transformation 
2.4.1 Overview 
National Grid has embarked on a comprehensive Customer Experience Transformation 
initiative that aims to fundamentally change how the Company interacts, serves, and 
communicates with customers via an omni-channel, digital engagement strategy. Although the 
Company is cognizant of the need for a multi-channel customer engagement strategy to meet 
the needs of its diverse customer base during the deployment of smart meters, it is also aware 
of the growing trends toward customers’ engagement with their utility via digital channels. As 
New York continues to enable more of its citizens with access to high-speed broadband 
internet, coupled with the increasing prevalence of smart mobile phones and tablets, the 
Company is preparing to deliver an enhanced digital experience for customers with the 
deployment of smart meters.   
This multi-year initiative seeks to continuously transform the customer experience for 
customers by implementing numerous capabilities, technologies, and program management 
strategies to enable a best-in-class customer experience. The initiative’s key focus areas, aimed 
at delivering maximum customer value during “moments that matter” (see Figure 2-1), include: 
 Move in and move out; 
 Distributed generation/new connections (electric and gas);

The deployment of smart meters offers opportunities for more sustainable and deeper

deliver:  
 Improved awareness of consumer needs and behaviors; 
 A 360° view of the customer;

 Personalized solutions.  
 
2.4.2 Customer Experience Transformation Work Streams

2.4.3 AMI-Enabled Enhancements 
AMI will enhance the Company’s capability to deliver on key focus areas by helping to achieve 
previously noted customer benefits.  This is particularly relevant to enabling both the “My 
Account/Preference Management” and “Customer Personalization Management” work streams 
described in additional detail below. Implementing AMI will allow the Company to begin 
receiving more frequent and granular customer energy consumption data. The more detailed 
information will immediately enable the Company to establish an improved experience for all 
customers built on personalized feedback and energy management opportunities.  
Furthermore, the detailed energy insights will help expand choice in how customers receive and 
act on that valuable information, including the authorized sharing of that information with 
qualified third-party solution providers. Additionally, the combination of obtaining granular AMI 
data with the implementation of foundational Customer Experience Transformation capabilities 
will best prepare and continuously assist customers in participating in future time-varying price 
(“TVP”) rate plans.

DEVELOPMENT OF THE CUSTOMER ENGAGEMENT PLAN 
In building its Customer Engagement Plan, the Company looked to prior filings from peer 
utilities, such as Consolidated Edison Company of New York, Inc. (“Con Edison”) and Orange and 
Rockland Utilities, Inc. (“O&R”), to develop and refine its approach to smart meter customer 
engagement.  The Company also used lessons learned from the Clifton Park Demonstration and 
its affiliates’ experience with the Worcester Pilot. The pilots allowed National Grid and its 
affiliates to test marketing and outreach material, understand customer needs, measure 
customer sentiments, and obtain other learnings to improve the customer experience for broad 
AMI deployment in New York.  Lastly, the Company conducted surveys and external research, 
providing insight into customer preferences, as well as feedback that informed the Plan. More 
details on these efforts are discussed in Appendix C. 
 
3.1 
Collaborative Process and Learnings 
National Grid also leaned heavily on the input and feedback gained from AMI Collaborative 
sessions initiated as part of the 2017 NMPC Rate Case Joint Proposal14 to develop the Customer 
Engagement Plan. The goal of the collaborative sessions was for the Company to share and 
receive feedback on its proposed customer engagement approach with Department of Public 
Service (“DPS”) Staff, intervenors, and other interested parties. Multiple sessions were held to 
discuss the AMI Awareness, Deployment, and Empowerment and Engagement phases for AMI 
implementation. The sessions had a robust approach in which the Company shared experiences 
from demonstrations, lessons learned, and future plans. The multiple perspectives lent a 
significant amount of experience and insight that helped ensure a focus on effective and 
targeted messaging. Feedback during these sessions has helped to strengthen and bolster the 
Customer Engagement Plan. 
 
3.2 Collaborative Sessions 
National Grid held AMI Collaborative sessions between April and August 2018. The large 
collaborative sessions took place on April 27, 2018, August 1-2, 2018, and August 30, 2018, and 
included topics related to the AMI Business Case, Benefit-Cost Analysis (“BCA”), developing the 
Customer Engagement Plan, and proposed metrics. In addition, National Grid hosted four 
customer engagement focused small collaborative working group sessions. The first small-
group session was held on May 24, and served to help shape topics and content for the 
remaining three sessions.

channels from pre-deployment communications to include advocacy groups, 
community events, and elected officials. 
 Develop an interactive mobile “hub” to build awareness of tools and 
complement other energy saving recommendations (as discussed in 
Collaborative, the mobile hub could move throughout the territory during all 
phases of AMI implementation). 
 Integrated web experience for all energy related information. 
 Make the path to late adoption of smart meters simple to allow customers 
that previously opted out to participate at a later time. 
 Develop tools like a bill calculator to aid customers in understanding the 
financial changes associated with new rates.

PHASES OF CUSTOMER EDUCATION AND ENGAGEMENT 
The overall objective of the Customer Engagement Plan is to transparently inform and educate 
National Grid customers on the benefits of smart meters, in order to increase participation in 
the acceptance of new smart meters and future introduction of new services and innovative 
rates. The increased granularity of usage data and reduced data latency enabled by smart 
meters, coupled with products and services designed to take advantage of this information, will 
enhance customers’ energy experience now and in the future, providing greater control, 
convenience and choice. In order to achieve this, National Grid will focus on 1) building 
awareness, transparency, and interest through education and outreach; 2) managing the 
customer experience through AMI meter deployment to foster customer participation; and 3) 
engaging customers to access new tools and options that will unlock the benefits of more 
actionable information and enhanced energy management, new and innovative pricing options, 
access to third-party services, enhanced customer alerting and personalization tools, and 
enablement of smart-home devices. 
Throughout this plan, the Company references education and outreach materials including 
samples from its AMI pilots in both Clifton Park, NY and Worcester, MA.  For the full 
deployment of smart meters, the Company will develop new materials to support a service 
territory-wide campaign that incorporates the valuable learnings from the Collaborative 
sessions and discussions, stakeholder feedback, industry best practices, and pilots.  The 
education and outreach will be aligned with deployment so that customers receive timely 
information that coincides with receipt of their new meter. 
National Grid has developed a robust education and engagement plan structured around three 
distinct phases and messages: customer AMI awareness prior to deployment; the customer 
experience through deployment; and continued empowerment and enablement following 
deployment and in preparation for the introduction of TVP.   
 Phase One (AMI Awareness): The Company will build an internal foundation of resources 
to support communication and engagement activities, educate and train internal 
employees, and initiate a territory-wide customer and stakeholder outreach effort to 
transparently build awareness of the smart metering program, generate interest and 
support, and proactively address customer concerns prior to meter installation. 
 Phase Two (Deployment): The Company plans to build on the broad education initiated 
in Phase One and narrow the focus of communication toward individual customers with 
information that will guide customers leading up to and through the day of smart meter 
installation, including the timeline of events, what to expect, and alternate choices 
available. 
 Phase Three (Customer Empowerment and Enablement): After smart meters are 
installed, customers will be able to take full advantage of the enhanced Customer 
Energy Management Platform and future TVP rate plans enabled by AMI meters. The 
sustaining nature of this phase will focus on empowering and enabling customers 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 168 of 313


--- Page 204 ---
19 of 144 
including how to access to their interval energy data, how to interpret their energy 
consumption, and how to manage/optimize their energy usage to reduce energy and 
costs. This phase will also introduce customers to how smart meters can animate the 
energy markets by connecting customers (e.g., via Green Button Connect) to third-party 
vendors who can help supplement customer needs with new and innovative products 
and services that are incremental to those established by the Company upon 
deployment.  
 
4.1 Customer Outreach and Education  
As illustrated in Figure 4-1, this phased approach will support the customer journey and will 
include specific marketing strategies with defined messages, audiences, channels, and 
educational materials to generate customer interest, increase customer acceptance, and drive 
customer engagement. The materials will reach a diverse audience and be presented with 
consumer-friendly messaging to allow for easily understandable text and images. 
 
Figure 4-1: Customer Outreach and Education Timeline 
 
A key objective for all phases will be to maximize the number of customers who have access to 
the benefits of the new smart meters. However, should a customer not wish to receive these 
benefits, they may choose to opt out. A customer may choose to not have a new smart meter 
installed (“meter opt-out”) or, when a smart meter is installed, the customer may opt-out of a 
future innovative rate/pricing plan (“rate opt-out”). Because new rates will be introduced on a 
staggered basis after phased meter installation has occurred, most of the initial focus of this 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 169 of 313


--- Page 205 ---
20 of 144 
Customer Engagement Plan will be on limiting meter opt-outs.15 Limiting rate opt-outs and 
encouraging meter opt-in of customers who had previously opted out of receiving an AMI 
meter will be the focus of the education ‘gap year’ between meter deployment and new rates 
becoming effective.  
Throughout each phase, the Company will apply a “Listen, Test, Learn” methodology that was 
deemed useful during the Worcester Pilot to ensure the messages are being received by 
customers as expected. The “Listen, Test, Learn” methodology will be supported by ongoing 
customer surveys that the company will conduct to continue refining the messages and 
outreach strategies. Messages that are not resonating will be adjusted and redeployed.  Metrics 
to understand and proactively manage customer engagement performance throughout the 
entire experience are described in detail in Section 6.  
 
4.1.1 Customer Choice and Opt-out 
National Grid is committed to customer choice. During all phases of deployment, customers will 
have the opportunity to decline the receipt of an AMI meter or, if an AMI meter exists, opt out 
of a future innovative rate/pricing plan. A key to the successful achievement of broader 
Company, local, regional, and New York state clean-energy goals will be the adoption of the 
smart meter and associated TVP-structured rates. Both behavioral science and the Company’s 
pilot experiences show that customers are more likely to stay with a default as opposed to 
actively making a change.16 Therefore, the Company has adopted an opt-out strategy for both 
meter installation and default TVP-structured rate plans. This approach is further validated by 
the Company’s expectation that customers and third-party market players will both ultimately 
benefit from both the granular usage data collected by AMI meters and the flexibility the 
meters provide in delivering innovative rates/pricing plans. 
Customers will be able to choose to opt out of a smart meter at any time.  If a customer 
chooses to opt out of a meter, the Company will seek to understand the reason for non-
participation. This will allow the Company to focus additional resources toward other regions, 
sectors, or specific customer groups that may be at risk of opting out. Customers choosing to 
opt out of a smart meter will initially receive additional education on the benefits they would 
likely forego by not participating based on their access to features and services requiring smart 
meter data (e.g., more personalized usage insights and bill-saving opportunities with new TVP 
plans). Customers will also be made aware that meter reading fees will be incurred if they opt 
out of receiving an AMI meter, similar to the previous automated meter reading (“AMR”) 
                                                          
15 As described in the Innovative Rate Design section, customers will receive a year of education 
between when their AMI meter is installed and new pricing plans take effect.  
16 A description of the Company’s opt-out proposal for Innovative Rates is provided in Section 5.

4.1.2 Understanding Customer Preferences and Future Surveys 
Although significant customer research has been undertaken in the past, National Grid is 
committed to understanding evolving customer preferences and is continuously collecting 
customer feedback and opinions on its ongoing operations, services, programs, and offerings. 
Instruments such as surveys are important tools to measure customer feedback and can be 
collected through a variety of formats, including online surveys, mail surveys, telephone 
surveys, in-person focus groups, online focus groups, and customer forums. The feedback from 
ongoing studies enhance the Company’s overarching “Listen, Test, Learn” approach envisioned 
                                                          
17 Niagara Mohawk Tariff https://www2.dps.ny.gov/ETS/jobs/display/download/2803455.pdf pg.123. 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 171 of 313


--- Page 207 ---
22 of 144 
for smart meter deployment, and aid in continually improving the customer experience.  
Additionally, as the Company continues its solicitation of customer feedback within its Clifton 
Park pilot, it will integrate learnings/insights as appropriate for service territory-wide 
deployment. 
Upon approval, future research/studies are planned to gain additional insights to help achieve 
the following objectives: 
1. Monitor customers’ awareness of smart meters and their overall engagement 
throughout all three phases. 
2. Optimize communications throughout the entire customer engagement journey to 
prioritize transparency with customers and to help mitigate meter opt outs.   
3. Track customer satisfaction across all three engagement phases. 
4. Identify customer attitudes and perspectives toward TVP structures in order to assist 
with detailed design of future pricing plans and to ensure maximum customer 
participation and benefits. 
5. Gauge customer interest in potential new service offerings to inform future design and 
implementation. 
As the Company deploys smart meters across its service territory, it will establish cohorts of 
customers18 to continuously track (either annually or twice-annually19) over the course of the 
three customer engagement phases. This structure enables the ability for the Company to 
develop valuable baselines prior to deployment, measure any changes post-deployment, help 
identify valuable trends, and, most importantly, continue tracking customer insights and 
feedback over time, especially as TVP plans are introduced.  Furthermore, it allows the 
Company to generate insights about the experiences that customers are having as they are 
having them, which are then translated quickly into needed enhancements or revisions in the 
Company’s overall outreach and education efforts.  The progression of the insight gathering will 
generally follow the timeline described below.  
Phase One – Awareness 
 Commercial and Industrial Customer Smart Meter Usage (early Phase One): Leveraging 
existing organizational knowledge from customer and community managers, 
jurisdictional liaisons, and account managers to analyze customer usage of existing 
interval meter technology to supplement future engagement strategies to this customer 
segment across all three phases.  The Company will also look to host discussions with 
individual commercial and industrial customers for direct insights and feedback.
