 Customer Engagement Communications Testing (early-mid Phase One): Testing different 
messages to maximize the impact of collateral development and dissemination. This 
effort will be refreshed as needed during Phases Two and Three to update education 
and outreach strategies based on the findings obtained. 
 Customer Awareness and Attitudes on Smart Meters and TVP Rate Plans (mid-late Phase 
One): A series of baseline measurement studies geared at understanding customer 
awareness of, and attitudes towards, smart meters, benefits, accompanying insights and 
tools (e.g., Customer Energy Management Platform), and future TVP plans.  The findings 
will be delineated by customer groups with a focus on acquiring feedback from the 
hard-to-reach customer segments, including low-income, non-English speaking and 
elderly/medical hardship customers.  These studies will include both surveys and focus 
groups in order to build a robust set of insights prior to deployment and subsequent 
introduction of TVP plans. 
Phase Two – Deployment  
 Smart Meter Deployment Monitoring (1-2 months following installation): Monitor 
customer awareness of, and satisfaction with, the deployment of smart meters through 
post-installation surveys. Studies will be conducted primarily online, but also 
supplemented by offline methodologies to ensure inclusivity of all customer groups.  
This initial measurement following installation allows for ongoing review of recent 
deployment, a comparison to pre-deployment metrics, and quick insights into the initial 
customer experience with the installation of a smart meter – all of which will inform the 
remainder of the Company’s deployment across its service territory. 
 Customer Engagement Communications Testing (as needed): Conduct a refresh of 
message testing post-installation to ensure that future content and messaging remain 
applicable to customer expectations and needs. 
Phase Three – Empowerment and Enablement  
 Customer Awareness and Attitudes on Smart Meters and Pricing: Conduct twice-annual 
surveys to monitor ongoing customer awareness and satisfaction with smart meters and 
utilization of accompanying information, insights, and tools offered (e.g., Customer 
Energy Management Platform).  There will be an ability to leverage this existing tracking 
mechanism/cadence with revised questions and content based on the highest priority 
topic at that given time (e.g., TVP plans in advance of introduction). 
 Customer Engagement Communications Testing (as needed): Conduct a refresh of 
content to ensure messaging remains applicable to customer expectations and needs. 
In short, National Grid will continue to keep customer research, insights, and feedback at the 
core of its design and decision-making processes as it progresses along the smart meter

4.1.3 Customer Segments May Need Specialized Communications 
National Grid will place a specific focus on hard-to-reach and vulnerable customer segments to 
provide clear understanding for all smart meter communications, including low-income, elderly, 
non-English speaking, and medical hardship customers.  It is critical that all literature, letters, 
forms, and any written communications are written clearly and concisely, and translated as 
needed in different languages for a given region. National Grid has previously found success in 
reaching out to low-income customers through direct email, targeted Facebook ads, and 
targeted bill inserts crafted to address the concerns experienced by this specific customer 
group.  Along with a new Mobile Sustainability Hub, the Company will utilize these forms of 
communication in its smart meter outreach and subsequent engagement on the introduction of 
new customer pricing plans.  More information on the Mobile Sustainability Hub is available in 
Section 4.3.3. 
National Grid’s Consumer Advocates will be a resource for these specific customer segments, 
providing support to ensure they have access to all the resources necessary for the transition to 
smart meters and new future pricing plans. The Consumer Advocates have found through past 
experience that most customers learn best by doing and seeing.   Therefore, outreach for low-
income customers will be through various channels including EXPOs, workshops, one-on-one 
interactions, and customer advocate groups. Smart meters and their benefits will be highlighted 
as a new agenda item during these types of events. Outreach will also include information 
about energy efficiency and assistance programs such as EmPower New York, NYS Homes and 
Community Renewal (“HCR”) Weatherization Assistance Program (“WAP”), and existing 
National Grid energy efficiency programs. 
Educating the Company’s network of key stakeholders in this space will help increase buy-in to 
encourage behavior changes and will be important for their assistance of customers. The 
Company’s Consumer Advocates have partnerships with many outside agencies (i.e., DSS, Office 
for Aging (“OFA”), Charities). Educating representatives of these agencies and providing 
resource materials will position them to assist customers who reach out to them with questions 
or concerns.  
The Company recognizes that barriers exist for these hard-to-reach and vulnerable customer 
segments with regard to access to information and technology that will allow them to 
experience the benefits of having a smart meter (e.g., reduce energy consumption by behavior 
modification and realizing the importance of no cost weatherization programs where customers 
can save money and experience greater comfort).  National Grid has committed to advancing 
energy affordability by developing initiatives focused on energy solutions for Low and 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 175 of 313


--- Page 211 ---
26 of 144 
Moderate Income (“LMI”) customers, while dedicating at least 20 percent of any additional 
levels of public investment in energy efficiency to the LMI sector. The Company is in discussions 
with New York State Energy Research and Development Authority (“NYSERDA”), the DPS and 
peer utilities to develop a comprehensive approach to help facilitate smart home technologies 
for the low-income customer groups to support smart meter benefits. 
Additionally, National Grid’s Energy Affordability Program (“EAP”) will continue to serve as an 
integral part for how the Company enables this customer segment. EAP offers tiered discounts 
based on the level of need, with the level of need demonstrated by receipt of a Home Energy 
Assistance Program (“HEAP”) grant and receipt of any HEAP “add-on” benefits to determine 
appropriate tiers for participants.  These customers are automatically enrolled in EAP, receive a 
monthly bill credit, and are enrolled into the Company’s Budget Plan (if there is no past-due 
balance at the time of enrollment) unless they choose to opt-out.  Customers may also be 
placed in a tier if they self-identify and provide appropriate documentation of low-income 
discount program eligibility. Low-income customers will be enrolled in EAP and will benefit 
from a tiered discount level as long as they receive HEAP grants. 
 
4.2 
Phase One: AMI Awareness 
4.2.1 Objectives 
The objectives of the Customer Engagement Plan in Phase One: AMI Awareness will be to: 
 Develop customer and stakeholder trust through continuous communication and 
transparency; 
 Build customer-facing assets and collateral for outreach and education efforts; 
 Educate and train National Grid employees on AMI deployment, including Customer 
Service Representatives, customer and community managers, account managers, and 
Corporate Communications; 
 Engage stakeholders including elected officials and community leaders who will interact 
with customers throughout the deployment process; 
 Initiate general mass-market outreach efforts to build smart metering awareness and 
commence education; and 
 Explain at a high level the rationale and benefits of smart meter installation and the 
enabling tools and options that will be available upon deployment.  
 
4.2.2 Approach 
During Phase One, National Grid will use a two-pronged approach to educate and engage 
customers by building a strong foundation internally and initiating proactive and transparent 
outreach efforts. As the Company learned from the Worcester Pilot and its Clifton Park pilot, 
National Grid customers want to be educated early and often on new features, technologies, 
and offers. Furthermore, internal customer surveys have shown that customers have a limited 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 176 of 313


--- Page 212 ---
27 of 144 
awareness of smart meters, indicating the need for early communication to help build 
awareness of AMI, generate interest and support, and mitigate any potential barriers or 
customer concerns.

To ensure an efficient smart meter transition, the Company will utilize outreach that has been 
previously successful to support the multi-phase, multi-channel outreach and engagement 
approach. An illustration of a successful approach utilized in Clifton Park can be seen in Figure 4-
3.

4.2.4 Employee Engagement and Training 
The Company’s employees are key ambassadors and vital to a successful deployment as many 
live in the Company’s service territory. Therefore, the Company will educate employees early 
and often on the entire AMI effort through a variety of channels, including employee forums, 
webinars, learning platforms, email outreach, senior management-led presentations and 
discussions, and other general communication methods utilized for critical Company updates.  
By enabling employees to serve as community champions for smart meter deployment, overall 
customer awareness and education is vastly improved. 
Within this larger employee base of ambassadors are key groups, such as Customer Service 
Representatives, customer and community managers, account managers21, and corporate 
communications. Field workers will be trained similarly with more detailed specifics closer to 
regional meter deployment. As shown in Table 4-2, all employees will receive communications, 
materials, and handouts with key messaging to best support their new National Grid 
ambassador role.  
Post-approval, all employees will be provided an online learning platform, which will include:  
 An overview of the AMI program, objectives, and customer benefits;

4.2.5 External Outreach to Stakeholders and Customers 
External outreach to key stakeholders, already underway as part of the Collaborative process, 
will continue following approval of AMI implementation and through all three engagement 
phases. This will ensure everyone is aware of the AMI plan, the benefits to all stakeholders, 
what to expect throughout the smart metering program, and the timelines associated with 
receiving meters that enable a new level of customer energy usage insights and action.  
Based on feedback provided during the AMI Collaborative sessions, the Company will prioritize 
the engagement of elected officials, specific customer groups (e.g., low-income consumer 
advocacy organizations), and community leaders prior to the onset of broader customer 
outreach as in Table 4-4. These engagements will include face-to-face meetings, phone 
discussions, and written communications.  Again, this prioritized engagement will continue 
throughout all three phases.

4.2.6 Addressing Customer Concerns  
The Company plans to leverage existing customer-facing materials and communications from 
its pilot experiences to develop new materials to address customer concerns related to privacy, 
security, data sharing, and the health impacts of smart meters. The Company has developed a 
broad repository of various customer concerns raised in both pilots, which will help ensure 
distribution of information across different customer touchpoints, including FAQs, Customer 
Service scripts, media materials, website, and town hall materials.

and will be updated to reflect the most relevant information for the Company’s customers.

4.2.7 Customer Opt-Out in Phase One 
The goals for managing opt-out will vary based on the engagement phase. In Phase One: AMI 
Awareness, the aim will be to inform customers of program benefits and privacy information to 
minimize meter opt-out rates via direct mail, email, the National Grid website, community 
meetings, advocacy groups, social media, paid media, and customer service support.  
Customers will be able to choose to opt out of a smart meter at any time.  The opt-out process 
can occur through Customer Service support and/or neighborhood installers/representatives. If 
a customer calls the Customer Service Center, the Customer Service Representative will ask to 
understand the reason for opting-out and provide additional information as appropriate. The 
Customer Service Center will communicate to the customer that future reading fees will be 
charged on a monthly basis if an AMI meter/module is not installed and will send the customer 
the appropriate forms to officially confirm that they do not wish to participate in receiving an 
AMI meter and/or gas module, thereby foregoing future benefits that are unlocked by the 
installation of a smart meter. 
 
4.3 
Phase Two: Deployment 
4.3.1 Objective 
The objectives of the Customer Engagement Plan in Phase Two: Deployment will be to: 
 Expand outreach and education efforts on AMI meters and benefits across multiple 
customer touchpoints;  
 Inform customers of the AMI deployment plan and transparently communicate how 
they will be impacted; 
 Ensure that customers understand the installation process and available choices (e.g., 
opt out) as well as address any concerns (e.g., safety or privacy); 
 Provide customers with timely notifications of installation; and  
 Preview tools and options that will be available to customers post-smart meter 
deployment.  
 
4.3.2 Approach: Ninety-Sixty-Thirty Day Communications Plan 
National Grid will leverage the numerous mass market communication and education strategies 
utilized during Phase One: AMI Awareness as a foundation for focusing the message throughout 
Phase Two: Deployment. Targeted customer communications will be rolled out ninety, sixty, 
and thirty days prior to installation of the smart meter and will be locally timed to the 
Company’s meter installation schedule.  
The Ninety-Sixty-Thirty Day Communications Plan is summarized in Table 4-6 below.

4.3.3 Ninety Days Prior to Deployment 
As regions are scheduled for AMI meter installation, the Company will build upon the steady 
education of, and engagement with, elected officials and community leaders from Phase One to 
begin direct and targeted education. Ninety-days prior to deployment, the Company will 
enhance its community outreach by hosting town hall meetings and Company employees will 
increase their presence at local events. The Clifton Park pilot has shown this type of 
engagement to be particularly successful in reaching customers. This type of outreach may 
include:  
 Orchestrating meet-and-learn events at local venues with customers, town officials, 
press and external stakeholders. Events will take place at various times (e.g., daytime, 
evening, weekday, weekend) to accommodate varied customer schedules;  
 Developing targeted presentations focused on customer benefits; 
 Providing customers with a “hands-on” experience with the new Customer Energy 
Management Platform and learning how to utilize it; and

 Providing step-by-step guides as take-home materials for customers after these events. 
 
Mobile Sustainability Hub 
The Company will first introduce the Mobile Sustainability Hub for customers and stakeholders 
to engage with prior to meter installation. It will serve as a “hands on” learning experience 
about the benefits of smart meters.  
The Company’s Mobile Sustainability Hub will be a branded recreational vehicle that allows the 
Company’s customers to come on board to consult with company employees on a variety of 
topics like smart meters, bill questions, energy efficiency and demand management tips, 
renewable energy, future pricing plans, and much more.  The Company will provide hands-on 
education and an interactive technology experience through this holistic approach of various 
integrated exhibits that can constitute a “Sustainability Hub”. The Company envisions working 
with community leaders, vendors, and local schools and colleges, to create a rich experience for 
the community fostering interest in smart meters while also driving customer engagement, 
awareness, and participation.  
The Mobile Sustainability Hub also allows for the opportunity to engage more personally with 
customers in each community since the Hub will move among the communities served by the 
Company. While a stationary location worked well in the Company’s Worcester Pilot, the 
flexibility to move throughout the service territory is more suited for a mass deployment and 
will allow more customers to reap the benefits of this hands-on experience. 
A driving component of the Mobile Sustainability Hub will be to showcase the same type of 
smart meter to be installed at customer residences. As community members learn how to 
implement energy savings in their own homes prior to, and following, the installation of a smart 
meter, they will be able to attain higher levels of energy savings, especially after the 
introduction of new pricing plans. In addition, it is more likely that participants will understand 
how to implement changes after an in-person demonstration versus only receiving written or 
verbal instructions. As customers come to realize the benefits, they may share their experience 
with neighbors, peers, business associates, and others while encouraging further participation.  
The Mobile Sustainability Hub will be deployed strategically to areas about to receive smart 
meters. The Hub will offer learning sessions at various advertised times of the day and phases 
of the project.  It will also offer standard “office hours” for customers to conveniently 
communicate with the Company’s staff.  The Hub will be equipped with customer educational 
materials and staff will be able to review and answer any questions the customers may have. 
In addition to the benefits of the Mobile Sustainably Hub, the Company has found public events 
to be fruitful venues to measure and gain feedback on message penetration.

4.3.6 Day of Meter Deployment 
On the day of meter installation, field workers will first begin by attempting to contact the 
customer. For customers that have both electric and gas service, both the electric meter and 
the module on the gas meter will be replaced.  For customers with electric service only, the 
electricity will be disconnected to provide for a safe working environment for the technician. 
Service will be reconnected after the new meter is installed. For customers with gas service, the 
gas measurement module of the gas meter will be replaced and service will not be 
disconnected.  There is no hazard imposed by replacing just the gas module so service 
disconnection and reconnection is not required. 
Upon installation of the AMI meter and/or replacement of the AMI module to the gas meter, 
National Grid will provide the customer with a doorknob hanger that confirms services have 
been performed and supporting “Welcome Brochure” information. This information will also 
include the toll-free number for customers to call and website addresses if they have questions 
about their new electric meter or gas module and/or general questions about the smart meter 
program. 
In the event a customer meter is unable to be upgraded, information will be left at the 
residence to include the reason installers were unable to upgrade the meter or gas module as 
well as any actions the customer may need to take for the service to be completed. If the issue 
preventing installation could cause harm to the customer or dwelling, the Company 
representative would not leave the property until service has been disconnected. The Company 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 194 of 313


--- Page 230 ---
45 of 144 
would then actively contact the customer to notify them of the issue and required steps to 
rectify the situation. 
The Company will monitor all communications channels to ensure customers feel supported, 
informed, and comfortable with their new meter. Based on the learnings from the Clifton Park 
pilot, field workers will be equipped with FAQs, “Welcome Brochures”, and meter completion 
doorknob hangers to support the customer experience.

4.3.7 Customer Opt-out in Phase Two 
If on the day of installation, the Customer chooses to opt-out when the utility representative 
makes initial contact, the technician will not install the new electric meter or gas module at that 
location.  The utility representative would contact a supervisor to hold installation and the 
Company would then provide the customer with a form for submission to confirm their choice 
to not receive the new equipment, with an acknowledgement that will incur future meter 
reading fees.  The Company will aim to learn as much as possible from the customer during this 
time, including why they are choosing to opt out of receiving the new equipment and foregoing 
the opportunity to participate in future benefits. Receipt of customer feedback during this time 
can also inform future messaging and outreach strategies to help limit future meter opt outs.  
If the customer still chooses to opt out and submits the opt-out form back to the Company, the 
Company will follow-up with a confirmation letter or email to the customer that clearly explains 
the new customer experience and specifically what the customer is responsible for moving 
forward (i.e., payment of future meter reading fees). 
 
4.3.8 Other Deployment Considerations 
4.3.8.1 Building code issues and policies 
Beginning with Phase One, the Company will coordinate with building inspectors, code 
enforcement, and municipalities to prepare them for a possible increase in requested services 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 195 of 313


--- Page 231 ---
46 of 144 
caused by smart meter installation. National Grid will provide the services to elevate the meter 
site, change the meter, verify communications, and record all necessary meter-related 
information. National Grid will not be responsible for meter channel and service and wiring, 
including service entrance cable.22  
If the Company encounters an issue that could be unsafe for the customer, the Company will 
notify the customer of the safety hazard as referenced earlier in Section 4.3.6. The Company 
does not expect a high volume of safety issues based on the past AMR deployment.  The 
Company will work with the customer to identify a time in the future that the installation could 
be completed. 
 
4.3.8.2 Indoor Meters/Life Support Equipment 
In the case of indoor or inaccessible meters (i.e., meters in basements), multiple attempts will 
be made to reach the customer to access the meter. For example, doorknob hangers will be left 
upon departure to schedule an appointment. After several attempts, the Company will reach 
out directly to the customer to schedule an installation appointment.  
For customers identified in the Company system as possessing life support equipment or 
requiring uninterruptable service for medical reasons, meters will NOT be changed without 
explicit permission and the scheduling of a pre-approved time. Multiple attempts will be made 
to reach the customer to schedule the meter change. When installers are in the area, doorknob 
hangers will be left to schedule an appointment if the customer does not come to door. Meters 
will not be changed without acknowledgement and permission from the customer.  
 
4.4 
Phase Three: Customer Empowerment and Enablement 
For the benefits of smart meter technology to be fully realized by both the customer and the 
Company, the Company must pair the new technology with proactive customer and market 
engagement initiatives that occur prior to and post-deployment. As noted in Section 2, National 
Grid will develop and implement a Customer Energy Management Platform. The Customer 
Energy Management Platform is a long-term, holistic approach that will incorporate digital 
channels to educate and engage customers (primarily mass-market) in their modern energy 
experience that is triggered by the installation of a smart meter.  With the changing customer 
expectations, the rise in cloud computing capabilities, and the need for new customer systems 
capable of fully leveraging the massive amounts of future smart meter data, there is a 
significant opportunity to provide new value to customers. This necessitates a more cohesive 
                                                          
22 Customers are responsible for certain equipment at the premise including the meter channel and wire that runs 
on the outside of the house and is connected to the Company’s service lateral.  If these are damaged and require 
repair for safety purposes it’s the customer’s responsibility.  Once the repair is made a municipal inspector must 
inspect/verify the service is safe before the Company can provide service by installing the meter.   
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 196 of 313


--- Page 232 ---
47 of 144 
and integrated digital solution and justifies the Company developing the Customer Energy 
Management Platform to meet the changing needs of its customers while ensuring access to 
valuable information for all customers in the Company’s service territory.  
The envisioned customer benefits that originate post-installation are made possible by several 
enabling technologies. The deployment of the smart meters and associated communication 
network will provide significantly more granular data than is available with current AMR 
technology. It will establish a two-way communication pathway between National Grid and 
customers, a grid-edge computing platform including software applications that are deployable 
to the meters, and smart home/business technology integration. The third-party data sharing 
enabled through the Green Button Connect My Data functionality will enable increased market 
participation and competitive customer offerings. The Customer Energy Management Platform 
will synthesize these many data sources and offerings to provide a simple and easy-to-use 
customer experience. To ensure maximum awareness and utilization, the Company plans to 
deploy and educate customers about the Customer Energy Management Platform prior to 
smart meter installation (i.e., prior to beginning of Phase Two). 
The following section provides an overview of customers’ current web experience, the vision 
for, and key features of, the Customer Energy Management Platform, and illustrative customer 
journeys (residential, commercial, industrial) to visualize the future customer experience 
enabled by smart meters.  
 
4.4.1 Objectives 
The objectives of Phase Three: Customer Engagement and Empowerment will be to: 
 Provide access to new online Customer Energy Management Platform;  
 Educate customers early and often about the Customer Energy Management Platform 
and other tools and options that will be available post-meter installation;  
 Encourage customers to interact with Customer Energy Management Platform to better 
understand energy usage and modify energy consumption;  
 Provide follow-up communications on new features to customers; and 
 Introduce and educate customers on new TVP plans. 
 
4.4.1.1 Summary of Key Activities for Both Digital and Non-Digital Delivery 
As previously noted, the Company recognizes that not all customers will seek to engage digitally 
with the benefits enabled by smart meters.  Although the Customer Energy Management 
Platform is a digitally-focused tool that supports the modern energy experience for customers, 
the Company will develop educational and engagement materials that ensure all customers are 
empowered and enabled with the right information, insights, and opportunities to take action 
in order to manage usage and total energy costs.  The Company will develop relevant and 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 197 of 313


--- Page 233 ---
48 of 144 
motivating messages tailored to customer segments and channels based on best practices from 
the Company’s previous smart meter pilots, which provides some insights into how to deliver 
an omni-channel engagement strategy.  
National Grid will develop a variety of educational materials to reach customers through 
traditional and online channels, reflecting the strategies utilized during the Company’s pilot 
programs. As the Company progresses through the Customer Engagement and Empowerment 
phase, the Company will utilize similar channels noted for Phase One and Phase Two (e.g., fact 
sheets, welcome brochures, Mobile Sustainability Hub).  Additional strategies planned for 
Phase Three include developing videos to educate customers to help them understand bill 
statements, creating a “Getting Started Guide” to provide step-by-step instructions and visuals 
for customers about the post-deployment experience (e.g., the Customer Energy Management 
Platform), developing testimonials and case studies, and engaging with customers via social 
media (e.g., Facebook, Twitter, YouTube). All educational materials will continue to be available 
in various languages based on customer demographics.  The Company will continue to get 
feedback throughout this phase on materials and channels to provide improvements and 
refinements. 
 
4.4.2 Approach  
The Company will begin educating customers on the new post-deployment tools and services, 
including the Customer Energy Management Platform, beginning in Phase One with broad 
awareness, continuing through deployment in Phase Two, and reinforced in Phase Three upon 
meter installation. This will allow both the meter and the new tools and services it provides to 
be top of mind for customers to help enable their engagement. For example, the Company will 
auto-enroll customers in high electric and gas usage alerts based on customer-selected 
preferred communication methods and applicable laws. High usage alerts is a feature that will 
notify customers during a billing cycle if they are predicted to use substantially more energy 
than usual by the end of the billing cycle. Without smart meters as the enabling technology, it is 
difficult to acquire this information and take action to manage energy usage. Educational 
material, both traditional and digital, describing these types of features as well as other aspects 
of the Customer Energy Manage Platform will be supplied on a frequent basis over the first four 
weeks following the installation of the smart meter.  
The Company will monitor customer utilization of these new tools and services, including the 
Customer Energy Management Platform, and will adjust messaging and channels by segment to 
drive increased utilization. Table 4-14 is an example of analysis conducted within the 
Company’s Clifton Park pilot of customer awareness with usage segmentation and helps the 
Company to refine messages through the “Listen, Test, Learn” approach.  As customer 
engagement increases over time, customers are better targeted by segments to maintain and 
improve engagement as shown in Table 4-15.

4.4.3 Current Web Experience 
The typical mass-market customer’s web experience today enables customers to take actions in 
multiple areas.  A non-exhaustive list of these functions includes: 
 Web account/general account information; 
 Billing and payment options; 
 Usage information and insights; 
 Service orders; 
 Enrollment with an energy service company (“ESCO”); 
 Energy savings programs (e.g., Energy Efficiency, DLM); and 
 Reporting (e.g., outages). 
Although the website (both desktop and mobile enabled) is an important resource for 
customers, there is a strong opportunity for a more integrated experience that National Grid 
aims to deliver for the modern energy customer upon the installation of a smart meter (i.e., 
within Phase Three).  Particularly noteworthy is the customer’s usage information and insights – 
all of which are greatly enhanced by the installation of a smart meter. Figure 4-12 displays 
many of the current web components available to customers today. 
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 200 of 313


--- Page 236 ---
51 of 144 
 
Figure 4-12: Current Customer Webpage Highlights 
Similarly, today’s web experience for C&I customers includes access to numerous energy 
savings programs, including access to Energy Profile Online (“EPO”), a subscription-based tool 
through which C&I customers with interval data meters can access interval load day and day-
ahead hourly energy prices.   
While this data provides C&I customers with energy usage information, it does not, however, 
provide many insights or direct actions as to how they can better understand and manage their 
energy.  National Grid will deliver this functionality (without additional fees) to all C&I 
customers through the deployment of smart meters and subsequent tools that pair interval 
load data with actionable data and insights. 
Overall, the Customer Energy Management Platform will create an experience that truly 
enables and empowers customers in the changing energy landscape, allowing for greater 
customer choice, convenience, and control. Building on the foundation established via the 
Company’s Customer Experience Transformation initiatives described in Section 2 and Appendix 
C, it will incorporate smart meter data to provide customers with greater, low-latency data and 
insights to guide customers in better understanding and managing their bills (e.g., high 
bill/usage alerts), and provide them with the potential and direction to more easily save energy 
and money on their bills (e.g., DLM programs and EE incentives).

4.4.4 Customer Opt-Out in Phase Three 
In Phase Three, there is a continuous aim to mitigate both meter and future rate opt-outs.  If a 
customer has opted out of a meter in the past, National Grid will provide information on 
program benefits, health and privacy, security, and the path to opt-in through the website and 
customer service support if they chose to opt back in to receiving a  smart meter.  
 
4.4.5 The Customer Energy Management Platform: Overview, Features, and an Omni-
Channel Experience 
The post-deployment strategies will be based on an omni-channel approach (prioritizing 
digital), with resources and collateral to empower and enable customers. The Customer Energy 
Management Platform will serve as an integrated hub of energy data, insights, and, actions for 
residential, commercial, and industrial customers. The Customer Energy Management Platform 
will leverage a service-oriented architecture approach to best provide a flexible platform for the 
Company to iterate, innovate, and improve on over time based on customer feedback and 
utilization of the various features. The information that is available through the digital channels 
of the Customer Energy Management Platform will also be available through traditional 
channels such as Customer Service Centers, bills, and other hardcopy collateral. 
A primary function of the platform is to aggregate data from a number of sources, provide 
analytics capabilities to transform that data into insights, and then use those insights to 
encourage and facilitate customer action. As such, the platform will streamline a number of 
existing customer portals, third party websites, and existing educational and safety information 
(currently provided to customers on the Company’s home webpage), with the goal of providing 
customers with simple, seamless access to tools, information, and actionable insights. 
In order to enable a more holistic and seamless customer experience, the Customer Energy 
Management Platform will include Company-facing features and integration with traditional 
collateral to better leverage customer data, insights, and recent account activities. In addition, 
tools for account managers, sales, and marketing teams will enable targeted program 
information and offers to improve program participation and performance rates for C&I 
customers. Integrations with billing and customer service will add personalized detail to the 
non-digital channels with the aim of proving equitable opportunity and customer experience 
for all customer sectors regardless of the customer’s preferred channel. One example of this 
could be providing Customer Service Representatives with access to load disaggregation data to 
review with customers during high bill calls and then providing summary follow-up information 
on the subsequent bill. 
The Customer Energy Management Platform will enable customers to access accurate and 
personalized energy usage information, as well as various choices and options to enroll in 
programs and services that can leverage the more granular data provided by AMI meters. These 
include programs and services such as energy efficiency programs, dynamic load management 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)

4.4.6 Third-Party Data Access, Market Animation, and Green Button  
Smart meters and the Customer Energy Management Platform will provide new opportunities 
for market animation. Third-party data access is critical to furthering many REV objectives and 
the supporting goals of the current Earning Adjustment Mechanisms    . Standardized access to 
authorized customer data helps remove barriers for third-party providers to offer energy 
services to the benefit of both customers and the distribution grid. Furthermore, National Grid 
is considering future ways the Customer Energy Management Platform and advanced metering 
infrastructure may serve as platforms for third-party enablement, akin to how smartphone 
providers deliver significant value for consumers and developers alike through an app store.  
The granular customer data generated by smart meters will be analyzed and turned into 
actionable insights for customers. As utility data industry group Mission:data recently noted:  
“realizing significant gains in energy efficiency will come not from consumers 
interacting with their raw energy data, but rather indirectly from technology 
companies and service providers who process, digest and act upon energy data 
on the customer’s behalf.”26  
This sentiment is increasingly important in a future with TVP, where there is an even greater 
economic incentive for customers to reduce and shift load. 
In support of the above vision, National Grid strongly supports secure, scalable, and accessible 
data sharing consistent with Company policies and procedures and applicable laws – discussed 
in more detail within Section Error! Reference source not found..  Currently, the industry-led 
reen Button initiative is viewed as the most widely deployed and adopted method for 
customers to grant data access to third-party service providers. Green Button Download My 
Data and Connect My Data solutions eliminate the need for third-party service provides to 
support various utility protocols and streamlines the customer authorization process. 
Many utilities, including National Grid, have implemented the Green Button Download My Data 
functionality. This national energy data format and initiative gives every utility customer the 
ability to download their personal energy consumption data directly to their computer in a 
secure manner. Figure 4-16 highlights an example today on using the Download My Data 
function on the Company’s current website downloading monthly usage information. 
Additionally, if customers are interested, they can separately upload their downloaded data to 
a third-party application. 
 
                                                          
26 Mission:data, Got Data? The Value of Energy Data Access to Consumers, January 2016. Pg. 36. 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 214 of 313


--- Page 250 ---
65 of 144 
 
Figure 4-13: Green Button Download My Data 
 
The Green Button Connect My Data functionality takes this process further by allowing utility 
customers to automate the data transfer process. With Green Button Connect My Data, 
customers can securely authorize both National Grid and vetted third parties to send and 
receive data on the customer’s behalf. As noted in Error! Reference source not found., this 
functionality will be developed and deployed as a key feature of the Customer Energy 
Management Platform. Further details on third-party data access policies are detailed in 
Section 7. 
 
 
Figure 4-14: Green Button Data Exchange 
 
The Green Button data standard is flexible and can accommodate a range of data intervals, 
from two-month to one-minute intervals. National Grid plans to offer fifteen minute electric 
interval data available on a four-hour latency for raw data and bill quality data will be available

Through careful integration of the Green Button Connect My Data functionality in the Customer 
Energy Management Platform, National Grid envisions a simplified and streamlined customer 
experience when accessing third-party services. National Grid is actively researching industry 
best practices of peer utilities with active Green Button deployments. For example, San Diego 
Gas & Electric, Pacific Gas & Electric, and London Hydro actively offer customers several dozen 
third-party offerings on a Green Button Connect My Data platform.27 Transparency and 
customer control are crucial to Green Button Connect My Data in managing the sharing of 
customers’ energy data.  
National Grid expects future partnerships with companies that connect customers with 
offerings such as energy storage, demand response, and distributed generation in support of 
both Company and New York State energy goals. One current example of this is the Company’s 
New York Solar Marketplace, offered to customers in partnership with EnergySage, a third-
party vendor. Through the Solar Marketplace, National Grid offers customers a simple process 
to research and purchase solar photovoltaic equipment. Customers are able to comparison-
shop solar opportunities and receive competitive quotes from pre-screened installers. The 
marketplace strives to provide a high quality customer experience by limiting participation to 
providers with robust qualifications, equipment, and customer service. The marketplace

highlights the opportunities that exist for the Company serve customers as a trusted energy

providers.  This will significantly reduce the cycle time for innovation, integration, and 
implementation, as well as associated costs, required to bring new offerings to customers. This 
future state will greatly accelerate progress toward meeting the State’s and Company’s policy 
goals, REV objectives, and the Company’s ability to continue to exceed customer expectations. 
 
4.4.7 Customer Journeys 
As the future procurement and detailed design of the Customer Energy Management Platform 
progresses, the Company offers the following customer journeys as illustrative examples of the 
customer experience with the Customer Energy Management Platform. The customer journeys 
are not intended to be comprehensive of all customer types or use cases, but offer several 
examples of envisioned experiences enabled by the Customer Energy Management Platform. 
 
4.4.7.1 Residential Customer Journey 
Each residential customer has different needs when it comes to using, understanding, and 
paying for their energy. This new Customer Energy Management Platform design will not just 
have better insights and tools in one place, but also allow for alerts, predictive analytics, and a 
window into the use of granular smart meter data.  Examples like alerts that can notify 
customers on high usage will proactively help customers to better manage their energy 
consumption.  
Residential Customer Example (Figure 4-18)

Schedule 1

68 of 144 
An elderly, low-income home owner has received a high bill, yet does not understand why their 
bill is so high, nor what to do about it. The customer can log onto the Customer Energy 
Management Platform web portal (A) and look into their high-level dashboard of options.  
Looking into paying their bill (B), the customer can see how much energy they have used and 
their total charges. From there and on the same page the customer may be able to live chat (C) 
or, if preferred, speak directly to a customer service representative (D).  
Customers can then look deeper into their energy history over time, and now with the addition 
of smart meter data the Company can provide high bill alerts (E) to notify a customer during 
their billing cycle that they may be heading towards a higher monthly bill.  These high-bill alerts 
can utilize this near real-time data to provide customers with the insights needed to understand 
how they are consuming and being charged for energy.  
With this knowledge the customer can explore and learn about energy savings programs (F), 
tips (G), budget plans, and 3rd party services to help save on their bills and all from the 
integrated Customer Energy Management Platform.

4.4.7.2 Non-Digital Mass Market Experience 
The same data and information described above will also be available to all customer support 
representatives. When a customer calls in regarding questions on their bill, the customer 
support representative will be able to provide the customer with the same insights, appliance-
level energy usage, tips and energy saving programs to help customers save on their bill.  
In the case of the example of an elderly customer calling in with a high bill, the customer service 
representative is able to tell the customer what the largest electric loads in the home may be to 
help target subsequent customer action. Post-deployment of TVP plans,  the customer service

support representative interactions with traditional collateral. 
Using the detailed load profile and load disaggregation information provided by smart meters, 
customers, will have new tools to understand their energy use and how to improve energy

Platform.   
 
4.4.7.3 Commercial Customer Journey  
Commercial customers have different needs than residential customers.  The new Customer 
Energy Management Platform design will not just have better insights and tools in one place, 
but will support a portfolio view of facilities along with other tools geared specifically for 
commercial customers.  Streamlined data access, visualization, and analytics combined with 
new and improved connections to DER programs and offerings will have the potential to 
significantly impact how commercial customers meet their energy needs.  
Commercial Customer Example (Figure 4-19) 
A commercial real estate property management customer currently faces challenges to manage 
energy across her portfolio and track the value of upgrades. In the Customer Energy 
Management Platform, the property manager is able to log-in and quickly view energy use 
information for the portfolio of properties. Links to the most common popular features are 
provided for quick access in (A). The customer is then able to drill down by building to access 
detailed data visualization and analytics tools on consumption data, energy use intensity 
benchmarking by building, and quickly view recommendations on the best opportunity (B, C). 
The customer is able to contact their account representative (D) and discuss relevant energy 
efficiency opportunities that match the top savings opportunities. This information is quickly 
connected with vetted service providers for turnkey energy services (E). This includes third-
party solutions and services, which over time will continue to develop increasingly innovative 
and competitive technology and financing offerings.  
The Customer Energy Management Platform continuously tracks energy efficiency upgrades, 
reporting achieved energy efficiency, peak usage reductions, and financial metrics. The 
Customer Energy Management Platform recommends the next best action for future projects 
to encourage continued participation.

4.4.7.4 Industrial Customer Journey 
Industrial customers can be very sophisticated as many have been heavily managing their 
energy due to the significant cost to their operations. Therefore, it is necessary that equally 
sophisticated tools are available to meet the needs of these customers. 
Industrial Customer Example  
The facility manager at a large manufacturing customer needs better access to mission critical 
data that the plant equipment requires to operate properly. 
With the low data latency that will be available through AMI, the customer is able to access 
data on power factor in near real-time on their smartphone. Through the Customer Energy 
Management Platform’s personalized notification tools, the facility manager has requested a 
text message alert to be notified when future power quality conditions may become 
problematic and require a shutdown of the production line.  
Upon receipt of the text, the customer is able to determining the root cause of the issue being 
the recent simultaneous start of several large electric motors that caused a voltage fluctuation. 
Now being able to visualize the root cause, the facility manager discusses this information with 
the production line supervisor to sequence the equipment so that this is not a future issue 
causing a loss in production time and therefore profitability.

4.4.7.5 Non-Digital C& I Customer Experience 
While a vast majority of C&I customers are utilizing digital means to obtain information and pay 
their bills online, there is still an important need to provide customers with a non-digital 
experience.  As mentioned above in the residential customer experience, all granular smart 
meter data, energy insights, tips, and access to EE and demand response (“DR”) programs can 
still be seen and accessed by the Company’s Customer Service Representatives. If a customer 
chooses to call in, their account representative or jurisdictional manager will have access to all 
same information and can provide a personalized experience to each customer. 
The Company will coordinate traditional marketing efforts and collateral with the digital 
platform. This coordination will help ensure that all information regarding smart meters and the 
insights and actions derived from the Customer Energy Management Platform are 
communicated and provided to each customer.    
 
4.4.8 Enabling Integration with Future Technologies  
The benefits of the Customer Energy Management Platform are made possible by the 
confluence of several technologies.  
First and foremost, the deployment of the smart meters and associated communication 
network will provide significantly more granular data than is available with current AMR 
technology, establish a two-way communication pathway between National Grid and the 
customer, and establish a grid-edge computing platform, including software applications that 
are deployable to the meters.  
Once deployed, future use cases can leverage these foundational infrastructure and capabilities 
to provide new customer solutions where feasible. For example, through the establishment of a 
home area network, home energy management systems will be able to send and receive secure 
communications from the Company or third-party market entities. This can enable 1) real-time 
customer access to meter data, including load/price signals, including potentially more 
advanced capabilities like real-time load disaggregation, and 2) real-time integration with smart 
devices such as thermostats, water heaters, and other appliances.  
Future integration with smart home/business devices will expand upon existing Dynamic Load 
Management, such as the Company’s ConnectedSolutions program.28  Within this program, 
with a customer’s authorization, the system would have the ability to automatically adjust 
energy consumption in response to pricing signals and calls for curtailment through connected 
devices. The Company has already found success integrating such an offering with AMI through 
the Worcester  Pilot. A 2017 Navigant evaluation of the Pilot found that customers who 
enrolled in the no-cost technology packages with direct load control devices (programmable 
                                                          
28 https://www.nationalgridus.com/Upstate-NY-Home/Energy-Saving-Programs/Connectedsolutions.  
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 221 of 313


--- Page 257 ---
72 of 144 
communicating thermostats, smart plugs, and for some customers a wired load control device) 
had up to roughly double peak event load reductions (kW) during CPP and peak time rewards 
(“PTR”) events as compared to customers who did not have direct load control.  While the 
Company’s AMI proposal does not include the provision of no-cost technology packages for 
customers, it envisions utilizing the Customer Energy Management Platform the array of energy 
efficiency and DLM offerings to enable customers with the ability to adopt smart, connected 
devices that can help deliver additional energy and peak demand savings. 
Building on learnings from the ConnectedSolutions program and the Worcester Pilot, the 
Company also recognizes the need to mitigate potential technology obsolescence risks, as the 
associated Internet of Things (“IoT”) and smart home industries are in the relatively early 
stages. To help mitigate these risks, National Grid will leverage open integration standards and 
protocols. The metering technology will be adaptable to firmware upgrades and integration 
with future home area network devices to accommodate multiple communication 
standards/protocols and upgrades over time to ensure strong technology agility in the future.  
Until relatively recently, the industry-standard approach to home area networks (“HAN”) was 
typically based on the Zigbee Smart Energy Profile 1.x standard. As shown in the Company’s 
Worcester Pilot, this approach can provide impactful energy saving technology. However, there 
are implementation challenges and lessons learned from past/current HAN programs to 
consider as well as significant advances in technology (primarily AMI network improvements 
and the rise of smartphones) in designing a modern HAN offering, as detailed in a 2017 Electric 
Power Research Institute (“EPRI”) whitepaper.29  
The Company is looking to enable a broad ecosystem of off-the-shelf products, find ways for 
customers to use existing devices, and streamline the customer experience to lower adoption 
hurdles. This requires flexibility in communication protocols/standards and network 
architecture. In addition to HAN offerings, web-based services may prove to be a preferable 
option for certain technologies and use cases. Eventually, the during the estimated twenty-year 
asset life of the AMI deployment, the long-term technology roadmap may extend towards 
offering an increasing amount of automation to provide new customer value. Accordingly, 
other important design considerations come from findings from a recent Smart Energy 
Consumer Collaborative (“SECC”) report that recommends: 
“Plan on evolution and embed flexibility. Customer expectations are evolving as 
the definition and capabilities of the Platform and its enabled products and 
services evolve, too. These two paths will not necessarily evolve in lockstep, so 
flexibility is the key to success. Service providers would do well to design products 
                                                          
29 Electric Power Research Institute, Home Area Network Issues and Architectural Alternatives, 
September 2017. 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 222 of 313


--- Page 258 ---
73 of 144 
and services that provide a solid level of assistance today, but could easily adapt 
to (and include) more automation tomorrow.” 
Consistent with these findings, the Company is investigating how best to enable a seamless 
customer experience that modern consumers have come to expect from design thinking-
oriented companies, such as Amazon, Airbnb, Apple, and others. The Company is taking an 
iterative and customer-centric approach to create an open-standards based connected device 
platform, automated energy management, and the associated modern digital experience.  
From a network and asset management standpoint, the Company is currently evaluating 
technology offerings to best leverage AMI as a flexible foundation to integrate with, and 
provide incremental value to, systems such as the Company’s Demand Response Management 
System (“DRMS”) and future Distributed Energy Resource Management System (“DERMS”), 
discussed in further detail in the Company’s 2018 DSIP Update. As adoption of DERs such as 
electric vehicles, solar energy systems with smart inverters, and battery storage systems 
accelerate, the ability to evolve such systems to integrate DER assets becomes increasingly 
important.  
Accordingly, the Company is currently working with manufacturing partners through the 
Company’s AMI procurement work stream to identify improved on-board computing and HAN 
options, including Wi-Fi-based communications. Other emerging front-runners that are in the 
early stages of development and deployment currently being investigated include DotDot over 
Thread (an emerging interoperable IoT language over an IP network) and IEEE 2030.5 (the 
default communication standard under California’s Rule 21 adoption of the IEEE 1547 
standard). Customer device provisioning will be enabled through the Customer Energy 
Management Platform. The specifics of the device provisioning process may ultimately depend 
on the manufacturing partner selected. Consistent with the Customer Experience 
Transformation vision, detailed design decisions made during implementation will include a 
strong focus on streamlining the customer experience.

smart meter data. 
As detailed in Section 2, National Grid’s multi-year Customer Experience Transformation 
initiative is currently underway and will coordinate closely with the development of the 
Customer Energy Management Platform. Upon regulatory approval for AMI, the Company will 
begin to coordinate the deployment of the Customer Energy Management Platform with other

5 
INNOVATIVE RATE DESIGN 
5.1 
The Company’s Long Term Vision  
National Grid believes that smart meters are a foundational technology in advancing REV goals 
of a cleaner, more dynamic energy sector with more engaged customers. The territory-wide 
deployment of smart meters will allow the Company to communicate actionable prices that 
better reflect actual costs for both delivery and supply for all mass market customers. Those 
prices intend to bring improved market function to the distribution system and lead to 
increased economic efficiency, which translates to long-run customer benefits through lower 
bills. Once customers’ incentives align with the Company’s and with New York State’s own 
ambitious energy goals, those who take steps to lower their bills will contribute toward a more 
efficient electrical network thus producing lower system costs.  
The Company’s current basic residential customer rates recover supply and delivery costs on a 
flat, volumetric (per kWh) basis, plus a fixed monthly customer charge. This rate structure only 
loosely reflects how most electric system costs are incurred, which leads to economic 
inefficiencies and inaccurate price signals. These inefficiencies and disconnected price signals 
lead to suboptimal levels of investment in energy technology, including energy efficiency and 
customer energy usage decisions. A move toward more economically efficient time-varying 
prices are a critical enabling development to further the adoption of beneficial electrification 
technologies and fostering economic sustainability as these markets continue to grow to meet 
New York’s energy targets. This proposal focuses on an improved default volumetric supply rate 
for mass market customers. While the Company will continue to work on and pilot improved 
delivery rates,30 they do not play a role in the benefits described in the accompanying AMI 
Report and Business Case.  
National Grid’s costs for electric supply are driven by the procurement of energy and capacity in 
the New York Independent System Operator (“NYISO”) wholesale electric markets.  Improved 
default supply rates, which include both a time-of-use critical peak pricing components are 
strong drivers of the benefits customers will realize through the deployment of AMI. The 
Company chose the combination of a CPP/TOU construct based on the success of the rate in 
national comprehensive studies, where it produced an average of twenty percent on-peak 
demand reductions,31 and the Company’s own experience in its two-year Worcester Pilot, in 
which active customers were able to produce an average of about seventeen percent demand 
reduction for each critical peak event.    
                                                          
30 Delivery costs reflect the Company’s costs to maintain the network to deliver safe, reliable electricity 
to customers within its service territory. Novel delivery rates do not factor into the BCA or the 
Company’s AMI Business Case.

The Company will continue to use demonstration projects and pilots (i.e., the Clifton Park pilot) 
to develop strategies for novel rates, and will include any additional learnings from the 
Worcester Pilot and Clifton Park project in future rate development.  The specifics of the 
Company’s proposed testing plan within its Clifton Park project is provided below in Section 
5.1.8.  
The Company’s AMI Report and Customer Engagement Plan are consistent with other DPS 
actions and the REV objectives regarding a more accurate assessment of value and consumer 
choice. The Value of Distributed Energy Resources (“VDER”) proceeding will produce Staff 
recommendations and Commission decisions regarding the Net Energy Metering (“NEM”) 
Successor Tariff and updated Standby and Buyback rate design options may further help to 
inform the Company’s approach. The NEM Successor Tariff proposals may be instructive for the 
future of mass market rate design. It is likely that both of these efforts will call for granular rate 
designs that depend on the installation of smart meters.  
 
5.1.1 The Path to Better Supply Rates 
In this Customer Engagement Plan, National Grid illustrates the methods used to produce the 
TOU/CPP supply rate design which will produce the benefits modeled in the accompanying 
benefit cost analysis (BCA).  Although the Company has worked closely with Staff and 
Stakeholders in the AMI Collaborative to refine these designs, they should not be considered 
final. The Company is committed to testing these designs in Clifton Park, for example. In short, 
the Company plans to use subsequent rate cases as the venue in which to work through final 
details of a TOU/CPP supply structure, on the way to operationalizing eventual tariffs.  
 
5.1.2 Lessons from Company Demos in Worcester and Clifton Park 
National Grid is well-positioned to offer TVP plans that will lead to customer and system savings 
based on its experience with its pilot projects in Worcester and Clifton Park.  
The Company’s Worcester Pilot was structured as an opt-out program with a default supply 
rate of TOU and CPP.  Customers were defaulted onto this rate, and they had the opportunity 
to save every day by shifting on-peak usage (8AM – 8PM) to off-peak hours (8PM – 8AM, 
weekends, holidays), and were notified a day in advance via customer-preferred channels of 
when critical peak pricing hours would go into effect.  Additionally, customers always had the 
opportunity to opt out of TOU/CPP and into a Peak Time Rebate (“PTR”) plan, in which they 
would earn bill credits for any energy reduced during a critical peak pricing event.  Over the two 
years of the Pilot, an overwhelming majority of the customers (about ninety percent) remained 
enrolled in the TOU/CPP rate, and for those customers that made an active choice to select a 
pricing plan prior to the rates going into effect, a majority of customers actively chose the 
TOU/CPP rate.   
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 227 of 313


--- Page 263 ---
78 of 144 
The TOU/CPP structure produced savings for customers and increased customer satisfaction. 
The final evaluation report for the Pilot found that during critical peak pricing events, load 
reductions ranged from four percent to thirty-one percent, as shown in Figure 5-1 below.  The 
range of peak demand reduction, on a per customer basis, varied based on the level of 
customer engagement.  Of specific note are the Level 1 Passive CPP customers, comprised of 
those customers on the TOU/CPP rate who were responding solely to the price signals (i.e., a 
critical peak kWh price), as well as the Level 1 Active CPP customers who were both responding 
to the price signals and also had some engagement with the Company-provided customer web 
portal.32  On average, these customers produced statistically significant peak demand savings 
while showing year over year improvement. Level 1 CPP Active Customers produced an average 
demand reduction over their Passive counterparts by a factor five times greater in the first year, 
and over twice as large in the second year, which indicates the significant value of additional 
web-based engagement and comprehensive education and outreach.  
 
Figure 5-1: Average Percent Peak Event Load Reduction by Residential Technology/Price Group 
 
The Pilot also offered multiple technology packages (Levels 2-4) to complement the critical peak 
pricing rate packages and facilitate customers’ ability to manage energy usage and bills. 
However, the evaluation report determined that once the impact of technology is removed

(i.e., Level 1 Active and Passive CPP customers), pricing alone accounted for forty-two percent 
of the total demand peak event savings.33 As noted in Section 4, the Company’s New York AMI 
proposal does not include the offering of technology packages for customers, but rather is 
focused exclusively on customer response to time-of-use and critical peak prices.  The Company 
believes that deploying AMI on a territory-wide basis is an important enabling function to allow 
the market for third-party offerings and services to develop and flourish. Additionally, to help 
better enable customers in achieving higher energy savings, the Company will leverage the 
aforementioned Customer Energy Management Platform, as well as available specific energy 
technologies that customers could obtain to help optimize energy management, such as a 
smart thermostat, which is offered currently through the Company’s energy efficiency 
programs, including its E-Commerce Platform.34 Moreover, the Company will test the efficacy of 
technologies to improve customer response and bill savings as part of its Smart Home Rate 
Demonstration project, discussed in the forthcoming updated Clifton Park report. 
The Company’s Worcester Pilot also included a contingent of low-income customers who were 
a specific area of focus within the evaluation.  The final evaluation report found that within 
either the Level 1 CPP Passive or Active groups, no statistically significant difference was 
identified in either group between the low-income customers and the larger customer 
population within that specific group. Additionally, low-income customers also reported a 
higher ability (relative to other customers) to better manage their electricity use.35  As 
described in Section 5.1.8, the Company will continue to test TOU/CPP impacts on low-income 
customers via its Clifton Park pilot. 
In all, customer response in the Worcester Pilot is consistent with those around the country, as 
summarized by the “arc of price responsiveness,” which standardizes a relationship between 
peak reduction and peak-to-off-peak price ratios (Figure 5-2).36   Pilot customers with robust 
technology packages reduced peak demand a little more than predicted by the arc, while those 
customers without technology packages (i.e., responding solely to pricing signals) were very 
close to the average level of the arc as indicated by their peak-to-off-peak ratio.

The implementation of a TOU/CPP construct also delivered meaningful bill savings for 
Worcester Pilot customers.  Customers who were enrolled in this pricing plan achieved an 
average of $236 over the course of the Pilot, compared to an average of $30 in total rebates for 
those customers who opted out of the TOU/CPP pricing plan and into the Peak Time Rebate 
pricing plan.  These results indicate a high potential value for the average customer, and offer 
significant opportunities when extrapolated across an entire customer base that is defaulted 
onto a TOU/CPP pricing plan.   
With respect to the Company’s Clifton Park pilot, a full formal evaluation has yet to be 
completed, and therefore does not have citable learnings. However, evidence suggests that 
Clifton Park customers are interested and willing to try TVP. Asked if they would try a “time-of-
use” pricing plan if their utility company automatically enrolled them in it, a majority of 
residential customers (fifty-three percent) said they would and only a few (twelve percent) 
                                                          
37 Navigant, National Grid Smart Energy Solutions Pilot, Final Evaluation Report (May 2017), at page 78. 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 230 of 313


--- Page 266 ---
81 of 144 
would opt out and the rest were uncertain. Saving money is a key motivating factor driving 
customer interest in TVP which informed the development of the Customer Engagement Plan.  
 
5.1.3 Opt-Out Rate Design 
The Company proposes to adopt the TOU/CPP rate structure as the default mass market rate 
design. Customers, if they choose, may opt-out to a non-TVP rate.  Customers opting-out of the 
TVP rate will be required to remain off the rate for one year. Research from around the country 
indicates that participation rates (and therefore aggregate benefits) are far higher for programs 
in which customers are offered TVP as the default design. Under an opt-in scenario, fewer 
customers would enroll in TVP, and the benefits in the aggregate are thus much smaller than 
under an opt-out scenario. In a comprehensive study of Consumer Behavior Studies of time 
varying pricing, the U.S. Department of Energy (“DOE”) found in a study of ten utility programs, 
that on average, enrollment rates were over six times greater for opt-out designs than for opt-
in designs (ninety-three percent vs. fifteen percent, respectively).38 These results are illustrated 
in Figure 5-3, where each bar represents a different study.  
 
 
Figure 5-3: Enrollment Rates for Opt-in and Opt-out by Treatment Group 
 
                                                          
38 Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory, American Recovery and Reinvestment Act of 2009: Final 
Report on Customer Acceptance, Retention, and Response to Time-Based Rates from Consumer 
Behavior Studies, November 2016. 
National Grid Worcester Pilot (98%) 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 231 of 313


--- Page 267 ---
82 of 144 
The same study found that retention rates across the opt-in and opt-out scenarios, were 
roughly similar across multiple different utilities and did not change dramatically in the first two 
years of the studies, a series of “results [which] were contrary to the expectations of the … 
utilities.”39 Finally, the larger enrollment numbers in opt-out scenarios lead to more cost-
effective program designs.  
As noted in Figure 5-3 above, the Company in its Worcester Pilot maintained a very high 
customer retention rate, on par with many opt-in programs, yet held a much higher volume of 
customers due to the opt-out design – driving a higher overall volume of customer and system 
benefits.  As noted in the Pilot’s final evaluation report, “the 98% retention rate achieved by 
National Grid after two years running the Pilot—coupled with the fact that the Company called 
more event days in each summer than any other dynamic rate pilot—is remarkable.”40    
 
Crucially, the greater degree of participation in opt-out scenarios versus opt-in scenarios drives 
far higher benefits, and higher benefit-cost ratios. For example, over a twenty-year net present 
value (“NPV”) calculation, National Grid estimates that the TOU/CPP pricing design would 
produce $49.33 million in benefits on an opt-in basis and more than double that amount, 
$137.54 million in benefits on an opt-out basis. Similarly, the 20-year NPV of the CO2 benefits 
of the TOU/CPP rates are $8.29 million in an opt-in scenario, but nearly three times that 
amount, $22.33 in the opt-out scenario. Costs are similar across both scenarios, but the 
increased benefits from the opt-out scenario move the overall societal cost test ratio from a 
1.02 for the opt-in case to a 1.15 in the opt-out case.  
The opt-out design is also consistent with the REV goal of choice for customers. National Grid’s 
experience in its Worcester Pilot is that when given the opportunity to save money with a 
TOU/CPP rate, customers, in general, will do so. However, for customers who do not want to 
engage with their energy use and manage it to save money, may opt out to a flat volumetric 
rate that is the status quo standard.  
 
5.1.4 Innovative AMI Supply Rates: Time-of-Use  
The Company proposes to implement a simple TOU structure to capture the variation in energy 
prices on a monthly basis. The TOU rate design strike a reasonable balance between price 
signals that reflect market conditions with stability and simplicity which facilitates customer 
comprehension and response.  
Supply costs will be recovered through on-peak and off-peak volumetric per kWh energy 
charges that vary by month and load zone. These rates are designed to recover the costs of 
energy and will be set on a monthly basis starting with the forecasted locational-based marginal 
                                                          
39 Id. p 26 
40 National Grid Smart Energy Solutions Pilot, Final Evaluation Report, at page 17. 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 232 of 313


--- Page 268 ---
83 of 144 
price (“LBMP”) for the next month, then adjusted to meet predetermined ratio guidelines for 
on-peak to off-peak, as well as for CPP to off-peak.  These rates will be reconciled to actual 
supply costs for the month through the Company’s Electricity Supply Reconciliation Mechanism 
(“ESRM”).  
To establish the on-peak period for energy, the Company carefully analyzed the pricing patterns 
over the years 2015 through 2017 over the NYISO load zones it serves. The Company analyzed 
pricing by hour and by zone across multiple time scales: annual, seasonal, and monthly and 
granularly to look for patterns that could provide the basis for grouping hours into peak and off-
peak periods. The Company aimed to strike a balance between reflecting market conditions in 
the 2015-2017 period, complexity, and feedback from the AMI Collaborative. Although 
different zones, months, and seasons exhibit varied pricing patterns, if the Company were 
proposing to implement the pricing plan under consideration for 2019, based on current 
conditions, it would propose an on-peak period of 10 a.m. through 9 p.m. on weekdays year-
round to capture the basic dynamic of more expensive electricity from the mid-morning 
through the evening. The off-peak period would then cover 9 p.m. to 10 a.m. on weekdays and 
all weekend hours as well as all holidays. Figure 5-4 illustrates the variation in hourly pricing by 
averaging all of the NYISO LBMPs by hour across the year with the proposed on-peak period 
shaded in blue.

In practice, supply costs are calculated monthly for mass market customers, so the proposed 
on-peak period must stand up to not just an annualized average, but the specifics of monthly 
pricing patterns. One simple test for whether to end the peak period is to determine whether 
the 8PM – 9PM hour is more like an on-peak period or an off-peak period in terms of NYISO 
pricing on a monthly basis for each load zone. To estimate this, the Company calculated the 
average of prices for the proposed peak and on-peak periods, but did not assign the 8PM – 9PM 
hour to either peak or off-peak.  Therefore, in essence, it calculated a peak period of 10AM – 8 
PM and an off peak of 9PM – 10AM.  The Company then estimated whether prices in the 8PM – 
9PM hour were closer to the proposed peak or off-peak periods. For the West region, the 8PM 
– 9PM. hour was closer to peak prices in seven of the twelve months, while it was closer to the 
peak pricing in eleven of the twelve months of the year for both the Central and Capital regions. 
Figures 5-5 illustrates the average hourly pricing for the Capital load zone for the winter 
months, defined as December through March, with the Company’s proposed peak period 
shaded in blue. Figures 5-6 and 5-7 illustrate the same patterns for the summer period, defined 
as June – September and the shoulder season of April, May, October and November. Appendix 
E contains similar charts for the Central and West load zone.
