As with every element of the selection of the peak and off-peak periods, the Company commits 
to revisit the proper length and timing of time periods as part of its next two rate cases before 
rolling the TOU rates out as the mass market default pricing plan on the schedule indicated in 
subsequent sections.  In that time, it is reasonable to expect that pricing patterns will change in 
the NYISO, especially due to the influx of new clean generation consistent with New York’s 
50x30 targets. Thus, while the time periods proposed here currently reflect an appropriate 
selection of time periods, they may need to be adjusted to mirror further market evolution in 
the next decade.  
 
5.1.5 Innovative AMI Supply Rates: Critical Peak Pricing 
The Company will use a critical peak pricing component to recover the costs associated with the 
capacity portion of the supply in the NYISO market. The Company has examined NYISO load 
data dating back to 2007 to determine appropriate critical peak pricing practices.  
In New York, National Grid’s capacity obligation is set by the highest load hour of the year in the 
New York Control Area. The actual peak varies by timing and magnitude depending on 
environmental and economic conditions. The lowest NYISO peak since 2007 occurred in the 
relatively cool summer of 2017, with a peak load hour of 29,699 MW in the 6 p.m. hour on July 
19. The NYISO’s highest peak hour in this period occurred on July 19, 2013, in the 5 p.m. hour 
with a peak of 33,956 MW. The NYISO peak hours, based on the data set provided to the 
Company for the period 2007 through 2017 is illustrated in Table 5-2 below.

Table 5-2: NYISO Peak Hours from 2007 to 2018 
 
For the purposes of mass market rate design, critical peak pricing spreads the capacity costs 
attributable to one single hour over a predetermined number of hours to transmit a per kWh 
price signal to applicable customers. Critical peak pricing has the virtue of transmitting the costs 
of the capacity product the Company pays in the wholesale market with relatively high fidelity 
to retail customers while insulating them from the extreme price impacts that would accrue 
from the usage based on a single hour.  
A critical peak pricing rate must be designed according to multiple criteria: 1) the costs it is 
intended to recover; 2) the number of hours over which time it recovers those costs; and 3) the 
time at which those hours, when grouped into events, occur. In this case, National Grid 
suggests structuring the critical peak pricing to recover capacity costs. The forecasts of these 
costs are known ahead of the rate year, and allocated to each class based on their share of 
contribution to the peak in the prior year. National Grid has engaged in careful study of NYISO 
conditions to arrive at seventy critical peak hours over the course of a summer period, defined 
as June through September. The timing of critical peak events will be determined by system 
conditions.  
In choosing the number of critical peak hours over which to spread capacity costs, National Grid 
balances the ability to accurately match the summer peak, which determines Installed Capacity 
(“ICAP”) costs, with criteria that are relatively simple to manage. In the AMI Business Case filed 
in April 2017, the Company proposed calling events whenever the NYISO forecast in the day-
ahead scenario showed its load would exceed 27,500 MW. The Company proposes a revised 
threshold quantity, and a more nuanced critical-peak call practice that corresponds to a NYISO 
threshold level of 29,000 MW statewide. In response to stakeholder input during the AMI 
Collaborative, the Company trimmed the number of hours for critical peak pricing events to 
seventy hours annually.

The timing and duration of critical peak pricing events are important components of the rate 
design. The rate is structured so that when NYISO load approaches 29,000 MW the Company 
will likely call an event. However, the Company will exercise discretion regarding when it calls 
CPP events. If New York experiences a particularly cool summer season as it did in 2017, the 
Company will adjust and call events for potential peaks below 29,000 MW. Conversely, for 
warmer summers such as those in 2013 which had 105 hours with a load above 29,000 MW and 
2018, which through September 10 had 101 similar hours, the Company may not call an event 
for every hour where load exceeds 29,000 MW. Instead the Company will focus on calling 
events in hours that are likely to reflect the NYISO ICAP hour.  
National Grid is also committed to calling critical peak pricing events that reflect NYISO 
conditions subject to customer ability to adjust and respond. In the April 2017 Business Case, 
the Company suggested twenty 8-hour events. Subject to feedback from Staff and stakeholders, 
the Company will strive to limit events to four to six hours. As a matter of practice, periods

where loads exceed 29,000 MW in the NYISO begin and end at a range of times throughout the 
day. For example, as illustrated by Figure 5-8, in the years from 2007-2012, when NYISO loads 
eclipsed 29,000 MW, twenty-nine percent of the time, the first hour in which they did so, was 
the 11 a.m. – 12 p.m. hour. By contrast, in the years from 2013 -2018, when loads surpassed 
29,000 MW, only eleven percent of the time, was the 11 a.m. -12 p.m. hour the first over the 
mark in the day, illustrated by the green bars.  By the 2013-2018 time period, the most 
common first hour of a period over 29,000 MW was the 1 p.m. – 2 p.m. hour, illustrated by the 
tallest of the green bars. The gentle move to the right in the concentration of the tallest green 
versus the blue bars captures the shift in NYISO loading in high demand periods. In later years, 
high demand periods are more likely to begin later.

5.1.6 Outreach and Education to Support Time-Varying Rates 
The Company recognizes the importance of ensuring that customers are aware of, familiar with, 
and able to successfully participate in a TVP plan.  Furthermore, this is of greater importance 
within an opt-out framework.  To address this need, the Company will ensure customer 
awareness of future TVP rates through all three phases of this Customer Engagement Plan.  As 
the Company begins its outreach to customers and other stakeholders about the forthcoming 
installation of smart meters, the developed messaging and content will introduce the concept 
of time-varying rates, and will illustrate the benefits, notably bill savings, that customers and 
can obtain within a TOU/CPP pricing structure.

5.1.7 Bill Guarantee 
Although the Company strongly believes in the value of an opt-out TOU/CPP construct for both 
the individual customer and the overall system needs, it fully recognizes the incremental 
complexity that may be introduced for customers who are more familiar with flat, volumetric 
rates.  In helping to enable and empower customers to best participate and derive value from 
an innovative pricing plan, the Company will offer a dynamic bill guarantee that aims to assist 
customers in understanding the following: 1) how a TOU/CPP pricing plan would operate (i.e., 
why the customer is enrolled and how the on-peak, off-peak, and critical peak hours are set); 
and 2) how this new plan can help produce customer bill savings through a variety of potential 
modifications in energy consumption patterns. Through the availability and utilization of 
granular interval usage data enabled by smart meters and the Customer Energy Management 
Platform, customers will receive education and guidance in making the right decisions that can 
help reduce total energy costs.  
The Company’s Worcester Pilot offered bill protection to customers enrolled in its TOU/CPP 
plan, and found that over two-thirds of customers who were aware of bill protection said that 
the feature made no difference in their efforts to manage their electricity use.  However, the 
Company still believes that bill protection is important to help introduce and transition 
customers to time-varying pricing and to ensure maximum customer participation and the 
receipt of benefits.  
The Company plans to offer a ‘gap year’ between when a smart meter is installed and when the 
new TOU/CPP pricing plan will take effect. All customers will continue to be billed on their 
current volumetric supply pricing plan, as well as relevant demand charges for those customers 
already incurring them today (i.e., commercial and industrial).  The Company envisions that this 
first year is primarily focused on customer awareness and education of the benefits that a new 
smart meter provides, such as access to more granular energy consumption and relevant 
insights.  This would also include access to the tools and services enabled by the Customer 
Energy Management Platform, and would introduce the personalization element that can offer 
customers a specific plan of energy-savings recommendations and actions.  The customer 
would be educated as to how a TOU/CPP plan would work, and would ensure that the customer 
is fully prepared to participate in the second year.  The Company’s Massachusetts affiliate 
utilized this ‘gap year’ strategy within its Worcester Pilot, and found that the additional lead 
time offered an extended opportunity to highlight the benefits and tools available for 
customers through various marketing channels, including grassroots community engagement 
and ‘word of mouth’ outreach with key stakeholders.  This ultimately helped to ensure that 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 241 of 313


--- Page 277 ---
92 of 144 
customers were able to actively participate in a time-varying pricing plan when the new rates 
went into effect.   
During this second year, all customers are auto-enrolled in a new TOU/CPP pricing plan, with 
the eligibility for a 100 percent bill guarantee, which will be calculated on a twelve-month 
basis.  This bill guarantee will compare what the customer paid under the TOU/CPP pricing plan 
to what they would have otherwise paid under the Company’s current basic volumetric supply 
rate for the entire twelve months.  The design of the CPP essentially requires that a bill 
guarantee be calculated on an annual basis rather than over a sub-annual increment. The CPP 
construct will collect the vast majority of capacity payments in the summer months versus 
spread over the entire year, as is currently the case in the flat volumetric supply price. So even 
if customers do not reduce or shift load, they would pay higher amounts for capacity in the 
summer and have lower or no capacity payments in the non-summer months.  If the bill 
guarantee was calculated on a period shorter than the full twelve months, there is the 
likelihood that the bill guarantee would be triggered in the summer months.  However, those 
higher payments in the summer could be offset by lower payments in the non-summer months 
and in the end may not trigger the bill guarantee over the twelve months.   
If the customer paid more under the TOU/CPP plan, they would be credited that amount on a 
future bill to ensure they paid no more than their current basic volumetric rate.  This will allow  
customers to learn about their new pricing plan without fear of paying more in the first year of 
enrollment.  In addition, if a “new” customer, one who has not been at a premise served by the 
Company since the introduction of TVP, moves into the service territory, they will also receive 
the one year bill guarantee. 
For low-income customers, the Company, through its collaborative discussion with the parties, 
understands that the transition for these customers to time-varying pricing may span a longer 
time horizon.  With that in mind, the Company plans to continue to offer a lengthier bill 
guarantee, albeit in a transitional fashion.  In year three upon installation of the smart meter, 
the Company will compare what the customer’s total bill is under the TOU/CPP pricing plan and 
what the customer’s total bill would have been under the otherwise applicable flat volumetric 
rate design.  If the comparison shows that low-income customer paid more than five percent 
above what they would have otherwise paid under the Company’s current basic volumetric 
supply rate for the entire twelve months, they will be credited that amount above 5%.  This 
would continue into year four with a limit of ten percent above what they would have 
otherwise paid under the Company’s current basic volumetric supply rate for the entire twelve 
months. In total, a low-income customer is exposed to four full years following the installation 
of a smart meter to best understand a TOU/CPP pricing plan, and during the fifth year the 
customer is then completely transitioned to that same plan with no provision of a bill 
guarantee.  As with non-low-income customers, if a “new” customer, one who has not been at 
a Company premise since the introduction of TVP, moves into the service territory, they will 
also receive the one year full bill guarantee, followed by the two years of partial bill guarantees.

Consistent with its “Listen, Test, Learn” approach, the Company plans to analyze those 
customers who require a bill guarantee to improve its outreach and education efforts. The 
Company will endeavor to analyze those customers who receive the bill guarantee by customer 
demographics and load profile. Such information will be combined to refine outreach and 
education efforts across the territory.  
 
5.1.8 Clifton Park 
The Town of Clifton Park, where National Grid has deployed AMI meters for both electric and 
gas service will offer the Company a testing ground for the rates described in this section, and 
the associated customer engagement strategies.  The Company will also test a possible Smart 
Home Rate analyzing the ability for customers or smart home energy management systems to 
control bills based on TVP rates.  
The Company’s proposal to test the rates in Clifton Park will be part of a separate filing, to 
follow in November 2018.  The Company will roll out the AMI rates (i.e., TOU/CPP) to customers 
in Clifton Park in the Fall of 2019, which will provide two years of data that will help inform the 
ultimate TVP design utilized in the territory-wide AMI rollout.   
One of the important areas that Clifton Park will test will be the impact of the TOU/CPP rates on 
vulnerable populations, including low income, elderly and chronically ill customers. In a study of 
low-income customers on TOU/CPP rates, the DOE found that compared to more affluent 
counterparts, more low-income customers stayed on the rates when they went into effect. 
Broadly, low-income customers did no better or worse than other customers on a TOU/CPP 
rate structure. Similarly, the research found that although elderly customers opted out of the 
CPP rate at a higher level compared to their younger counterparts, those who remained 
experienced similar bill savings, and correspondingly high levels of satisfaction and a willingness 
to remain on the rate (over ninety percent). Chronically ill populations experienced similar 
impacts to non-chronically ill populations, and similar satisfaction levels.41 The Company will 
work to make sure that vulnerable customers benefit in similar fashion as other customers 
when given the opportunity to save on their bills from time-varying rates.  
                                                          
41 Cappers, Peter, C. Anna Spurlock, Annika Todd and Ling Jin. “Experiences of Vulnerable Residential Customer 
Subpopulations with Critical Peak Pricing.” Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory. September 2016.

6 
METRICS OF SUCCESS 
To determine the success of National Grid’s customer engagement plan, the Company will 
measure customer awareness of smart meter benefits and tools, customers’ usage of those 
tools, and customer reception of future TVP rate plans. To do this, National Grid has created a 
number of customer engagement metrics that are categorized into three categories: 
awareness, enablement/empowerment/tools, and rates. The metrics within each category are 
detailed in the following sections.  
 
6.1 
Awareness 
The awareness category includes three metrics that broadly measure to what extent customers 
have heard about smart meter technology. The first metric measures awareness of smart meter 
technology, features, and benefits through a survey. It is measured on a semi-annual basis 
starting six months prior to smart meter deployment and focusing on the cohort of customers 
currently going through the deployment process. The second metric is the number of forums 
that provide smart meter information and related details (e.g., type of forum, targeted 
audience, date and frequency of events, attendance numbers), which is measured on a semi-
annual basis. The third metric is the number of low income events where smart meter 
information is presented and related details (e.g., date and frequency of events, attendance 
numbers), which is also measured on a semi-annual basis. Of the three metrics, the first two 
were also measured by Con Edison. Taken together, these metrics provide both an outcome 
metric and a measure of the breadth of outlets where National Grid customers can learn about 
smart technology and its benefits. 
 
6.2 
Enablement, Empowerment, and Tools 
Unlike the awareness category of metrics, which mainly focuses on understanding of smart 
meters prior to deployment, the enablement/empowerment/tools category of metrics broadly 
aims to measure customers’ usage of smart meter-enabled tools post-deployment. The first 
metric is the number of new customers and the number of returning customers in each region 
with smart meters that log onto the Customer Energy Management Platform usage/analytics 
page at least once, with breakdowns by service class and low income. As a corollary to this 
metric, National Grid will also measure the percent of customers logging on more than once per 
reporting period. This will be measured on a semi-annual basis, and has also been measured by 
Con Edison. The second metric is a breakdown of customers using specific functions enabled by 
the Customer Energy Management Platform (e.g., personalized insights, high bill/usage alerts, 
established marketplaces, and Green Button Connect), which will be measured semi-annually. A 
corollary measure will be the percent of customers using the platform year over year, which 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 246 of 313


--- Page 282 ---
97 of 144 
will be measured annually. In addition, the impacts of the smart meter awareness campaign 
effectiveness to drive actions will be measured. The final metric will measure satisfaction with 
Customer Energy Management Platform and other tools via a survey, to be performed annually. 
Taken together, these metrics will bring color to how and to what extent National Grid 
customers use smart meter-enabled tools, as well as highlight areas for National Grid to further 
help customers in their modern energy experience.  
 
6.3 
Pricing Program 
In order to understand the success of future TVP rate plans, the Company will measure the 
number and percent of customers who opt-out of the proposed TVP plan each year.

7 
DATA ACCESS, SHARING, AND PRIVACY 
As National Grid invests in foundational technologies to advance grid-of-the-future policies such 
as REV, the Company has re-doubled its commitment to protecting customer data. With the 
deployment of AMI, the Company’s commitment takes on renewed importance.  
The increased opportunities and benefits AMI fosters for advancement of REV priorities creates 
an opportunity to educate customers about the Company’s data privacy protection practices, as 
well as the customers’ rights relative to the collection and use of energy usage data. The 
Company will integrate messaging on data privacy and security into outreach and other 
communications throughout all phases of deployment. 
To that end, National Grid has developed a comprehensive, integrated data privacy framework 
with policies, standards, guidelines, and statements designed to ensure compliance with 
privacy and information security obligations. This approach centers on three key components: 
1) a commitment to core data-privacy principles; 2) regular assessments of the Company’s 
performance in accordance with the principles; and 3) constant vigilance. Such an approach to 
data privacy and management leaves the Company well positioned to safely and securely 
implement AMI.  
The Company’s three-tiered approach is also reflected in its risk-based cyber security 
framework that tracks across people, process, and technology: 
 Setting forth policies and standards intended to ensure the Company works to common 
security objectives by regularly updating privacy and security guidance (including 
incident management and reporting) for those with legitimate business needs to access 
customer data; 
 Addressing privacy throughout the data lifecycle, working to prevent accidental 
misuse/loss/exposure of information; and 
 Ensuring cyber security controls are implemented, information risks are understood, 
and technologies are selected to keep pace with threats. 
The supporting data privacy program utilizes a cross-functional framework that addresses legal 
and regulatory requirements, as well as the ever-changing landscape of privacy and identity 
theft vulnerabilities that can compromise customer data. The framework for compliance, 
privacy, security, and identity theft prevention incorporates accountabilities, policies, 
procedures and business practices, and a fabric of technical and operational controls to 
effectively manage data privacy risks.  
 
7.1 
Core Data Privacy Principles 
National Grid has a commitment to the protection of personal information. The Company has 
policies, procedures, training, and internal communications intended to ensure employees are 
aware of their responsibility to protect personal data of employees and customers.  National 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 249 of 313


--- Page 285 ---
100 of 144 
Grid’s data management standards and data privacy provisions are designed to ensure 
customer personal data is protected for confidentiality and integrity.  
The core principles of National Grid‘s data management standards apply to customer data that 
is created, collected, held, used, shared, transformed, published, or processed by National Grid; 
regardless of whether the data is structured (i.e., organized and searchable) or unstructured 
(i.e., not organized and not readily searchable). Moreover, the principles apply at all stages of 
the data lifecycle, including the Company’s use of historical data in business operations. 
National Grid’s commitment to customer data privacy is guided by seven core principles, which 
build into a data management hierarchy. The principles further define the structure by which 
the Company’s performance requirements are organized. The seven principles are below. 
1. Data is managed and secure: All data is subject to governance and protection from 
unauthorized access throughout its full lifecycle (from planning and collection through 
to retention and disposal).  
2. Data is fit for purpose: Data should be of the quality required for its intended uses.  
3. Data is standardized: In terms of its definition, format, content and categorization 
providing the ability to link differing forms of related data together.  
4. Data has a single authoritative source: For all data there shall be a single and identified 
authoritative (master) source.  
5. Data is accessible: Business units within the Company should all have the appropriate 
access to the data they need to carry out their respective roles.  
6. Data is an asset: It has a purpose, cost value and lifecycle. 
7. Data publication: Any data that is published should be defined, appropriate, quality 
assured, verifiable, and, if necessary, aggregated and anonymized. 
In support of the seven key principles, the Company is further committed to ensuring that 
customer data is: 
 Processed fairly and lawfully in accordance with customer rights as dictated by relevant 
legal requirements; 
 Obtained for specified business and/or legal purposes and not processed in a way that is 
incompatible with the purpose(s) for which it was collected; 
 Adequate, relevant, and not excessive for the purpose(s) for which it is processed; 
 Accurate and, where necessary, kept up to date; 
 Not kept for longer than is necessary to fulfil the purpose(s) and then either made 
anonymous or disposed of securely; 
 Appropriately protected against unauthorized, inadvertent, or illegal processing and/or 
disclosure; and 
 Restricted to designated countries unless the rights and freedom of individuals are 
protected.

7.1.1 Audits 
The New York Public Service Commission mandated that National Grid conduct annual data 
privacy assessments, utilizing an independent, third party, of National Grid’s data privacy 
control environment for the protection of customer information. 
• Summary of 2018 Findings: The review found that leading practices were defined and 
followed across the organization and a strong control environment was present in all 
control domains that were reviewed. It was established that National Grid had 
addressed most findings to which management committed remedial action and seven of 
the eight domains (Corporate Accountability; Policies, Procedures and Guidelines; 
Training, Education and Outreach; Credentialing; Network Security; Physical Security; 
and Incident Response) were awarded a control maturity rating of “High”. 
• General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) Internal Audit—GDPR readiness: All 
recommendations completed on schedule. 
 
7.1.2 Lifecycle Data Management 
The data privacy policy and framework is designed to provide efficient and systematic control 
of the collection, processing, and disposition of personal information throughout its lifecycle 
(see below) based on relevant laws and regulations and internationally recognized best 
practices. In implementing the framework, National Grid’s employees, contractors, and vendors 
all have a responsibility to protect personal information throughout the various stages of its 
lifecycle, as shown below in Figure 7-1. 
• Collection: Give notice and obtain consent where appropriate to collect information and 
only collect what is needed for the intended purpose. 
• Storage: Store personal information securely to protect it from unauthorized or 
unlawful processing and against accidental loss, destruction, or damage. 
• Use and disclosure: Only process information for the use specified during collection and 
disclose on an authorized need to know/have basis. 
• Access and updating: Provide individuals with access to the information held about 
them and allow them to make corrections. 
• Transfers: Only transfer personal information to approved companies and/or countries 
that have or can provide adequate levels of privacy protection through contractual 
agreements that specify the privacy and security requirements. 
• Retention: Retain personal information only for as long as necessary in accordance with 
the Company’s records retention policies. 
• Disposal: Dispose of personal information safely and securely. 
 
 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 251 of 313


--- Page 287 ---
102 of 144 
 
Figure 7-1: Data Lifecycle 
 
National Grid’s current data privacy policy recognizes that consumption data constitutes 
personal information and that many of the privacy concerns can be mitigated by limiting the 
processing of personal information to what is necessary. Limiting the processing of personal 
information is a key data privacy management principle. Where there is an operational need for 
personal information, National Grid has implemented controls to ensure that the processing of 
such information is restricted to what is necessary. 
 
7.1.3 Data Governance and Privacy Program Management  
National Grid’s data privacy program management is led by the Group Assurance Department’s 
Global Information and Records Management (“GIRM”) team. The GIRM team oversees a 
network of SMEs and members through routine reporting and meetings to track progress and 
manage delivery of data privacy responsibilities/activities. As shown in Figure 7.2 below, the 
GIRM team networks include: a Data Privacy Forum; data privacy champions embedded in all 
business units controlling personally identifiable information (“PII”); and a Community of 
Practice Forum that includes a network of data privacy SMEs and records coordinators in every 
business area. The objectives of the program include: maintenance and updating of data 
privacy policies, procedures, training, and communications to ensure the highest level of 
protection for customer and employee personal information.

Figure 7-2: Data Governance 
 
Energy usage data the Company collects is managed in a manner intended to protect the data 
from unauthorized access. For example, with the Clifton Park Demonstration energy usage data 
is processed by a Meter Data Management System (“MDMS”), which creates billing 
determinants and distributes the data to downstream systems (e.g., the Customer Service 
System (“CSS”), Advanced Data Analytics (“ADA”) Department and the demonstration project’s 
customer engagement vendor). The meters retrieve 15-minute interval information and pass 
the meter reads to the MDMS. The data is then transferred from the MDMS to the Company’s 
customer billing system, CSS, where the data is combined with the applicable customer 
information and becomes PII. The Company retains customer billing data on the CSS 
system/servers, as well as on vendor servers located in the United States. The energy data flow 
is generally depicted below in Figure 7-3.

7.1.4 Data Usage 
National Grid will use energy data only for authorized purposes such as billing, internal data 
analytics, and to assist customers with reducing their consumption or lowering their energy 
bills. Any transfer or use of energy data outside of National Grid will only be done with the 
customer’s consent or by Commission approval. National Grid has developed a customer-facing 
smart meter privacy statement to specifically inform customers how it collects, stores, utilizes, 
and protects the smart-meter energy data.43 The customer-facing smart meter privacy 
statement will be provided to customers during the Phase Two and will be available on the 
National Grid website as well as by request.  
 
7.1.5 Third-Party Data Access 
In the event that National Grid provides access to customer usage information to an authorized 
third party vendor, the third party is required to enter into a contractual relationship with the 
Company. The contract will contain the Company’s standard non-disclosure agreement (“NDA”) 
and information security addendum. The NDA sets forth the terms upon which the third party 
will ensure that energy usage data it accesses will remain confidential and not be disclosed to 
unauthorized parties. The information security addendum is included consistent with relevant 
data privacy laws in the states where the Company and its affiliates operate. The addendum 
                                                          
43 See Draft National Grid Smart Meter Privacy Statement included in the Appendix F. 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 254 of 313


--- Page 290 ---
105 of 144 
contains provisions to ensure that a third party has the necessary policies and infrastructure 
available to protect the information that it holds on behalf of National Grid and its customers. 
Further, it contains provisions regarding the obligation of the third party to notify National Grid 
in the event of a breach of the information and to indemnify the Company for any losses that it 
may incur as a result of the third party’s negligence in connection with any breach. In the event 
that National Grid provides access to customer usage information to an authorized third party 
energy services entity (“ESE”), the Company requires the third party to enter into a data 
security agreement (“DSA”) and submit a “Self Attestation” regarding compliance with certain 
minimum cyber security requirements.  The DSA sets forth the terms upon which the third 
party will ensure that customer and energy usage data it accesses will remain confidential and 
not be disclosed to unauthorized parties.  Pursuant to the business-to-business process 
adopted by the Commission in the Data Security Proceeding (Case 18-M-0376), the DSA is 
required for any ESE, including ESCOs, EDI Vendors, Direct Customers and certain DER 
Suppliers. The DSA contains provisions to ensure that a third party has the necessary policies 
and infrastructure available to protect the information that it holds on behalf of National Grid 
and its customers. Further, it contains provisions regarding the obligation of the third party to 
notify National Grid in the event of a breach of the information and to indemnify the Company 
for any losses that it may incur as a result of the third party’s negligence in connection with any 
breach.  While energy usage data is not statutorily considered PII, the Company’s policies and 
procedures including the requirement of the aforementioned contractual provisions essentially 
treats it as such. 
National Grid has developed policies, standards, and guidelines that govern data access and the 
protection of sensitive information that requires information to be classified appropriately and 
protected in accordance with the classification. National Grid’s Data Privacy Policy states that 
personal information will not be disclosed unless: 
 The disclosure is fair and lawful and consistent where appropriate, with the notified 
purpose(s); or 
 The individual has given appropriate ‘consent’; or 
 The disclosure is necessary (e.g., in the individual’s vital interest); or 
 The disclosure is covered by ‘exemption’ from any relevant legislation. 
Requests requiring disclosure of PII through the judicial, regulatory and/or criminal 
investigation processes are exceptions and must be referred to legal counsel and/or the data 
privacy leads for review and approval unless there is an established approved procedure in 
place.  Personal information relating to employees, customers or vendors may only be 
forwarded to a National Grid business affiliate if applicable legal requirements are met44 and if: 
 The transfer is based on a clear business need and is undertaken securely; 
 The receiving business provides appropriate security for the personal information; and

 The receiving business ensures compliance with this National Grid’s policy for the

 Comply with relevant privacy laws and the businesses policies and procedures; 
 Process personal information strictly in accordance with the businesses instructions; 
 Implement appropriate security measures to deliver the required levels of protection; 
 Seek permission from the National Grid for further onward transfers (e.g., to sub-

 Promptly report to National Grid any breaches, risks or issues to personal information; 
 National Grid’s right to audit the third party for compliance with the data privacy 
policies and procedures; and 
 Either return the personal information or dispose of it securely upon termination of the 
agreement. 
 
7.1.6 Data Retention 
As described in the Company’s customer-facing privacy statement,45 National Grid retains 
energy usage data for six years. The Company determined this retention period based on the 
highest retention requirements46 for the collection of the following data:  
 
 The meter read interval (i.e., the time frame when the meter is read);  
 The customer number or account number (depending on the account); and  
 The actual meter read. 
 
 
7.1.7 Regular Data Privacy Impact Assessments (“PIAs”) 
National Grid ensures that an individual’s right to privacy is safeguarded, personal data is used 
only as intended, and that precautions preventing misuse or loss are both effective and 
appropriate through regular Data Privacy Impact Assessments (“PIAs”). The PIAs are a 
mechanism the Company uses to assess its data collection and management practices.   
7.1.8 Data Privacy Champions 
Each business area that controls personal data is required to nominate a data privacy champion 
to work with GIRM team to identify data repositories. For each such depository, the data 
privacy champion must capture set-up details, assess the risk of exposure and ensure effective 
controls are in place. The GIRM team has also created a PIA template to help data privacy 
                                                          
45 See Draft Energy Usage Data Privacy Statement included in Appendix F.

meter records.

champions capture the information needed to carry out required risks assessments and track 
implementation of any mitigating actions raised.   
One PIA form needs to be completed for each separate repository of personal data controlled 
by a department within the Company. All completed PIA forms are uploaded to the Company’s 
Governance Risk and Compliance (“GRC”) system and will form part of the central record of all 
of the Company’s personal data processing activities. GRC is a global application and therefore 
will provide the required documentation for U.S. and U.K. personal data protection. All PIAs 
need to be kept up to date on an on-going basis to ensure that they always reflect current 
practice. When a repository is decommissioned, the associated PIA form is deleted.  
The inventory provides a data map to where and how National Grid accesses, collects, stores, 
and processes PII. The Company’s Digital Risk and Security (“DR&S”) Vendor Assurance team 
works closely with the GIRM team to evaluate the security controls of vendors/third-parties 
who are accessing, collecting, storing or processing PII on behalf of the Company, its customers, 
and/or its employees. 
Data privacy champions are responsible for documenting personal data processing activities 
and collecting evidence to demonstrate that local data management practices are both lawful 
and secure. Departments that do not control personal data make a declaration to that effect, 
and do not need to conduct PIAs. Those departments that process personal data must initiate a 
structured, thorough analysis of their processes to identify all personal data repositories. Such 
departments must then assess how they manage the personal data against the legal 
requirements in force. If the assessment reveals any gaps, the department must put the 
required controls in place to achieve compliance.   
A department is deemed to be compliant when all the local information security controls are in 
place and all legal processing requirements are met for the personal data repositories under its 
control. 
National Grid’s Enterprise Risk Management (“ERM”) team maintains a Global Risk Standard 
and Risk Procedure, which enables the Company to manage existing processes and provide 
end-users with detailed work instructions regarding subjects such as risk scoring, escalation and 
recording. ERM identifies executive level strategy risks related to data privacy and incorporate 
them into the ERM process. 
 
7.1.9 Privacy by Design 
Privacy by Design is a process to identify data privacy concerns before implementing, 
transforming, upgrading, or replacing a program or system. The Company uses this pre-launch 
approach to proactively analyze and reduce the risk that PII may be misused when the 
Company implements the new initiative. The Company assesses the potential impact on an 
individual’s PII throughout the lifecycle of the new program. National Grid already conducts 
PIAs for projects and change programs that involve the processing of sensitive PII such as 
credential, financial or health information; such assessments are also an integral part of the 
Privacy by Design approach. As part of REV, National Grid completed an initial PIA to 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 257 of 313


--- Page 293 ---
108 of 144 
understand the current strategy and develop a baseline of potential privacy risks. The 
maintenance of an accurate register of types of personal information used by the business and 
the adoption of the “Privacy by Design” approach are critical components the Company’s data 
privacy strategy and will be carried out as part of AMI implementation. 
 
7.1.10 Constant Vigilance 
The Company reviews its data privacy policies and standards on an annual basis (and more 
often if circumstances require). For example, new and emerging laws, regulations (and security 
related intelligence) are continually monitored and the impacts assessed to ensure the 
Company’s privacy practices continue to evolve to meet current and anticipated future 
obligations and safeguard against emerging privacy threats, vulnerabilities and associated risks. 
The Company proactively addresses many of the privacy related issues through internal 
training, such as its mandatory “Learning Link” curriculum, and it makes the policies available to 
all employees and contractors through the Company’s intranet site. Likewise, the Company 
works to ensure that vendors who have a business need to work with protected information 
become familiar with the Company’s policies during the on-boarding process.  
In addition, the privacy provisions in the Company’s data privacy framework, include: 
Annual privacy training and ongoing awareness communications and activities to all employees, 
contractors, and third parties who have access to PII. National Grid will (in accordance with the 
Department of Energy Voluntary Code of Conduct) educate consumers and other individuals 
about the privacy risks associated with the evolution of the grid and opportunities for 
mitigation.   
 
7.1.11 Augmenting Data Privacy Principles   
National Grid is also considering additional high-level principles of conduct for both utilities and 
third parties as set out in the “DOE’s Data Privacy and the Smart Grid: A Voluntary Code of 
Conduct (“VCC”). These principles would augment the Company’s existing policies, procedures, 
and statements, in particular those that are customer facing: 
 
 Customer Notice and Awareness: Develop practices that explain on a recurring basis the 
data collection policies and procedures to customers, focusing on customer options and 
responsibilities. 
 Customer Choice and Consent: Develop processes that continue to empower customers 
to control access to customer specific data for secondary purposes (i.e., to authorize 
differential access to multiple third parties, limit the duration of access, keep a record of 
data releases, rescind authorizations, and dispose or de-identify data once authorization 
or the need for the data has expired). The Company will identify the data types and 
disclosures that do not require customer consent, including requiring certain data to be 
obtained directly from the customer. 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 258 of 313


--- Page 294 ---
109 of 144 
 Customer Data Access: Refine procedures that allow customers to access data, identify 
and correct possible inaccuracies, and include potential fees for non-standard requests. 
 Data Integrity and Security: National Grid’s data privacy program includes 
methodologies for creating aggregated or anonymized data. 
 Self-Enforcement Management and Redress: Establish procedures that customers the 
opportunity and process to challenge National Grid’s compliance with its privacy 
statement and privacy practices (i.e., compliance with the VCC).  
 
7.1.12 How the European Union’s General Data Protection Regulation impacts National 
Grid  
The European Union’s (“EU”) General Data Protection Regulation (“GDPR”) reinforces the 
principle that information and data protection is a fundamental right. Therefore, information 
can only be processed legally, under strict conditions, for a legitimate and transparent 
purpose, and with explicit consent of the individual. GDPR applies to companies outside the EU 
who do business with EU residents or persons residing in EU. National Grid’s obligations for 
GDPR compliance include the primary concern for the customer in the US as it relates to the 
protection of customer usage information and the necessary consents that may have to be 
obtained prior to releasing this type of information involves an EU resident that may be 
purchasing service in the US. Specifically, in the event an EU resident has property in the US and 
maintains his or her EU address to facilitate business with National Grid, the Company has 
instituted procedures to address any concerns that may arise under the GDPR. Accordingly, 
should there be a specific request for access to an EU customers’ energy usage information, 
those requests will be handled pursuant to the principles set forth in the GDPR.  
  
7.2 
Incident Responses for PII Breaches 
National Grid’s Information Security Incident Management Policy ensures that a consistent and 
effective approach is applied to the Company’s response to and management of information 
security incidents, including privacy-related breaches. Privacy-related breaches can involve 
customer PII. PII is treated as confidential information and any suspected breach triggers an 
incident management response. Following an investigation by the Incident Response Team, a 
determination will be rendered by the Legal Department regarding whether a breach of PII has 
occurred. In the event the investigation reveals that a breach has occurred, customers will be 
notified as legally required. National Grid has developed a series of playbooks covering a 
number of breach scenarios, including a Privacy Incident Playbook, which sets out how National 
Grid would deal with a breach response and a notification event.  
The management of incidents involving personal information often requires close coordination 
among personnel from across the organization, such as the Chief Information Officer, Chief 
Information Security Officer, group data privacy leads, the system owner, data owner, legal 
counsel, and public relations officer. The cyber incident response process defines roles and 
responsibilities and ensures that the right people are included in the incident.  
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 259 of 313


--- Page 295 ---
110 of 144 
A data privacy breach exercise involving customer personal information is conducted on an 
annual basis and lessons learned are captured and fed back into the Company’s incident 
response plans. For specific incidents as defined by the incident response process, a post-
incident review is performed and lessons learned are captured. Lessons are either immediately 
incorporated into response plans, mitigating controls or any residual risk is logged, tracked and 
managed. The procedures are regularly tested to identify potential breaches and misuse of 
personal information including the timeliness of breach notifications when necessary.

8 CONCLUSION 
The Company developed this Customer Engagement Plan to inform and educate customers 
about National Grid’s smart meter implementation – with a primary goal of enabling customers 
to save and to choose how they consume energy. This aim supports the Company’s Customer 
Experience Transformation initiative and New York's REV policy objectives –  specifically its 
goals to make energy more affordable for its citizens and to empower New Yorkers to make 
more informed energy choices.   
Full-scale deployment of AMI progresses National Grid’s vision for its customers in the digital 
age. Having the ability to view, access, and respond to smart meter data offers customers the 
opportunity to not only monitor their energy usage but change their consumption behaviors in 
order to reduce total energy costs.  Overall, the Company maintains smart meters give 
customers more opportunities to save and make effective energy decisions.  
With the support and cooperation of fifteen stakeholder groups, the Company engaged in a 
Collaborative process to develop this Customer Engagement Plan. After eight large and small 
group collaborative sessions, the involved parties assessed multiple options to present this Plan 
and define the most effective approach. The Collaborative process gave all parties an 
opportunity to contribute to and further discuss the choices made in support of the Customer 
Engagement Plan. After these discussions, the Company decided on a three-phased plan to best 
reach, engage, and involve customers: 1) AMI awareness and education prior to meter 
installation, 2) engaging with customers leading up to and during meter deployment, and 3) 
empowering and enabling customers to utilize their smart meter data with new tools and third-
party applications via the Customer Energy Management Platform. Throughout each phase, the 
Company has proposed to track a variety of metrics to confirm successful execution of smart 
meter implementation. 
In particular, smart meters make innovative rate designs, such as Time-of-Use and Critical Peak 
Pricing, possible and easier to implement. With the influx of data, the Company is committed to 
protecting customer data 
 and has instituted core data privacy principles, including routine audits, data governance 
structure, defined data retention periods, and a narrow third-party data access policy. 
Customers expect more from their utility; they not only want the utility to deliver affordable, 
reliable, and safe energy, but also increasingly look for access to actionable information and 
greater choice and control over their energy use. The implementation of smart meters in New 
York allows National Grid to fulfill customer expectations while meeting NY REV objectives. 
With the recent advances in smart metering solutions, customers will be able to see firsthand 
the results of changing their energy consumption behaviors, giving customers greater control to 
reduce their own energy costs. National Grid will be able to provide customers with these 
insights into their consumption patterns and offer more pricing options, all while maintaining 
affordable, safe and reliable energy service.

APPENDIX C: LEARNINGS FROM INTERNAL AND EXTERNAL EXPERIENCES 
Appendix C.1 The Company’s AMI/Smart Meter Pilots 
National Grid and its Massachusetts affiliate currently operate two smart grid pilots (which 
include deployment of AMI) to test marketing and outreach material, understand customer 
needs, measure customer sentiments, and obtain other learnings to best improve the customer 
experience for a broader AMI deployment. The first pilot started in January 2015 in Worcester, 
Massachusetts, and the second pilot started in January 2017 in Clifton Park, New York.  
The Company’s approach is built off learnings from its affiliates’ Worcester Pilot and its own 
Clifton Park demonstration project, both of which focused on: 
 Communicating early and often with simple, easy to understand language; 
 Using the “Listen, Test, Learn” approach to capture feedback, measure customer 
experience, and modify strategies as needed; 
 Utilizing personalized messaging; and 
 Having a strong presence in the community.  
 
Worcester, MA Pilot 
The Worcester Pilot originated from the 2008 Massachusetts Green Communities Act, which 
required utilities to pilot AMI deployment with time-of-use rates. It had the goal of achieving 
five percent active customer energy savings and five percent demand reduction, which were 
achieved. The Worcester Pilot followed a “Listen, Test, Learn” approach, in which ongoing 
improvements have been made and learnings fed into the Company’s AMI proposal for its New 
York service territory. In addition, the Worcester Pilot promoted “Choice, Control, 
Convenience” for customers. As part of the pilot’s offerings, the Company’s Massachusetts 
affiliate utilized an opt-out time-of-use and critical peak pricing structure (with the opportunity 
to opt-in to a peak time rebate pricing plan), provided customers with a web portal and Green 
Button Download capability, and provided a choice of home energy management devices 
(including automated DR capability with customer authorization).  All of this was supported by a 
community Sustainability Hub and a comprehensive education and outreach program. The 
community Sustainability Hub is a physical retail space in downtown Worcester that is open to 
the public and contains sustainable technology with which customers can interact. It is staffed 
by employees who can answer questions about the Pilot and about general energy 
management strategies. The Pilot has taken place all within the city of Worcester, with roughly 
15,000 meters comprised of mostly residential and small business customers. There have been 
approximately 3,000 customers with third-party suppliers who were ineligible for participation 
in the Pilot’s pricing plans and in-home technologies.  
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)

The Worcester Pilot provided National Grid invaluable learnings that allowed the Company to 
understand customer sentiment regarding their awareness of AMI and interest in managing 
energy usage, as well as how to improve customer outreach methods. 
The Pilot was launched with a two-day Green2Growth summit that brought together 350 
diverse stakeholders.  In terms of communication methods, the Pilot highlighted the need for a 
multi-channel targeted approach to customers, such as providing a digital-first approach while 
recognizing that there is a customer population that prefers traditional, non-digital options. 
Communications included weekly emails, monthly paper reports, quarterly newsletters, and bi-
monthly bill inserts. Other key learnings from the Worcester Pilot team are listed in the table 
below which was included in the Pilot’s final evaluation report.47

Clifton Park, NY Demonstration 
In New York, the Company launched the Demand Reduction REV Demonstration in Clifton Park 
in January 2017 with the installation of over 13,000 electric AMI meters and nearly 11,500 
AMI-compatible gas encoder receiver transmitters (“ERT”) that function over a cellular 
network. The Clifton Park pilot was designed to provide residential customers with price 
signals, tools and information, deep energy insights and an enhanced customer portal to help 
customers better manage energy use. National Grid provides customers a web portal to view 
near real-time energy consumption, the ability to earn rewards by participating in Peak Time 
Rewards events, and provides information on DER opportunities. The project is supported by a 
comprehensive outreach and education plan under the theme “Take Control, Earn Rewards, 
Make Choices.” 
There are currently no formal evaluation results as initial analyses were used to modify project 
elements for the second year of implementation. The project completed its second year of Peak 
Time Rewards this summer, having called thirteen “conservation day” events that were aligned 
with local weather forecasts, and awarded reward points under a two-tiered system based on 
amount of load curtailed. Second season event data is currently under review.   
In addition, National Grid is seeking to align the Clifton Park pilot with its AMI Business Plan. 
The project will likely be modified to provide customers innovative pricing signals and introduce 
smart technologies under a revised Smart Home Rate REV demonstration to assist in managing 
price signals.  
Appendix C.2 Company Surveys and Studies 
National Grid is committed to understanding customer preferences and is continuously 
collecting customer feedback and opinions on ongoing operations and services. Instruments 
such as surveys are an important tool to measure customer feedback and are collected through 
a variety of formats including: online surveys, mail surveys, telephone surveys, in person focus 
groups, online focus groups, and town hall meetings.  
National Grid values the insights provided through these studies to better understand customer 
needs, concerns, preferences, and attitudes. The findings from the studies listed below inform 
National Grid’s customer outreach messaging and which benefits to highlight during AMI 
deployment. 
 Value Propositions of New Technologies – This study was originally conducted in 2014, 
and then again in 2017. This survey allows National Grid to assess customers’ baseline 
interest and awareness of AMI, areas for improvement in outreach materials and 
engagement, as well as behaviors and preferences related to managing energy use. 
 Household Energy Usage, Behaviors and Preferences – This study was initially conducted 
in June 2018, and again in August 2018 with additional questions to better understand 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 272 of 313


--- Page 308 ---
123 of 144 
customer attitudes toward demand charges.48 These surveys looked to understand 
metrics across the service footprints of the Company, including New York. 
 
In 2014, the Value Propositions of New Technologies study targeted understanding customer 
feedback on grid modernization (among other topics). Some key relevant findings for New York 
customers include: 
 Nearly all (ninety-seven percent) were willing to reduce electricity usage during peak 
periods in exchange for a billing incentive. 
 Residential customers were more willing than commercial to shift some usage from high 
demand times, yet the main reason is because they are not home during the day 
anyway. 
 There was clear interest in energy management devices, and in 
interactive/programmable thermostats. 
 Customers need more education on smart meters but do not seem to reflexively reject 
them if offered free installation. 
The study was repeated in 2017 and confirmed the findings from the prior study, but also 
offered additional insights. 
 Asked if they would try a “time-of-use” pricing plan if their utility company automatically 
enrolled them in it, a majority of residential customers, fifty-three percent, said they 
would and only a few, twelve percent, would opt out (with the rest uncertain).  
 Main reasons for NOT being willing to try a “time-of-use” pricing plan showed that 
residential customers’ resistance to a plan like this centered around not wanting to be 
automatically enrolled in anything and wanting to be in control. 
National Grid and its affiliates launched a Customer Council in 2018. This is an online 
community of approximately 6,000 residential customers who represent a wide cross-section of 
the customer base in New York, Massachusetts, and Rhode Island. The customers have signed 
up to be a part of the Customer Council and have agreed to provide ongoing feedback to the 
Company on various topics.  
In June of 2018, National Grid conducted the Household Energy Usage, Behaviors and 
Preferences study. The study was conducted online among approximately 2,000 Customer 
Council members – 550 in New York. Some key findings are: 
 Customers are generally open to changing the time of day they conduct certain 
behaviors if electricity was cheaper at night. They are most likely to run their 
                                                          
48 Note: Mass-market demand charges are not included within the Company’s AMI proposal. 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)

Page 273 of 313


--- Page 309 ---
124 of 144 
dishwasher, dry their clothes, charge their phones, wash clothes and adjust their air 
conditioner or heat during off-peak hours.  
 The technology most utilized by customers for monitoring their accounts and usage 
patterns is via an online portal. This is consistent across the entire customer base, 
including low-income and elderly segments. 
A significant percentage of customers indicate that they are willing to modify some of their 
behaviors in response to time of use rates. In the chart below, more than seventy percent of 
customers indicated they would be willing to do laundry and run their dishwashers at a 
different time.   
 
Figure C-1: Willingness to Modify Behaviors of National Grid Customers49 
 
For both non-low-income and low-income customers, the Company’s web portal is the 
technology that is preferred for monitoring accounts.  Generally, the preferred technologies are 
very similar between the two groups.  Email and smartphone are also popular among low-
income customers.  There is a slightly higher likelihood that a low-income customer would want 
to communicate with a representative (via phone, chat, or in-person), but these are clearly not 
the technologies that are preferred by the majority of low-income customers.

Appendix C.3 External Resources 
National Grid also looked to a wide array of external resources in developing the Customer 
Engagement Plan. This includes relevant non-profit research, other utilities that are or have 
deployed AMI, and industry research. The information below details those findings and the 
resulting impact on customer outreach.  
 
Appendix C.3.1 Smart Energy Consumer Collaborative Research 
National Grid further researched how consumers interact with smart meter functionality as it 
developed the Customer Engagement Plan. To that end, National Grid turned to the Smart 
Energy Consumer Collaborative (“SECC”) and its extensive industry research. The SECC is a 
nonprofit organization that works to learn the wants and needs of energy consumers in North 
America, encourages the collaborative sharing of best practices in consumer engagement 
among industry stakeholders, and educates the public about the benefits of smart energy and 
energy technology.”51

126 of 144 
The SECC, in its Consumer Platform of the Future report, examines customer engagement with 
smart grids that leverage deployment of AMI. It found that energy customers have six main 
areas of interest that can be addressed by an AMI-enabled smart grid: 
 Two-Way Communication: Personalization of notifications and EE recommendations, 
high-usage alerts, enrollment in TVP, home-energy display devices, and third-party 
devices via an application program interface (“API”). 
 Energy Efficiency: E-Commerce Platform, online and home audits, instant rebates 
(online and at store), and home-energy displays. 
 See Your Power: Energy management portal, bill alerts, enrollment in TVP, and 
NILM/disaggregation. 
 Home Area Network: Connected solutions, connected devices, enrollment and 
participation in Dynamic Load Management programs, non-intrusion load monitor 
(“NILM”)/disaggregation, home-energy displays, and third-party devices via API. 
 Renewable Resources: Solar marketplace and EV marketplace. 
 Electric Vehicles: Electric vehicles (“EV”) marketplaces and charging station maps.  
From its research, the SECC also distilled the following six themes associated with what it 
dubbed the “2018 State of the Consumer”: 
 Don’t wait until they ask – start a conversation now. 
 Use an “all of the above” channel strategy.  
 Start with the basics: bill and payment options. 
 Consumers want to be in the driver’s seat. 
 Consumers want cleaner energy solutions. 
 Programs that shift energy usage can be a win-win.  
With these customer themes and concerns in mind, National Grid developed a customer 
engagement and outreach strategy with an eye toward informing and empowering customers. 
The Company also took a proactive approach of reaching out to customers and designing the 
proposed web-based Customer Energy Management Platform in a manner that is 
straightforward and puts customers’ needs at the forefront.  
 
Appendix C.3.2 Other Utilities 
National Grid can learn from other utilities that have deployed AMI. As such, National Grid 
considered the AMI deployment and customer engagement experiences and lessons learned of 
peer utilities, to develop this Customer Engagement Plan.  
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)

127 of 144 
In New York, Con Edison and Orange and Rockland Utilities, Inc. (“Orange and Rockland”) 
collectively published a customer engagement plan in July 2016. This document closely follows 
the approach Con Edison planned to take with respect to customer education, Green Button 
Connect and data sharing, rate design, cost saving and revenue opportunities, as well as opt-
out designs.52 The Con Edison document provides a comprehensive example of topics worth 
noting in outlining a customer engagement strategy. In this customer engagement plan, 
National Grid covers relevant topics from the Con Edison plan and expands on other areas in an 
effort to fully address National Grid’s customer concerns as they relate to AMI deployment and 
customer engagement efforts specific to the Company’s customers and their needs.  
 
Appendix C.3.3 Industry Reports 
National Grid looked to a wide array of sources as it developed the Customer Engagement Plan 
to both validate results and findings of the Company’s research and to also leverage emerging 
trends. Several key features of the Company’s outreach efforts align with industry research as 
shown in Accenture’s New Energy Consumer – New Paths to Operating Agility report published 
in 2017.  
In the report, Accenture examines new developments in customer behavior and preferences as 
they related to their interaction with their energy providers based on surveys with energy 
customers across multiple countries. The report highlights that energy consumers have 
constantly evolving expectations and that successful energy providers are meeting those 
expectations with new operating models that capitalize on digital innovations.53  
One of the primary developments in customer preferences has been a shift towards 
personalization in products and services. The vast majority of consumers surveyed prefer a 
personalized experience, and over half are comfortable sharing additional information in this 
pursuit. Personalization was cited as among the most important factors energy consumers 
considered when deciding to purchase additional products and services. That said, customers 
prefer low-effort channels for information on personalized products and services, such as email 
or SMS text messaging.54  
As National Grid continues to enhance its outreach and messaging strategy, the Company will 
continue to maintain customers’ focus on personalization at the forefront. This includes

continually evaluating the outreach strategy for different demographic segments of National 
Grid’s customer base, such as customers who prefer materials in different languages, 
customers with specialized medical needs (e.g., those on life support), low-income customers, 
and the elderly. As a result, communication materials will be tailored to each of these customer 
segments. Customers will be able to request materials in a format of their choosing and have 
the ongoing capability of managing their preferences.  
Beyond personalization, a further driving force in customer preferences is the need for digital 
channels. The New Energy Consumer – New Paths to Operating Agility report underscores 
findings that customers would consider digital channels for a wide range of products and 
services. This includes energy-related services, such as basic rate plates, customizable rate

appliances, heating, ventilation, and air condition (“HVAC”) units, and lightbulbs. Customers 
would further consider connected home technologies.55

Appendix D.1 Residential Customers 
National Grid provides electric and gas distribution across seven regions in New York, serving 
approximately 1.7 million residential customer accounts with the largest segment located 
within the Capital region, and the least within the Northern region.  
 
 
Figure D-1: Residential Customer Accounts by Region 
 
The customers can be grouped by energy type into three categories: electric only, combined 
gas and electric, and gas only. Electric only is the largest group at approximately sixty-two 
percent, followed by combined gas and electric at thirty-seven percent, and gas only at two 
percent. Figure D-2: Customer Accounts by energy type by region highlights the breakout of 
electric only, gas only, and combined electric and gas by region for residential customers.

Appendix D.1.1 Addressing the Needs of Vulnerable Customers  
Approximately thirteen percent of all residential customer accounts (nearly 200,000) are 
categorized in “collection-protected” status. This varies by region with the lowest being in the 
Western region (nine and one-half percent) and the highest in the Frontier region (Seventeen 
and eight-tenths percent).  
The two largest categories (approximately ninety-six percent) under collection-protected 
status are low income and elderly, blind, or disabled (“EBD”).  Low-income accounts are 
identified through a file matching process with the Department of Social Services. The 
accounts consist of a combination of accounts that received energy assistance through the 
Home Energy Assistance Program (“HEAP”) in the last twelve months and those that received 
HEAP in the past twelve months for fuel suppliers. 
To comply with the Low Income Home Energy Assistance Program (“LIHEAP”) Performance 
Law, National Grid began a file match in October 2016 with the department of Health and 
Human Services (“HHS”).  Each year, the HHS file is received from the Office of Temporary and 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 280 of 313


--- Page 316 ---
131 of 144 
Disability Assistance (“OTDA”) in October and a return file is sent back to OTDA with specific 
requested data by November 15th.  As directed in the Low Income Order, Case 14-M-0565, in 
January 2018, National Grid began using the information from the HHS file to add eligible 
customers to our low income program.  As a result, 25,363 qualifying low income customer 
accounts were added in January and an additional 2,938 low income customer accounts were 
added in April 2018. Going forward, this matching process and identification of eligible 
customers will be done in October/November.”

the breakdown by region.

APPENDIX F: DRAFT NATIONAL GRID SMART METER PRIVACY 
STATEMENT 
 
National Grid Smart Meter Privacy Statement  
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“National Grid” or the “Company”) is 
committed to protecting its customers’ privacy. The Company offers customers advanced 
metering infrastructure (“AMI”), also commonly referred to as “smart meters,” which allow for 
the wireless transmission of detailed energy usage data.  With this technology, energy usage 
information is automatically and wirelessly sent to the Company and also made accessible to 
the customer.  
National Grid collects smart meter energy usage data for the purpose of servicing customer 
accounts and encouraging customers to reduce energy use and save money.  Smart meters do 
not store or transmit personal information (e.g., addresses, phone numbers, or customer 
names).  This Privacy Statement outlines how and why customers’ smart meter energy usage 
data is collected, used and disclosed by National Grid. 
TYPE OF DATA COLLECTED 
Smart meters store and communicate customers’ electric energy usage data, as well as non-
energy information, such as the meter identification number, amount of energy used, meter 
events and alarms, the time interval during which the energy was used, frequency, reactive 
power, voltage, device location, status, and communication protocol. A customer’s meter 
identification number is associated with a geolocation; however, geolocation data is not 
deciphered until encrypted smart meter usage data has been transmitted to National Grid and 
decrypted by the Company’s secure systems.  
National Grid only collects smart meter energy usage data: 1) as is reasonably required to 
provide utility services to customers; 2) as approved by regulatory agencies; and 3) as required 
by law. The Company does not collect any personally identifiable information (“PII”) through 
customers’ smart meter.  
HOW THE COMPANY USES AND DISCLOSES SMART METER DATA 
National Grid will only collect, process, retain, use, and disclose smart meter energy usage data 
for legitimate National Grid utility-related business purposes, or as specified by a customer’s 
express consent to disclose such information to a third party (e.g., Green Button Connect My 
Data or the electronic data interchange used to send information to energy service companies).  
The primary purpose for which the Company uses the data is to bill for electric service.  In 
addition, the data enables the Company to identify energy usage trends, develop and promote 
programs and services to encourage more efficient energy use, manage electricity demand, 
send marketing communications to customers, and to provide quality service, among other 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 292 of 313


--- Page 328 ---
143 of 144 
things. National Grid complies with all applicable laws regarding the use and sale of customer 
data. National Grid will only share smart meter data with third parties when: 1) the customer 
expressly consents; 2) there is a business purpose for sharing the information and there are 
contractual or tariff protections in place with the third party to protect the data; or 3) the smart 
meter data has been properly aggregated and anonymized in accordance with applicable 
regulatory requirements.   
In some instances, National Grid may use smart meter data, as necessary or appropriate, to 
comply with applicable legal processes or requests from government or judicial authorities, or 
to comply with requirements or directives of governing regulatory agencies (e.g., the New York 
State Public Service Commission and the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission).  Likewise, in 
the event of any reorganization, merger, sale, joint venture, assignment, transfer or other 
disposition of all or any portion of the Company’s business, assets or stock (including in 
connection with any bankruptcy or similar proceedings), the Company may disclose smart 
meter data as necessary or required to effectuate such a transaction.  The Company may also 
use this information to enforce the terms and conditions of service in applicable tariffs, to 
protect the rights, privacy, safety or property of the Company and its customers, and to pursue 
remedies or limit damages the Company may sustain. 
CUSTOMER CHOICE 
Once the Company begins deploying AMI meters, customers may notify the Company’s 
Customer Service support center at (800) 642-4272, neighborhood installers, or use online 
channels to inform the Company of their decision to opt-out of receiving a smart meter.  
Certain fees, as set forth in the Company’s tariff, apply to customers who opt out of the smart 
meter installation.  Customers are also given the opportunity to choose whether to share their 
smart meter data with third parties through the Green Button Connect My Data program, 
arrangements with an energy service company, or some other secure technical or 
programmatic method that complies with applicable rules and regulations.  
SECURING SMART METER DATA  
National Grid has implemented reasonable physical, technical, and administrative security 
measures to safeguard smart meter energy usage data. Specifically, National Grid works with a 
third-party vendor to safeguard the transmission of smart meter usage data across the entire 
technology infrastructure, which includes the meter itself, the communications network (which 
transfers meter data via a cellular radio or a mesh network) and the system that aggregates and 
analyzes the data for billing purposes. Further, all data transmitted from the smart meter is 
encrypted and de-identified. Customers’ names and addresses are not tied to the data until it 
reaches National Grid’s secure systems.  
National Grid employees and third-party contractors may not access, remove, disclose or use 
smart meter energy usage data, unless they are doing so for legitimate business purposes, and 
even then, only if the individual has a need to know the information.  All employees and 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-1)
Schedule 1
Page 293 of 313


--- Page 329 ---
144 of 144 
contractors who have access to smart meter energy usage data are required to maintain the 
confidentiality of that information.  This restriction applies during employment with National 
Grid or engagement as a contractor, and after the employment or engagement ends. 
RETENTION AND DISPOSAL  
National Grid retains smart meter data for the period reasonably necessary to carry out its 
business functions and services, unless a longer retention period is required or permitted by 
law.  To that end, the Company disposes of smart meter data in accordance with its record 
retention and disposal guidelines.  
SPECIAL NOTES 
This Privacy Statement only applies to the collection, use and disclosure of customer smart 
meter energy usage data.  It does not apply to the Company’s website, mobile application, or 
customer agreements and means, other than smart meters, by which customers provide 
information to National Grid.  The Company may modify this Privacy Statement from time to 
time to reflect legal, technological, or other developments.  Such updates will be reflected on 
this page. 
CONTACTING THE COMPANY 
Please contact the Company right away at the address or email address below:   
 If, for any reason, you believe your smart meter data is no longer secure; 
 You have any questions about this Privacy Statement; 
 To request a copy or your energy use data; or  
 To update any potentially inaccurate or outdated customer information.
