* During the current rate plan, customers will receive the full benefit of AMI efficiency savings through the Net Utility Plant tracker mechanism. The Company's share of the achieved savings (20%) will be booked as a regulatory asset.
In the next rate case and going forward, customers will continue benefiting from the AMI efficicency savings through lower ratebase and depreciation. Recovery of the Company's regulatory asset will be determined in subsequnt rate proceedings.
**The Company will share in any efficiency savings on NMPC's capital costs to the extent actual capital costs upon project closure (i.e ., after full AMI deployment) are less than the forecast; provided the savings are $10 million or more.

PURSUANT TO THE PROVISIONS of the State Administrative Procedure Act, NOTICE is
hereby given of the following action:
Proposed Action: The Public Service Commission is considering whether to approve or reject, in
whole or in part, the Report of Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid on the
Proposed Implementation of Advanced Metering Infrastructure, wherein the Company seeks
approval to deploy advanced metering infrastructure (“AMI”) throughout its service territory.
Statutory authority: Public Service Law Sections 5 and 66.
Subject: Implementation of AMI across Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National
Grid’s (“NMPC” or the “Company”) service territory.
Purpose: To approve or reject NMPC’s Report on the Proposed Implementation of Advanced
Metering Infrastructure.
Substance of the proposed rule: In accordance with Section 15.4(f) of the Joint Proposal
approved by the Public Service Commission on March 15, 2018 in Cases 17-E-0238 and 17-G-
0239, the Company filed a Report on the Proposed Implementation of Advanced Metering
Infrastructure,
seeking
approval
of
a
six-year
AMI
meter
deployment
program
and
accompanying opt-out time-varying pricing rate structure. The Commission may approve, reject
or modify, in whole or in part, NMPC’s request.
Text of proposed rule and any required statement an analysis may be obtained by filing a
Document Request Form (F-96) located on our website
https://www.dps.state.ny.us/f96dir.htm. For questions, contact: John Pitucci, Public Service
Commission, 3 Empire State Plaza, Albany, New York 12223-1350, (518) 486-2655, email:
john.pitucci@dps.ny.gov
Data, views or arguments may be submitted to: Kathleen H. Burgess, Secretary, Public Service
Commission, 3 Empire State Plaza, Albany, NY 12223-1350, (518) 474-6530, e-mail:
secretary@dps.ny.gov
Public comment will be received until: 60 days after publication of this notice.
Regulatory Impact Statement, Regulatory Flexibility Analysis, Rural Area Flexibility Analysis
and Job Impact Statement
Statements and Analysis are not submitted with this notice because the proposed rule is within
the definition contained in Section 102(2)(a)(ii) of the State Administrative Procedure Act.

Dear Secretary Burgess: 
 
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid (the “Company”) submits for filing the 
following supplement to the Report on the Proposed Implementation of Advanced Metering 
Infrastructure (the “AMI Report”).   
 
The Company initiated an AMI collaborative process in April 2018 in accordance with the Joint 
Proposal approved by order of the Commission in the above-captioned cases.  The collaborative 
process culminated in the November 15, 2018 filing of the AMI Report.  Since then, the 
Commission commenced a public comment session with several collaborative stakeholders 
submitting comments and the Company filing a response.  The Company filed an updated 
confidential benefit-cost analysis (“BCA”) model incorporating New York Independent System 
Operator prices (and related adjustments) that were released after the filing of the AMI Report. 
Department of Public Service (“DPS”) Staff submitted four sets of data requests seeking more 
information about the Company’s proposal.  And, the Company has continued to negotiate with 
AMI vendors and review recent peer utility AMI filings.  This supplemental filing incorporates 
refinements to the AMI Report from this continued inquiry, showing an improved BCA.  The 
Company continues to believe its AMI proposal, as further supported by this filing, provides an 
opportunity to drive value for customers in line with the State’s and the Company’s clean energy 
objectives.  The Company greatly appreciates the ongoing interest and feedback from the 
collaborative stakeholders, and looks forward to working with the Commission as it considers this 
proposal.

Supplemental Filing of  
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid 
on the Proposed Implementation of Advanced Metering Infrastructure 
I. 
Introduction. 
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“National Grid” or the 
“Company”) hereby provides this supplemental filing (“Supplemental Filing”) in support of its 
Report on the Proposed Implementation of Advanced Metering Infrastructure (“AMI Report”) 
filed with the New York State Public Service Commission (the “Commission”) on November 15, 
2018. 
Since filing the AMI Report, New York State (the “State”) adopted the Climate Leadership 
and Community Protection Act of 2019 (“CLCPA”),1 “the most ambitious and comprehensive 
climate and clean energy legislation in the country.”2  In a press release accompanying execution 
of the CLCPA, Governor Cuomo stated, “[C]ries for a new green movement are hollow political 
rhetoric if not combined with aggressive goals and a realistic plan on how to achieve them.”3  
                                                     
1 Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act of 2019, S. 6599 (signed by Governor Cuomo July 
18, 2019). 
2 Governor Andrew M. Cuomo Press Release, Governor Cuomo Executes the Nation’s Largest Offshore 
Wind Agreement and Signs Historic Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act (July 18, 2019).  
3 Id. 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-2)
Schedule 1
Page 4 of 14


--- Page 354 ---
 
3 
 
National Grid continues to believe AMI is a foundational investment to any realistic plan for 
achieving the shared vision of the clean-energy future.   
As set forth in this Supplemental Filing, the case in support of AMI has gotten stronger 
since the Company filed the AMI Report.  Vendor negotiations have led to lower costs.  Further 
analysis has also helped identify additional benefits consistent with AMI filings from peer utilities.  
Collectively, the refinements result in updated benefit-cost analysis (“BCA”) ratios of 1.36 for the 
New York-only opt-out scenario and 1.23 for the opt-in scenario,4 both of which continue to 
support territory-wide deployment of AMI. 
II. 
Procedural Posture. 
On January 2, 2019, the Commission filed a State Administrative Procedure Act (“SAPA”) 
notice regarding the AMI Report in the State Register, commencing a 60-day public comment 
period.5  On February 22, 2019, the Company, consistent with footnote 51 of the AMI Report, 
filed an updated version of the BCA model, reflecting the New York Independent System 
Operator’s release of new Congestion Assessment and Resource Integration Study (“CARIS”) 
Phase 2 Base Case Input Assumption Prices and related adjustments to the Social Cost of Carbon 
and Energy Insights/High-Usage Alerts benefits (“CARIS Update”).  
On March 4, 2019, at the close of the public comment period, Pace Energy and Climate 
Center and Multiple Intervenors (“MI”) filed comments on the AMI Report.  On April 29, 2019, 
the New York Department of State’s Utility Intervention Unit (“UIU”) filed comments on the AMI 
Report and the CARIS Update.  The Company filed a response to UIU’s comments on May 29, 
                                                     
4 The Company is proposing territory-wide meter deployment on an opt-out basis.  The reference to opt-
out and opt-in for purposes of the BCA pertains to the time-varying pricing rate design used to evaluate 
AMI benefits.  See AMI Report at page 14. 
5 New York State Register, SAPA ID No. PSC-01-19-00004-P (January 2, 2019). 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-2)
Schedule 1
Page 5 of 14


--- Page 355 ---
 
4 
 
2019.  On June 7, 2019, the International Brotherhood of Electrical Workers (“IBEW”) Local 
Union No. 97 filed reply comments. 
In addition to the CARIS Update and comments, Department of Public Service Staff 
(“Staff”) submitted four sets of information requests (“IRs”) with a total of twenty-five questions.  
The Company responded to the IRs on February 25, July 8, and July 29, providing the responses 
to Staff as well as stakeholders who participated in the Company’s AMI Collaborative process.      
III. 
Supplemental Information. 
a. Decreased AMI Costs. 
Through continued vendor negotiations the Company has added value for customers.  The 
largest share of the downward adjustment is driven by a reduction in AMI software costs. The 
Company has also recognized a modest increase in meter costs driven, in part, by the addition of 
outage management system (“OMS”) integration.  In total, AMI costs are projected6 to decrease 
by $16.08 million on a 20-year net present value (“NPV”) basis.  As further described below, 
vendor negotiations have also resulted in anticipated unquantified customer benefits.  
Throughout this process, the Company has also continued to monitor the condition of its 
current set of electric automated meter reading (“AMR”) meters and gas modules; both of which 
are nearing end-of-life conditions.  The Company designed its proposed deployment of AMI and 
AMI-compatible gas modules to closely align with the planned replacement cycle for those 
devices.7  As set forth in the AMI Report and the Company’s response to UIU, addressing the 
                                                     
6 The projected costs are not final, as the Company is awaiting Commission approval before finalizing and 
executing vendor contracts. 
7 See AMI Report, Exhibit A AMI Business Case at page 7 (“The currently install[ed] AMR electric meters 
and gas modules are reaching the end of their twenty-year useful life.  In the absence of the AMI proposal, 
the Company will begin the required replacement of the majority of the AMR assets.  By fiscal year 2025, 
approximately 96 percent of electric meters and 80 percent of gas modules will have reached the end of 
useful life.”). 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-2)
Schedule 1
Page 6 of 14


--- Page 356 ---
 
5 
 
AMR asset condition issues with AMI requires considerable lead time for design and 
implementation.  In the absence of a near-term solution, National Grid anticipates an increased 
likelihood of needing to address potential meter failures through the like-kind replacement of AMR 
meters, as well as the need to deploy more expensive cellular AMI technology in response to 
evolving rate design policies, such as the Commission’s Standby Buyback Rate Order.  Moving 
forward in such a manner would reduce the benefits of the cost reductions identified above, while 
also delaying benefits to customers who are seeking to take meaningful steps toward the 
achievement of clean energy goals. 
b. Increased AMI Benefits. 
As set forth in the AMI Report, the Company identified four broad quantifiable AMI 
benefit categories: 1) avoided AMR and VDER meter costs; 2) avoided operation and maintenance 
(“O&M”) costs; 3) customer benefits; and 4) societal benefits.8  In reviewing the AMI-related 
filings of peer utilities and after further refining its analysis, the Company has recognized two 
additional benefit-related adjustments affecting the above categories.   
 
First, National Grid, unlike other utilities,9 did not include a societal outage notification 
benefit as part of the AMI Report. Upon further review, the Company believes AMI could decrease 
outage notification by two minutes through autonomous alerts. This presents a two-minute 
                                                     
8 See AMI Report, Exhibit A AMI Business Case at page 32. 
9 See e.g., Case 19-E-0378 et al., Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules 
and Regulations of New York State Electric & Gas Corporation for Electric Service (“NYSEG/RG&E Rate 
Case”), Direct Testimony of Advanced Metering Infrastructure Panel (May 20, 2019) at page 9 (“AMI 
provides a platform for achieving very significant societal benefits in the form of reductions in customer 
outage costs....”) and page 44 (Non-Operational Societal Benefit of $91.9 million (NPV) from “Reduced 
Customer Outage Time”); Case 15-E-0050, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, 
Charges, Rules and Regulations of Consolidated Edison Company of New York, Inc. for Electric Service, 
Direct Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel at pages 24-25 (“AMI will produce a 
series of societal benefits. … The enhanced monitoring and control functionality associated with AMI will 
also reduce the occurrence and duration of outages at critical facilities.  The reduction of outages has 
important implications for the safety of the public and access to critical services”). 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-2)
Schedule 1
Page 7 of 14


--- Page 357 ---
 
6 
 
reduction from initial outage to Company notification, resulting in a decrease of total customer 
outage time, from occurrence of the initial outage to resolution.  Using the Lawrence Berkeley 
National Laboratory’s Interruption Cost Estimate model, the Company conservatively estimates 
that the two-minute reduction will result in a benefit of $85.02 million (nominal) and $36.99 
million (20-year NPV). 
 
Second, the Company re-examined the investments that are necessary to fulfill the role of 
the Distributed System Platform (“DSP”) provider and how territory-wide deployment of AMI 
will supplant some of the technology that would otherwise be required to fulfill the DSP 
responsibilities.10  The Company identified two major types of DSP-related investments that can 
be reduced or avoided with AMI approval: 1) grid sensors; and 2) interval metering required for 
participation in demand response (“DR”) and energy efficiency (“EE”) programs.     
Because AMI offers more granular (temporal and geospatial) system information (e.g., 
voltage measurements), the Company estimates that full-scale AMI deployment will augment 
existing feeder-monitoring efforts, further enhancing situational awareness and grid operations by 
providing granular monitoring of the secondary system from customer meters.11  With this 
additional AMI-enabled feeder monitoring, the Company expects it will avoid on average two 
                                                     
10 Case 14-M-0101, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission in Regard to Reforming the Energy Vision,

consumption and bills and drive overall system efficiencies. Additionally, AMI will facilitate customer

sensors12 per feeder and is consistent with other utilities13.  Based on the Company’s existing head-
end sensor installation efforts14, each sensor is estimated to cost $0.039 million (all in costs 
including communications) in 20-year future worth.  When considered across an estimated 1,922 
feeders15 that would require additional sensors to enable DSP functionality, the avoided costs, 
consistent with the findings of peer utilities,16 are approximately $148.96 million in future worth 
and $82.39 million in 20-year NPV. 
The Company also agrees with the Commission that “AMI will provide information that 
affords customers the opportunity to participate in demand response and energy efficiency 
programs, as well as innovative rate structures, allowing them to better manage electricity 
consumption and bills and drive overall system efficiencies.”17  Therefore, in the absence of AMI, 
the Company expects more expensive cellular interval metering will be required to facilitate 
customer participation in such programs. With an estimated cost of $482 per cellular interval meter 
(all in costs including communications) in 20-year future worth, the Company expects full AMI

deployment will enable it to avoid $5.41 million in future worth18 and $2.99 million in 20-year

c. BCA Ratios. 
Incorporating the above adjustments in the CARIS Update BCA model19 results in a New 
York-only AMI cost for the opt-out20 scenario of $640.96 million (20-year NPV) and benefits of 
$873.63 million (20-year NPV) for a BCA ratio of 1.36.  Likewise, the same adjustments, when 
applied to the opt-in scenario, result in New York-only AMI costs of $621.71 million (20-year 
NPV) and benefits of $766.01 million (20-year NPV) for a BCA ratio of 1.23.   
National Grid’s Rhode Island and Massachusetts affiliates continue to evaluate advanced 
meter functionality (“AMF”) solutions.21  To the extent either jurisdiction adopts an AMI/AMF 
strategy, there would be opportunities for the jurisdictions to share certain cost synergies (e.g., 
back-office systems).  While such multi-jurisdictional cost-sharing opportunities are important, 
the ratios only improve slightly when costs are shared among the Company’s affiliate jurisdictions.  
The opt-out BCA ratio for a joint New York-Rhode Island deployment is 1.40, which marginally 
improves to 1.44 if AMI is deployed at the same time across all three jurisdictions.  The opt-in 
scenario similarly results in marginal improvement, with a BCA ratio of 1.28 for a joint New York-
Rhode Island deployment and a ratio of 1.32 if all three jurisdictions simultaneously implement 
AMI. 
                                                     
18 The following variables were considered in the analysis: 1) forecast projections for EE and DR growth over 20 
years, 2) saturation in the participation of EE and DR for the 2029-2039 period, 3) number of average customers 
without interval metering today per MW of EE and DR growth. 
19 In accordance with Public Officers Law §§ 87(2)(d) and 89(5)(a)(1), as well as the Commission’s 
implementing regulations 16 NYCRR §6-1.3, the Company separately filed the BCA model, which contains 
confidential proprietary trade secret information, with the Commission’s Records Access Officer.

functionality or AMF.

From a timing perspective, the Company’s Rhode Island affiliate continues to progress a 
comprehensive AMF strategy, as contemplated in the Amended Settlement Agreement approved 
by the Rhode Island Public Utilities Commission (“Rhode Island PUC”) in Docket 4770.  An 
extensive collaborative process commenced in October 2018, which has driven analysis and dialog 
surrounding AMF and the value it can provide for customers and grid-facing initiatives.  The 
Company’s Rhode Island affiliate is continuing to work collaboratively with interested 
stakeholders and anticipates filing its comprehensive AMF proposal with the Rhode Island PUC 
later this year. 
Likewise, the Company’s Massachusetts affiliate is mobilizing efforts for future 
opportunities to file an AMI proposal with the Massachusetts Department of Public Utilities 
(“DPU”).  To understand current AMI technology and roadmaps, the DPU hosted an industry 
workshop on May 30, 2019.  The DPU used the workshop to directly engage with key vendors 
across the AMI industry and to learn about new advancements in customer engagement 
technologies, as well as alternatives for advanced metering.  The Company’s affiliate further 
expects the DPU to host a similar workshop with utilities to align AMI deployment with 
Massachusetts’ customer and state energy policies. 
d. Enhanced Meter Functionality. 
In addition to the updated cost estimates and benefit categories quantified above, the 
Company has also identified significant unquantified benefits for customers through enhanced 
meter functionality.   
For example, the proposed AMI solution will include a physical radio22 and associated 
firmware to provide a wireless signal to a home-area network (“HAN”), which will allow 
                                                     
22 The physical radio will either use the IEEE 802.11- or IEEE 802.15.4-based wireless network. 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-2)
Schedule 1
Page 11 of 14


--- Page 361 ---
 
10 
 
customers to monitor and control their energy usage instantaneously.  Using open and secure 
communication standards, customers will be able to leverage third-party vendor technology, as 
well as internet-based options, to achieve real-time energy awareness.  The Company’s proposal 
supports the HAN-enabled approach, which offers a cost-effective solution with greater 
functionality than alternatives like KYZ circuit cards or non-meter-enabled home-energy monitors.   
For a customer to connect a HAN-related device to an AMI meter, the customer will first 
confirm the eligibility/compatibility of the device with the AMI meter and then activate the device 
by logging into their secure online account on the Customer Energy Management Platform 
(“CEMP”).  Once logged in, the customer will navigate to the activation page, enter the applicable 
device credentials, and receive an activation acknowledgment through encrypted channels.  From 
there, the customer may begin using his or her HAN device, such as an in-home display or home-
energy manager.  In this way, the Company will facilitate customer management of their total 
energy bill through the secure use of HAN technology to receive real-time energy usage 
information. 
The Company’s proposed solution also offers the opportunity to upgrade the meter 
firmware over the air, without having to replace meter hardware.  With the rapid pace at which 
technology is evolving, the opportunity to mitigate and avoid the potential for stranded assets was 
a critical consideration for the Company.  The upgrade capabilities help to safeguard the 
Company’s AMI metering solution from the risk of technological obsolescence. 
The proposed AMI solution also offers distributed grid-edge computing technologies; 
enabling the possibility of customer-facing and grid-facing distributed intelligence use cases.  
Early examples of such opportunities include: high impedance detection, theft detection, locational 
awareness/grid mapping, intelligent voltage monitoring, reliability analysis, and electrical fault 
Exhibit ___ (AMIP-2)
Schedule 1
Page 12 of 14


--- Page 362 ---
 
11 
 
identification.  Collectively, the grid-edge capabilities have the potential to improve reliability, 
better manage voltage fluctuations with the proliferation of distributed energy resources, and, 
importantly, improve safety by identifying unsafe conditions that customers can proactively 
address.   
e. Clifton Park Innovative Pricing Proposal Update. 
Finally, in the AMI Report,23 the Company committed to working with Staff to align the 
Clifton Park Demand Reduction REV Demonstration Project (“Clifton Park Project”) with the 
customer engagement strategies and rate proposal in the AMI Report.  To that end, on February 
14, 2019, the Company filed a Request for Approval of Tariff Leaves Modifying the Clifton Park 
Demand Reduction REV Demonstration Project to Test Innovative Pricing Proposals on an Opt-
Out Basis.24  On March 20, 2019, the Commission published a SAPA notice commencing a 60-
day public comment session on the Company’s proposal.25  On May 20, 2019, at the close of the 
public comment period, MI filed comments on the Company’s proposal.  National Grid and Staff 
have subsequently engaged in further discussions regarding the innovative rate designs the 
Company proposes to test in Clifton Park.  Based on those discussions, the Company intends to 
file a revised rate proposal in Fall 2019, with anticipated roll out of innovative pricing in Fall 2020.  
The learnings from testing the innovative rate designs will further inform the Companies future 
rate filings and AMI customer engagement strategies. 
IV. 
Conclusion

For the foregoing reasons, National Grid continues to believe AMI is a cost-effective 
solution that will enable the Company, its customers, and the State to make large strides toward 
achieving clean energy goals.  Therefore, the Company respectfully reaffirms its request that the 
Commission: 1) approve National Grid’s proposed implementation of AMI as set forth in the AMI 
Report as supplemented herein; and 2) grant such other and further relief as may be required for 
the benefit of National Grid and its customers.

ELECTRIC
FERC
Account
Description
Allocator
(Case 17-E-0238)
Proposed
Allocator
Notes
370
Meters and Meter
Installation
Meter Cost
including
CT/PT’s
Meter Cost
excluding
CT/PT’s
CT/PT’s are much more expensive when compared to the
meters and have a much longer life. Their net book value
should be allocated directly to the services classes utilizing
them.
397
